# Neonatology

### Page 1

## 1. Normal Newborn

### Page 1

### Definitions

-   **Newborn period:** First 4 weeks after birth.
    -   First week = **early newborn**
    -   Next 3 weeks = **late newborn period**
-   **Average birth weight in India:** 2.9 kg
-   **Birth-weight classification:**
    -   `< 2.5 kg` --- Low birth weight
    -   `< 1.5 kg` --- Very low birth weight
    -   `< 1 kg` --- Extremely low birth weight
    -   `> 4 kg` at the time of birth --- Big baby/macrosomia
    -   Normal birth weight: approximately **2.5--4 kg**
-   Birth weight should always be interpreted with gestational age using
    an **intrauterine growth chart (percentile chart)**.
    -   Lower reference curve = **10th percentile**
    -   Upper reference curve = **90th percentile**
    -   Any value above 90th percentile = **Large for Gestational Age
        (LGA)**
    -   Birth weight between 10th and 90th percentile = **Appropriate
        for Gestational Age (AGA)**
    -   Birth weight `<10th percentile` = **Small for Gestational Age
        (SGA)**

### Small for Gestational Age

**Types:** - Normal variant/constitutional SGA --- more common. -
Abnormal variant: **IUGR (intrauterine growth restriction/retardation)**
--- always associated with pathology.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 2

### Points to differentiate IUGR from normal variant SGA

-   IUGR babies have loose skin folds; skin is also wrinkled.
-   Placenta/umbilical cord appears thin.
-   Head circumference and chest circumference:
    -   Normal difference between head and chest circumference is **3
        cm**.

### Types of IUGR

|Feature|Maternal cause|Fetal cause|
|---|---|---|
|Common cause|Disorders of placenta, placental insufficiency like gestational HTN|Genetic defects, anomalies|
|Onset|Late (`> 3rd` trimester)|Early (`1st` trimester)|
|Effect|Brain sparing because majority of blood supply is directed towards the brain; head size is normal while body is small; called **asymmetric IUGR**|No brain sparing because of early onset; every part of the body undergoes growth restriction; called **symmetric IUGR**|


### Ponderal index

**PI = [Weight (g) × 100] / [Length (cm)]³**

-   `PI > 2` → Symmetric IUGR
-   `PI < 2` → Asymmetric IUGR

### Large for Gestational Age (LGA)

-   Birth weight `>90th percentile`.

**Causes:** 1. Constitutional LGA/normal variant. 2. Infant of diabetic
mother (gestational diabetes).

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 3

3.  **Sotos syndrome (cerebral gigantism):**
    -   Child has intellectual disability.
    -   Premature tooth eruption.
    -   Developmental delay.
4.  **Beckwith-Wiedemann syndrome:**
    -   Macroglossia
    -   Micrognathia
    -   Anterior abdominal defects like:
        -   Omphalocele
        -   Umbilical hernia
    -   Hemihypertrophy
    -   Increased risk for Wilms' tumour.
    -   Features shown in the notes include macroglossia, umbilical
        defect and ear-pinna creases.

### Assessment of maturity

Newborns are divided into **preterm, term and post-term** babies
according to gestational age.

-   `GA <36 weeks + 6 days` → Preterm baby
-   `GA 37 weeks to 41 weeks + 6 days` → Term baby
-   `GA ≥42 weeks` → Post-term baby

### Expanded New Ballard Score (ENBS) — searchable reconstructed table

The original source contains pictorial scoring cells. Where the scan is not reliably legible, this searchable reconstruction gives the standard scoring descriptors rather than inventing unreadable cell values.

**Purpose:** estimate gestational age from physical and neuromuscular maturity.

- 6 neuromuscular signs + 6 physical-maturity signs.
- Individual items contribute from **−1 to +5** according to the standard table.
- Total score: approximately **−10 to +50**.
- Standard maturity mapping spans approximately **20–44 weeks**.

#### Neuromuscular maturity

| Sign / score | −1 | 0 | 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Square window (wrist)** | >90° | 90° | 60° | 45° | 30° | 0° | — |
| **Arm recoil** | — | 180° | 140–180° | 110–140° | 90–110° | <90° | — |
| **Popliteal angle** | 180° | 160° | 140° | 120° | 100° | 90° | <90° |
| **Scarf sign** | Opposite shoulder | Opposite axilla | Past midline | At midline | Nipple line | Short of nipple | — |
| **Heel-to-ear** | Heel at ear | Heel to ear | Heel near face | Heel at chin | Heel at shoulder | Heel at chest | — |
| **Posture** | — | Extended | Partial flexion | Moderate flexion | Good flexion | Full flexion | — |

#### Physical maturity

| Physical sign | Score | Descriptor |
|---|---:|---|
| **Skin** | −1 | Sticky, friable, transparent |
|  | 0 | Gelatinous, red, translucent |
|  | 1 | Smooth pink; visible veins |
|  | 2 | Superficial peeling and/or rash; few veins |
|  | 3 | Cracking; pale areas; rare veins |
|  | 4 | Parchment-like, deep cracking; no vessels |
|  | 5 | Leathery, cracked, wrinkled |
| **Lanugo** | −1 | None |
|  | 0 | Sparse |
|  | 1 | Abundant |
|  | 2 | Thinning |
|  | 3 | Bald areas |
|  | 4 | Mostly bald |
|  | 5 | — / not scored in standard table |
| **Plantar surface** | −1 | Heel-toe length about 40–50 mm; very immature sole |
|  | 0 | >50 mm, no creases |
|  | 1 | Faint red marks |
|  | 2 | Anterior transverse crease only |
|  | 3 | Creases over anterior two-thirds |
|  | 4 | Creases over entire sole |
|  | 5 | — |
| **Breast** | −1 | Imperceptible breast tissue |
|  | 0 | Barely perceptible |
|  | 1 | Flat areola, no bud |
|  | 2 | Stippled areola, ~1–2 mm bud |
|  | 3 | Raised areola, ~3–4 mm bud |
|  | 4 | Full areola, ~5–10 mm bud |
|  | 5 | — |
| **Eye / ear** | −1 | Lids fused; pinna remains folded |
|  | 0 | Lids open; pinna flat/soft; slow recoil |
|  | 1 | Slightly curved pinna; soft cartilage; slow recoil |
|  | 2 | Well curved pinna; soft but formed; recoil present |
|  | 3 | Formed pinna; immediate recoil |
|  | 4 | Thick cartilage; firm recoil |
|  | 5 | — |
| **Male genitalia** | −1 | Scrotum flat and smooth |
|  | 0 | Empty scrotum; few rugae |
|  | 1 | Testes high in canal; rare rugae |
|  | 2 | Testes descending; few rugae |
|  | 3 | Testes down; good rugae |
|  | 4 | Pendulous scrotum; deep rugae |
|  | 5 | — |
| **Female genitalia** | −1 | Clitoris prominent; labia flat |
|  | 0 | Clitoris prominent; small labia minora |
|  | 1 | Clitoris prominent; enlarging labia minora |
|  | 2 | Labia majora and minora equally prominent |
|  | 3 | Labia majora enlarging; minora smaller |
|  | 4 | Labia majora cover clitoris and labia minora |
|  | 5 | — |

#### Total maturity score → approximate gestational age

| Total score | GA | Total score | GA | Total score | GA |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| −10 | 20 wk | 10 | 28 wk | 30 | 36 wk |
| −5 | 22 wk | 15 | 30 wk | 35 | 38 wk |
| 0 | 24 wk | 20 | 32 wk | 40 | 40 wk |
| 5 | 26 wk | 25 | 34 wk | 45 | 42 wk |
| 50 | 44 wk | | | | |

**Verification note:** This is the searchable reconstruction used in the newer v39 addition; the original source's pictorial cells remain the visual authority where a descriptor cannot be read confidently.

### Differentiating points based on appearance

| Feature | Preterm | Term |
|---|---|---|
| Posture | Extension | Flexion |
| Tone | Less tone | Good tone |
| Scarf sign | Positive — flexed elbow crosses the midline | Negative — flexed elbow does not cross the midline |
| External genitalia — male | Scrotum appears flat; testes not palpable as they descend by 36 weeks | Testes palpable |
| External genitalia — female | Labia majora and minora are visible | Labia majora are well developed and cover labia minora |
| Breast buds / nodule | <5 mm | >5 mm |
| Ear recoil | Slow (elastic cartilage not well developed) | Fast (well-developed elastic cartilage) |

### Points to identify post-term baby

1.  Meconium staining over nails, fingers and body.
2.  After 42 weeks, the anal sphincter opens.
3.  Overgrown fingernails.
4.  Wrinkling of skin due to decreased amniotic fluid.

### Page 6

## 2. Routine Newborn Care

### Page 6

### Routine newborn care

#### 1. At birth

-   Clean:
    -   Hands/gloves
    -   Surface/towel used to receive babies should be clean and warm.
    -   Blade/scissors for cutting the umbilical cord.
    -   Cord clamp.
    -   Baby should be dried.

### Delayed cord clamping

-   Delayed clamping by **30--60 seconds**.
-   Results in increased blood flow via placenta, which decreases risk
    of anemia in later life.
-   In preterm babies:
    -   Decreases risk of **intraventricular hemorrhage**.

#### 2. Prevention of hypothermia

-   Delivery-room temperature: **25°C**
-   No free draft of air in the delivery room.
-   Facilitate skin-to-skin contact of baby and mother.

#### 3. Breastfeeding

-   Should be started as early as possible after birth.

#### 4. Rooming-in

-   Keep the baby and mother together.
-   Facilitates bonding between mother and baby.
-   Also helps in breastfeeding.

#### 5. Prophylaxis at the time of birth

**Vitamin K** - Activates clotting factors II, VII, IX and X. - Given IM
in the anterolateral aspect of the thigh.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 7

### Vitamin K prophylaxis

-   Dose:
    -   **1 mg**
    -   If weight `<1 kg` → **0.5 mg**
-   Prevents **hemorrhagic disease of the newborn**.

### Normal observation in newborn

#### Vital signs

-   Temperature: **36.5--37.5°C**
-   Heart rate: **110--160/min**
-   Respiratory rate: **40--60/min**
-   Blood pressure: **60/40 mmHg**
-   **CFT (capillary filling time):** measure of circulation in the
    body.
    -   Checked over bony prominence.
    -   Preferred site: sternum.
    -   Normal: **\<3 seconds**
    -   In adults: **\<2 seconds**

### Normal findings in newborn

1.  Skin and mucosa
2.  Erythema toxicum neonatorum
3.  Pustular melanosis
4.  Effects of maternal estrogen
5.  Epithelial inclusion cysts
6.  Mongolian spots
7.  Strawberry angioma/salmon patch
8.  Physiological phimosis

> Erythema toxicum is a misnomer; now called **erythema neonatorum**.

### Erythema toxicum vs pustular melanosis

|Feature|Erythema toxicum|Pustular melanosis|
|---|---|---|
|Appearance|Red-and-yellow papulo-pustular lesions|Hyperpigmented patches + pustules|
|Onset|Appears after a few hours|Appears within 24 hours|
|Microscopy|Eosinophils|Neutrophils|
|Treatment|Reassurance|Reassurance|


------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 8

### Effects of maternal estrogen

-   White discharge per vagina (mucus from cervix).
-   Breast engorgement.
-   Vaginal bleeding may occur due to withdrawal of maternal estrogen
    leading to endometrial sloughing.

### Epithelial inclusion cyst/retention cyst

-   **Milia**
    -   Around the nose and face.
    -   Also called milk spots.
-   **Palatal Epstein pearls**
-   **Bohn's nodules**
-   Palate: Epstein pearls.

### Common skin findings

-   **Milia:** milk spots.
-   **Mongolian spots**
    -   Hyperpigmented macules.
    -   Very common in Indian and Asian people.
-   **Salmon patch/strawberry angioma/stork bite/nevus simplex**
    -   Bright red-coloured lesion.
    -   Also seen on the forehead and nape of the neck.

### Other normal findings

-   Small subconjunctival/retinal hemorrhages while passing through the
    birth canal.
-   Hymenal skin tags in female babies.
-   Physiological phimosis in male babies.

**Treatment:** Reassurance is the only treatment required for all normal
newborn findings.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 9

## Head swellings in newborn

|Feature|Caput succedaneum|Cephalhematoma|
|---|---|---|
|Location|Subcutaneous|Subperiosteal|
|Nature|Superficial swelling in scalp|Deep swelling, localized and limited by suture lines|
|Reason|Prolonged labour → congestion of scalp veins, edema|Instrumental delivery → trauma to skull|
|Contents|Fluid/fovea may be present|Blood|
|Appearance|At birth/soon after birth|Slowly increases over the first 12--24 hours after birth|
|Course|Resolves spontaneously|Resolves gradually as hematoma breaks down|
|Bilirubin|---|Breakdown of blood forms bilirubin|


------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 10

### Subgaleal hemorrhage

-   Beneath the galea aponeurosis.
-   Associated with **vacuum-assisted delivery**.
-   Diffuse accumulation of blood.
-   Pallor due to decreased circulating volume.
-   Jaundice due to lysis of RBCs.

### Head swellings summary

  Type                   Description
  ---------------------- -----------------------------------------------
  Caput succedaneum      Superficial, diffuse swelling
  Cephalhematoma         Deep, localized collection of blood
  Subgaleal hemorrhage   Diffuse collection of blood beneath the galea

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 11

## Neonatal reflexes

Also called **primitive/immature reflexes**.

Primitive reflexes disappear after the brain matures.

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
| Reflex | Onset | Fully developed | Duration |
|---|---|---|---|
| Palmar grasp | 8 weeks of gestation | 32 weeks of gestation | 2--8 months after birth |
| Rooting reflex | 28--32 weeks of gestation | 34--36 weeks of gestation | Less prominent 1 month after birth |
| Moro reflex | 28--32 weeks of gestation | 37 weeks of gestation (term) | 5--6 months after birth |
| Asymmetric tonic neck reflex | 35 weeks of gestation | 1 month after birth | 6--7 months after birth |
| Parachute reflex | 7--8 months (postnatal) | — | Persists throughout life |
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

### Palmar grasp

-   Present at birth and established during late gestation.
-   Usually integrates by about 4--6 months of age.

### Rooting reflex

-   Attachment of sucking while breastfeeding.
-   Develops during late gestation and is normally present at birth.
-   Usually integrates by about 3--4 months of age.

### Moro reflex

-   Seen mainly in term babies.
-   Fully developed around 28--32 weeks of gestation.
-   Disappears by 5--6 months.

### Asymmetric tonic neck reflex

-   Turning the head causes extension of limbs on one side and flexion
    on the other.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 12

### Components of Moro reflex

-   The Moro reflex is normally present at birth after appearing around 28--32 weeks of gestation.
-   It usually integrates by about 4--6 months.

-   Extension + abduction
-   Followed by adduction
-   Cry

### Abnormalities

#### Absent Moro reflex

Seen with: - Anomalies like **anencephaly** - **Hypoxic ischemic
encephalopathy**, especially stage 2 - Unilateral Moro reflex → **nerve
injury**, e.g. brachial plexus injury / Erb's palsy. - Bony injury such
as fracture/dislocation, most commonly shoulder. - **Most common
fracture at the time of birth:** fracture clavicle. - Persistent Moro
even after 10 months → immaturity of brain / **cerebral palsy**.

### Asymmetric tonic neck reflex

-   Appears in late gestation and is normally present at birth.
-   Usually integrates by about 4--6 months.

If the baby's head is turned to one side: - Upper limb on the same side
extends. - Other upper limb becomes flexed.

### Rooting reflex

Stimulation of the baby's mouth causes the mouth to turn in the
direction of the stimulus.

### Reflexes appearing after birth / infantile reflexes

**Mnemonic: SPL (special)**

-   Protective extension reaction forward prevents head from falling
    down.
-   **Forward parachute reflex**

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 13

### Symmetrical tonic neck reflex

Also called **crawling reflex**.

-   Neck extension → upper-limb extension + lower-limb flexion.
-   Neck flexion → upper-limb flexion + lower-limb extension.
-   Appears by **4--10 months after birth**.
-   Duration: **8--14 months after birth**.
-   Helps in crawling.

### Landau reflex

On horizontal suspension: - Flexion of neck → flexion of limbs. -
Extension of neck → extension of limbs.

-   Appears: **3 months after birth**
-   Duration: **9 months after birth**

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## 3. Management of Low Birth Weight Babies

### Page 14

## Management of LBW babies

### Reasons for LBW

1.  IUGR/growth restriction --- common in developing countries.
2.  Most common problem faced by these babies: **hypothermia**.

### Temperature regulation in a newborn

-   Normal: **36.5--37.5°C**
-   Site of measurement: **axilla**
-   For precise recording:
    -   Bulb of thermometer should be placed over the root of a dry
        axilla.
    -   Keep for a minimum of 3 minutes while holding the axilla close
        to the baby's body.

### Modes of heat loss in a newborn

1.  **Conduction**
    -   Baby loses heat when placed on a cold surface.
2.  **Evaporation**
    -   Loss of heat with evaporation of water/sweating.
3.  **Convection**
    -   Current or airflow around the baby causes heat loss.
4.  **Radiation**
    -   Heat from the baby radiates to roof/walls of the room.

**Most important mechanism of heat loss:** Radiation.

**Most important site of heat loss:** Head, because it has the largest
body surface area.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 15

-   Baby's head must be covered to prevent excessive heat loss.

### Why LBW babies are more prone to hypothermia

|LBW/preterm|Normal-weight baby|
|---|---|
|Extension posture → more heat loss|Flexion posture → protects from heat loss|
|Less body fat → less insulation → heat loss|More body fat|


### Sources of heat production

-   Increased physical activity:
    -   Crying/movements → increased heat production.
-   Cold exposure → stress → sympathetic surge → release of sympathetic
    hormones → increased heat production through **brown fat**.

### Brown fat

Brown fat cells are specialized structures present in newborns that help
in heat production.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 16

### Location of brown fat

1.  Nape/back side of neck --- most common.
2.  Interscapular region.
3.  Axillary or groin region.
4.  Perirenal areas.

## Non-shivering thermogenesis

Mechanism of generation of heat in a newborn without shivering, with the
help of brown fat tissue.

-   In a fat cell, oxidation of fatty acids takes place, followed by
    phosphorylation.
-   Normally, this produces ATP.
-   With cold exposure:
    -   Stress
    -   Sympathetic surge
    -   Release of sympathetic hormones, especially norepinephrine
    -   Phosphorylation is inhibited.
    -   Oxidation is diverted towards heat generation.

### Hypothermia

-   Normal temperature: **36.5--37.5°C**

**Classification:** 1. **Cold stress:** 36.0--36.4°C - Extremities
become cold, trunk remains warm. - Initial stage. 2. **Moderate
hypothermia:** 34--36°C - Entire body becomes cold. 3. **Severe
hypothermia:** `<32°C` - Entire body becomes cold.

### Prevention of hypothermia

For a baby who is hemodynamically stable: - Gently cover the baby,
especially the head. - If LBW/preterm: - **Kangaroo mother care
(skin-to-skin contact)**

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 17

## Kangaroo Mother Care (KMC)

-   Heat transfer from mother to baby protects baby from hypothermia.

### Position

-   Baby: vertical/upright.
-   Face: turned to one side.
-   Legs: flexed at hip and knees --- **frog-leg posture**.
-   Head: additionally covered.

### Feeding

-   Exclusive breastfeeding.

### Early discharge

-   Early discharge of baby due to better care because of contact with
    mother.

### Duration

-   Variable; can be up to **24 hours**.
-   Long duration is most preferred.

### KMC initiation

-   Term baby: from birth.
-   Preterm baby: when the baby is stable.
-   **Short duration (<1 hour/session) is avoided.**
-   KMC can be stopped when the baby is **term** and/or weighs **>1.5 kg**.

### Unstable hemodynamically

-   Manage in NICU.

### Radiant warmer and incubator

-   Heat generated over the surface radiates down to the baby.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 18

## Nutrition of LBW babies

### \<28 weeks of gestation / extremely premature

-   **Total parenteral nutrition (TPN) + IV fluids**
-   No enteral feeding as gastrointestinal tract is not completely
    developed.

### Preferred IV fluids

-   First 48 hours: **10% dextrose**
-   Day 1: **60--80 mL/kg/24 hours**
-   Increase by **10--20 mL/kg every day**
-   Up to **150 mL/kg/24 hours** maximum, then maintain.

### Feeding in very premature babies

-   Only suckling burst is seen.
-   Suckling + swallowing + breathing are not fully coordinated.
-   Milk should be given carefully.
-   Baby may aspirate if given directly by mouth.
-   Therefore, feeds are given through:
    -   Nasogastric tube
    -   Orogastric tube

### Length of insertion of NG tube

-   Nose → ear lobule → midpoint between xiphoid and umbilicus.
-   Should be measured and marked, then inserted.

### 28--34 weeks of gestation

-   Coordination is better developed.
-   Expressed breast milk can be given with:
    -   **Paladai**
    -   **Katori/spoon**

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 19

## Nutritional supplements

  Weight                               Supplements
  ------------------------------------ -----------------------------------
  1--1.5 kg                            Vitamin D + iron
  \<1.5 kg --- very low birth weight   Iron + Human Milk Fortifier (HMF)

-   Iron: approximately **4 mg/kg/day** from 4--8 weeks after birth
    until 1 year.
-   LBW babies have inadequate iron stores.
-   Nutritional sachets continued until the baby reaches **40 weeks of
    gestation after birth**.
-   Continue iron + vitamin D for 1 year.
-   HMF contains micronutrients, minerals and vitamins except iron.
-   Therefore iron supplementation is also required.

### Problems faced by LBW babies

-   Low nutrient reserves
-   Hypoglycemia
-   Hypocalcemia
-   Kernicterus
    -   High levels of bilirubin
    -   Easy passage into the brain because the blood-brain barrier is
        more permeable in preterm babies
-   Increased risk of polycythemia
    -   Intrauterine hypoxia → chronic stress → release of EPO →
        increased erythropoiesis → polycythemia.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 20

### Problems faced by premature babies due to immaturity of organ systems

#### Respiratory problems

-   Less surfactant.
-   Meconium aspiration may occur due to intrauterine stress.

#### Intraventricular hemorrhage (IVH)

-   Germinal matrix is a group of fragile blood vessels around the
    ventricles.
-   Germinal matrix undergoes involution after approximately 24 weeks.
-   Baby `<34 weeks` → germinal matrix still present → prone to bleeding
    → rupture → bleeding into ventricles.

#### Apnea of prematurity

-   Increased risk of apnea of prematurity.
-   Apnea = absent breathing for **20 seconds or more**.

#### Sensorineural hearing loss

-   Increased risk of developing sensorineural hearing loss.
-   **Otoacoustic emissions (OAE)** preferred.

#### Retinopathy of prematurity

-   Increased risk of developing ROP, possibly leading to blindness.
-   Due to immaturity of retina.
-   Monitoring of SpO₂; target in the notes: **90--95% maintenance**.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## 4. Neonatal Resuscitation

### Page 21

## Neonatal Resuscitation Program / NRP 2021

### CURRENT EXAM-FACING UPDATE — 2025 AHA/AAP Neonatal Resuscitation / NRP 9th edition

> **This current box supersedes conflicting older FiO₂, escalation or timing values elsewhere in this chapter.** The classic Marrow SpO₂ transition table is retained, but oxygen-start recommendations should be learned from the 2025 AHA/AAP guidance.

**Core sequence:** warm, dry, stimulate and position the airway → assess breathing and heart rate → if apneic/gasping or HR <100/min, initiate PPV promptly → titrate oxygen with pulse oximetry → optimize ventilation before compressions → if HR remains <60/min after 30 seconds of effective ventilation that moves the chest, begin compressions.

#### Initial oxygen

| Gestational age / situation | Initial FiO₂ |
|---|---:|
| ≥35 weeks | **21% (room air)** |
| 32 to 34+6/7 weeks | **21–30%** |
| <32 weeks | **30–100%**, titrated rapidly to target SpO₂ |

#### Target preductal SpO₂ during transition

| Time after birth | Target preductal SpO₂ |
|---|---:|
| 1 min | 60–65% |
| 2 min | 65–70% |
| 3 min | 70–75% |
| 4 min | 75–80% |
| 5 min | 80–85% |
| 10 min or later | 85–95% |

Use the **right hand or right wrist** for preductal measurement. These are transition targets, not later NICU saturation targets.

#### High-yield decision points

| Trigger | Action |
|---|---|
| HR <100/min with apnea/gasping or ineffective breathing | **PPV**; ensure chest movement and rising HR |
| HR remains <60/min despite 30 s effective ventilation | **Chest compressions**, ideally with an alternative airway; **3:1** compression:ventilation ratio |
| HR remains <60/min after compressions + ventilation | **Epinephrine**; umbilical venous access preferred; IO may be considered if IV/umbilical access is not feasible |
| No HR after ~20 min despite all steps effectively completed | Discuss **redirection of care** with the team/family, considering circumstances and local protocol |

#### Key airway / ventilation points retained from the newer reconstruction

- Correct mask seal and airway position are essential; if HR does not rise, perform ventilation corrective steps before escalating.
- Endotracheal tube selection: uncuffed tube is generally preferred in newborns; approximate internal diameter **2.5 mm (<1 kg), 3.0 mm (1–2 kg), 3.5 mm (2–3 kg)**.
- ET insertion depth may be estimated as **6 + weight (kg) cm**; confirm position with clinical assessment and **exhaled CO₂/capnography** where available.
- Compressions use the lower half of the sternum and are coordinated 3:1 with ventilation.

**Source-vs-current note:** The Marrow page contains a historical oxygen-start scheme and a classic 1–10 minute target table. Keep the transition SpO₂ table, but use the current gestational-age oxygen-start scheme above for exam preparation.


The guidelines for neonatal resuscitation are based on **NRP**.

### Priority in newborn babies

**Respiration → temperature → circulation**

Resuscitation in newborns is not done under room air but according to
the NRP algorithm.

### Initial assessment

Four pre-birth questions are asked:

1.  Gestational age of the baby.
2.  Is the amniotic fluid clear?
3.  Additional risk factors.
4.  Is there good muscle tone / breathing or crying?

### Umbilical cord management

-   Delayed cord clamping by **30--60 seconds**.

### Algorithm

-   Assess crying/breathing effort.
-   If no response, proceed through the resuscitation algorithm.
-   If still no improvement and heart rate is `<60/min`, escalate
    resuscitation.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 22

## Initial steps

### Temperature

-   Needs to be maintained by putting the baby under the warmer.

### Stimulation

1.  Rubbing the back of the baby.
2.  Drying the baby.

### Airway position

-   Slight extension/sniffing posture.
-   Easily done by placing a rolled cotton towel behind the shoulder
    blades.

### Suctioning

-   Optional step.
-   Suction secretions from:
    -   Mouth first
    -   Then nose
-   Routine suctioning is no longer recommended.

### Heart rate

If the baby still does not cry after initial steps, assess heart rate to
check for hypoxia.

-   Significant hypoxia causes a decrease in heart rate.
-   `HR <100/min` is abnormal.
-   Normal HR: approximately **140--160/min**.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 23

## Assessment and respiratory support

### If HR ≥100/min but breathing is inadequate/labored

-   Oxygen hood

### If HR \<100/min and baby is not breathing/apneic

-   Babies `<32 weeks`: CPAP / appropriate respiratory support.
-   Bag-mask ventilation.
-   Endotracheal tube if required.

### Chest compressions

If there is still no response and heart rate is `<60/min`, chest
compressions are indicated.

-   **Two-thumb technique**
-   Site: lower one-third of sternum.
-   Avoid xiphisternum.
-   Compress approximately one-third of the anterior-posterior diameter
    of the chest.
-   Chest compressions should be combined with ventilation.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 24

## Neonatal resuscitation --- chest compression

-   Site: lower half of sternum.
-   Avoid xiphoid process.

### Compression : ventilation ratio

**3:1**

-   90 compressions
-   30 ventilations
-   Total = 120 events/minute.

Ventilation is usually given by an alternative airway such as
endotracheal ventilation.

-   Cardiac monitor is necessary at this stage to monitor HR.

### Adrenaline

If there is no response to chest compressions:

-   Adrenaline injection: **1:10,000 strength**
-   Route: **umbilical vein (IV preferred)**

**Dose** - IV/intraosseous: **0.2 mL/kg or 0.02 mg/kg** - Endotracheal:
**1 mL/kg or 0.1 mg/kg**

### Volume expansion

If there is still no improvement: - Consider volume expansion with
**normal saline 10 mL/kg**. - Not a routine step. - Usually a last
effort when definite blood loss is present, e.g. PPH.

### Time limits

-   Maximum duration of neonatal resuscitation: **20 minutes** in the
    notes.
-   Call off resuscitation if all steps are done and there is no HR.
-   Initial steps → PPV: approximately **1 minute**
-   PPV → chest-compression initiation: **30 seconds**
-   Chest compression → adrenaline: **30 seconds**

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 25

## Bag-and-mask ventilation / BMV

-   Volume of bag for resuscitation: **240--750 mL**
-   Desired response: increase in HR to `>100/min`.

### If no response

Perform ventilation corrective steps:

1.  Reposition head.
2.  Open the mouth.
3.  Increase pressure.
4.  Use an alternative airway (ET tube).

### Correct position of mask

### Pressure

-   First breath: **30--40 cm H₂O**
-   Subsequent breaths: **15--20 cm H₂O**
    -   Lower pressure to prevent alveolar trauma.

### Recommended FiO₂

-   Babies born `≥35 weeks`: **room air (21% O₂)**
-   Babies born `<35 weeks`: **21--30% O₂**
-   Further titration depends on SpO₂ response.

### Target SpO₂

| Time after birth | Target SpO₂ |
|---|---:|
| 1 min | 60–65% |
| 2 min | 65–70% |
| 3 min | 70–75% |
| 4 min | 75–80% |
| 5 min | 80–85% |
| 10 min | 85–95% |

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 26

### Oxygen

-   Excessive oxygen can lead to deleterious effects.
-   Untreated oxygen-related injury can lead to blindness.

### Pulse oximetry

-   SpO₂ is usually checked by placing the pulse oximeter on the **right
    hand**.
-   Target SpO₂ at 3 minutes: **70--75%**.

### Congenital diaphragmatic hernia

In congenital diaphragmatic hernia: - Intestines are already compressing
the lungs. - Positive-pressure ventilation can push more air into the
intestines, leading to further compression of the lungs.

## Endotracheal intubation

-   Before ET:
    -   Laryngoscopy with a straight blade.
    -   `0` for preterm and `1` for term babies.
    -   Visualize the glottic region.
-   **Uncuffed tube** is preferred in newborns.
-   Cuffed ET may cause airway/laryngeal injury.

### Internal diameter of ET tube

-   `<1 kg` child: **2.5 mm**
-   `1–2 kg`: **3.0 mm**
-   `2–3 kg`: **3.5 mm**

### Length of insertion

-   Length depends on weight.
-   Notes give:

**ET-tube insertion length (cm) = 6 + weight of child (kg)**

### Best way to confirm ET position

-   **End-tidal CO₂ with capnography**

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 27

## 5. Infections in Neonates

## Types of infections

Superficial infections and systemic infections.

### Superficial infections

#### 1. Omphalitis

- Infection of the umbilical cord/umbilicus.
- Can present as redness, induration and pus discharge around the umbilicus.
- If untreated, it can cause systemic infection (sepsis).
- Management depends on the size of induration and the presence of sepsis.

| Size of induration | Presence of sepsis | Treatment |
|---|---|---|
| Less than 1 cm | No | Topical 0.5% gentian violet, 4 times a day |
| More than 1 cm | Yes/No | Systemic antibiotics (treat like sepsis) |

#### 2. Oral thrush

- Caused by *Candida* species.
- Differentiated from other white oral lesions by:
  - Difficulty in wiping off the lesion.
  - Presence of hemorrhagic spots after forceful removal.
- Treated with topical **nystatin** or **clotrimazole**, 4 times a day, until all lesions are resolved.

#### 3. Conjunctivitis (ophthalmia neonatorum)

- Acquired during delivery of the baby along the infected birth canal.

|  | Gonococci | Chlamydia |
|---|---|---|
| **Organism** | *Neisseria gonorrhoeae* | *Chlamydia trachomatis* |
| **Time of presentation** | Day 3-7 | From 5th day of delivery |
| **Discharge** | Copious and purulent | Scanty |
| **Treatment** | IV ceftriaxone/cefotaxime for 7-10 days | Oral azithromycin for 3 days / erythromycin for 14 days |

### Page 28

## Gonococcal conjunctivitis

- Treated as an emergency.
- Blindness and systemic infection can occur if untreated.
- **0.5% erythromycin eye ointment** is used as prophylaxis against ophthalmia neonatorum.

## Systemic infections

### Neonatal sepsis

- Occurs in the **first month of life**.
- Listed  as the **2nd most common cause of neonatal mortality after prematurity**.
- Diagnosed by the presence of clinical features of sepsis along with bacteremia (presence of bacteria in blood as detected by blood culture).

### Causative organisms

| Setting | Causative organisms |
|---|---|
| **India** | *Klebsiella* > *Staphylococcus aureus* > *E. coli* |
| **World** | Coagulase-negative staphylococci; Group B Streptococcus is also highlighted in the source |

### Early-onset vs late-onset sepsis

| Feature | Early-onset sepsis | Late-onset sepsis |
|---|---|---|
| **Onset** | Within 72 hours of life | After 72 hours of life |
| **Source** | From the mother (in utero / passage via birth canal) | Hospital-acquired infection (nosocomial) |
| **Risk factors** | • Chorioamnionitis (high fever in mother + foul-smelling liquor); • Duration of ROM >24 hours (>72 hours: very high risk); • PPROM (any duration); • ≥3 unclean vaginal examinations | • Unclean hands of healthcare personnel (most important preventable cause); • Preterm/low-birth-weight babies; • Absence of breast feeding |
| **Type of infection** | Pneumonia | Septicemia, meningitis |

### Page 29

## Diagnosis of neonatal sepsis

-   Any sick baby → suspect sepsis → start IV antibiotics.
-   No single most important clinical feature is diagnostic.

### Early features

-   Altered feeding pattern
-   Lethargic baby

### Reliable clinical feature

-   Respiratory distress is emphasized as a more reliable feature,
    particularly in preterm babies.
-   Manifestations of affected organ system:
    -   Respiratory distress
    -   Seizures
    -   etc.

### Investigation

#### Blood culture

-   Takes approximately **48--72 hours** for the result.

### Sepsis screen

Screen for sepsis using the following components; presence of any
significant abnormality may support sepsis.

#### Total leukocyte count

-   `<5000/µL` --- abnormal in the notes.
-   Immature immune system.

#### Absolute neutrophil count

-   Neutropenia is significant.

#### Immature/total neutrophil ratio

-   `>20%` immature neutrophils suggests infection.

### If screening is negative

-   Absence of disease is more likely.

### If screening is positive

-   Do blood culture to confirm sepsis.

### C-reactive protein

-   Role: supportive marker.
-   Disadvantage: can be negative in early infection.

### Procalcitonin

-   Newer/better marker of inflammation.
-   Not routinely used in sepsis screening.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 30

## Other investigations

-   Chest X-ray in suspected pneumonia.
-   Lumbar puncture in symptomatic babies with suspected/confirmed
    sepsis, except where contraindicated.

## Management

### Empirical antibiotics

-   **Ampicillin + Amikacin/Gentamicin**

For suspected meningitis, septic shock or other severe infection,
treatment is intensified as indicated.

### Suspected *Staphylococcus aureus* infection

-   Add **Cloxacillin**.

### MRSA

-   Add **Vancomycin**.

### Duration of antibiotic therapy in neonatal sepsis

|Diagnosis | Duration |
|---|---|
|Culture-negative sepsis / screen negative with reassuring course | 5–7 days |
|Blood-culture positive without meningitis | 14 days |
|Meningitis | 3 weeks |
|Ventriculitis | 4–6 weeks |


------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 31

## 6. Birth Asphyxia, Hypoxic-Ischemic Encephalopathy and Neonatal Seizures

### Page 31

## Perinatal asphyxia

Impaired gas exchange in the fetus during pregnancy or due to
complications of labour.

### Leads to

-   Hypoxia
-   Hypercarbia
-   Umbilical arterial blood pH `<7.2` in the notes.

### WHO definition of birth asphyxia

A condition considered in any newborn baby wherein the baby fails to
initiate breathing or is not able to sustain breathing at the time of
birth.

### Consequences of birth asphyxia

-   Hypoxic ischemic encephalopathy (HIE)
-   Multi-organ failure
-   Brain injury
-   Anaerobic lactic acidosis in tissues

### HIE

HIE is seen in babies with birth asphyxia and is associated with: -
Hypoxia - Multi-organ injury - Brain injury

### APGAR score

APGAR score is noted as an assessment associated with birth asphyxia.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 32

### Hypoxia

-   Decreased oxygen to tissues.

### Ischemia

-   Hypoperfusion
-   Inadequate blood supply to tissues.

### HIE

-   Leading cause of neonatal brain injury worldwide.
-   Associated with increased risk of neonatal mortality.
-   Surviving babies may have neurodevelopmental sequelae such as:
    -   Cerebral palsy
    -   Intractable seizures

## Sarnat & Sarnat staging

### CURRENT SEARCHABLE SARNAT/SARNAT RECONSTRUCTION — including duration and prognosis

The original source continues the Sarnat table across pages 32–33 with **duration and outcome** columns. The searchable reconstruction makes those exam-relevant columns explicit.

| Feature | Stage I — mild | Stage II — moderate | Stage III — severe |
|---|---|---|---|
| Consciousness | Hyperalert | Lethargic | Stupor / coma |
| Neuromuscular tone | Normal / increased | Hypotonia | Flaccid |
| Reflexes / suck | Hyperreflexia; strong suck often | Sluggish suck; Moro weak | Absent suck; Moro absent |
| Seizures | Uncommon | Common; often within first 24 h | May be absent despite severe encephalopathy |
| Autonomic | Sympathetic predominance | Parasympathetic predominance | Variable / often depressed |
| Pupils | Mydriasis | Miosis | Variable; may be unequal/poorly reactive |
| Heart rate | Increased | Decreased | Variable |
| Posture | Normal | Flexion / distal flexion | Decerebrate |
| EEG | Relatively normal | Low voltage; seizure activity may appear | Severe suppression / burst-suppression may occur; may become isoelectric |
| Typical duration | <24 h | ~2–14 d | Days to weeks; may persist |
| Prognosis | Usually normal | Higher risk of neurological sequelae | High risk of death/severe neurological sequelae |

**Exam pearl:** Sarnat stage is both a severity descriptor and a prognostic indicator. A baby does **not** have to progress sequentially from Stage I → II → III and may present directly in any stage.

**Neonatal seizures / HIE continuity:** HIE is a major cause of neonatal seizures. Stabilize airway/ventilation/oxygenation, glucose, temperature, blood pressure and electrolytes; obtain aEEG/EEG when available; consider therapeutic hypothermia when eligibility criteria and timing are met; treat persistent electrographic/clinical seizures according to current neonatal seizure guidance. Phenobarbital remains a common first-line agent, and unexplained refractory seizures should raise consideration of pyridoxine-dependent epilepsy.


| Feature | Stage 1 | Stage 2 | Stage 3 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Consciousness | Normal / hyperalert | Lethargic | Comatose |
| Reflexes (Moro, sucking) | Easily elicitable / hyperactive | Sluggish | Absent |
| Seizures | − | +++ in first 24 hours | − (no brain activity) |

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 33

### Sarnat & Sarnat staging --- additional features

-   **Autonomic nervous system**
    -   Stage 1: sympathetic predominance
    -   Later stages: parasympathetic changes.
-   **Pupils**
    -   Mydriasis
    -   Miosis
    -   May become unequal/non-reactive.
-   **EEG**
    -   Normal/completely normal pattern in mild disease.
    -   Low-voltage pattern and seizure spikes may be seen in severe
        disease.
-   Course may range from **days to weeks**.

### Posture

-   **Normal posture:** universal flexion resisting passive extension.
-   **Decerebrate posture:** all limbs in extension.

### Prognosis

-   The notes state:
    -   In stage 2, around 20% of babies develop neurological sequelae.
    -   In stage 3, all surviving babies/approximately 80% of survivors
        develop cerebral palsy as sequelae.
-   A child does not always progress sequentially from stage 1 to stage
    3 and may present in any stage.

**Sarnat & Sarnat staging is also indicative of prognosis.**

### Neonatal seizures

-   HIE is the leading cause of neonatal seizures.
-   Neurological sequelae depend on:
    -   Area of brain affected by HIE
    -   Blood supply of brain
    -   Gestational age
    -   Metabolic demands of different parts of the baby's brain

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 34

## Parasagittal infarct

### Parasagittal cerebral injury

-   Brain injury in **term babies** due to asphyxia.
-   Watershed area supplied between the **anterior and middle cerebral
    arteries**.
-   Motor cortex/subcortical areas are affected.
-   Initially, upper limbs are predominantly affected.
-   Eventually all four limbs can be affected → **spastic quadriplegic
    cerebral palsy**.
-   Lower-limb motor distribution can also be affected.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 35

## Other patterns of ischemic brain injury in term babies

### Focal ischemic necrosis

-   Associated with:
    -   Focal seizures
    -   Hemiparesis
-   Upper or lower limb on one side may be affected.

### Selective neuronal necrosis

Particular areas of the brain are commonly affected:

-   Deep nuclei of brain:
    -   Putamen of basal ganglia
    -   Causes extrapyramidal symptoms.
-   Hippocampus
-   Purkinje cells of cerebellum:
    -   Ataxia
    -   Vertigo

### Multiple areas affected together

-   Cerebral palsy with low IQ.

### Imaging

-   Cranial ultrasound visualizes only a small part of the brain.
-   Used to rule out significant intracranial hemorrhage.
-   **MRI** is the definitive imaging modality for brain injury.
-   MRI can show early abnormalities within a few hours after birth.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 36

## Treatment of HIE

-   Cannot completely cure; only supportive management.
-   Maintain:
    -   Blood sugar
    -   Temperature
    -   Adequate fluids

### aEEG

**Amplitude-integrated EEG**

-   Monitoring of seizure activity in a newborn.
-   Only 1--2 electrodes are used.
-   Conventional EEG uses many more electrodes.
-   Conventional EEG requires expert evaluation.
-   Facilitates continuous monitoring of seizure activity.
-   Helps detect subclinical seizure activity in ICU.
-   Screening test only.
-   Diagnostic test: conventional EEG.

### Therapeutic hypothermia

-   Inducing hypothermia by placing ice packs/cooling devices.
-   Can be:
    -   Selective head cooling
    -   Whole-body cooling
-   Whole-body cooling is preferred because it offers uniform cooling
    and better outcomes.

### Temperature

-   Maintained around **33--34°C**.
-   Notes state approximately **32.5°C**.

### Mechanism

-   Decreases neuronal injury by:
    -   Decreasing neuronal apoptosis
    -   Decreasing production of free radicals and nitric oxide
    -   Decreasing production of excitatory amino acid glutamate
    -   Decreasing incidence of seizures

### Criteria for therapeutic hypothermia

-   Post-menstrual age: `≥36 weeks` of gestation.
-   Birth weight: `≥2 kg`
-   Stage 2 / Stage 3 HIE.
-   Should be started within **6 hours of birth**.
-   Duration: **72 hours**

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 37

## Types of neonatal seizures

### Subtle seizures

Minimal manifestations.

-   Ocular movements:
    -   Deviation of eyes
    -   Continuous blinking for a few seconds
-   Orofacial/lingual movements
-   Limb movements:
    -   Cycling movement

Seizure-generated impulse is not transmitted rapidly in the immature
brain.

### Clonic seizures

-   Focal clonic
-   Best prognosis.

### Myoclonic seizures

-   Brief, rapid muscle jerks that may be focal, multifocal or generalized.
-   In neonates, myoclonic-appearing movements can be epileptic or nonepileptic; **EEG correlation is important** when the diagnosis is uncertain.


### Hypotonic seizures

-   Worst prognosis.

## Etiology

1.  **HIE**
    -   Most common cause of neonatal seizures in the notes.
    -   May occur within hours after birth.
2.  **Metabolic causes**
    -   Hypoglycemia
    -   Hypocalcemia
    -   Hypomagnesemia
3.  **Infections**
    -   Sepsis
    -   TORCH infections
4.  **Intracranial hemorrhage**
    -   Term: subdural hemorrhage is noted.
    -   Associated with poor outcome.
5.  **Developmental defects**
    -   Congenital anomalies, e.g. anencephaly.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 38

### Pyridoxine deficiency/dependent seizures

-   Rare cause of neonatal seizures.
-   Typically manifests as **refractory seizures**.

### Neurotransmitter pathway

-   Glutamate → excitatory
-   GABA → inhibitory
-   Pyridoxine is involved in conversion of glutamate to GABA via
    glutamic acid decarboxylase.

## Management of neonatal seizures

**TABC** - Temperature - Airway - Breathing - Circulation

### Correct reversible causes

-   Hypoglycemia
-   Hypocalcemia

### Anticonvulsant treatment

-   **First line:** Phenobarbitone IV
-   **Second line:** Phenytoin followed by benzodiazepines
-   **Refractory seizures:** trial of pyridoxine

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 39

## 7. Necrotizing Enterocolitis

### Page 39

## Necrotizing Enterocolitis (NEC)

### Introduction

-   Most commonly affected part of intestine: **terminal ileum**.
-   Considered a gastrointestinal emergency in a neonate.
-   Characterized by inflammation due to bacterial colonization of the
    intestine, leading to necrosis of that part of the intestine.

### Risk factors

-   Prematurity
-   Cow's milk/formula milk
-   Maternal abuse of cocaine
-   Use of H₂ blockers in the neonate
-   Asphyxia

### Why breast milk is protective

1.  Lowers gastric pH, making it more acidic and preventing bacterial
    colonization by pathogenic organisms such as:
    -   *E. coli*
    -   *Klebsiella*
2.  Promotes intestinal peristalsis.
3.  Contains substances such as **lactoferrin**, which protects against
    growth of bacteria such as *E. coli*.
4.  Increases normal gut flora.

### Pathogenesis

-   Prematurity → immature GI tract → inflammation → necrosis.
-   Top feeds → bacterial colonization → inflammation → necrosis.
-   Asphyxia → vasoconstriction of splanchnic vessels → predisposition
    to necrosis → NEC.
-   Cocaine abuse → vasoconstriction of splanchnic vessels →
    predisposition to necrosis → NEC.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 40

-   H₂-blocker use → decreases HCl amount in stomach → increases gastric
    pH → loss of protection from bacterial colonization.

## Features of NEC

### CURRENT SEARCHABLE MODIFIED BELL STAGING + STAGE-LINKED MANAGEMENT

The exam-relevant distinction is **Stage IIIA = severe NEC without perforation** versus **Stage IIIB = advanced NEC with intestinal perforation**.

| Stage | Core pattern | Imaging / key finding | Management framework |
|---|---|---|---|
| IA/IB — suspected | Feeding intolerance, abdominal distention, occult/gross blood; minimal systemic illness | Normal/nonspecific X-ray | Stop feeds, gastric decompression, IV support, serial examination/X-ray; antibiotics when clinically indicated |
| IIA — definite, mild | Definite NEC; mild systemic illness, tenderness/absent bowel sounds | Pneumatosis intestinalis | NPO + decompression + IV fluids/nutrition + antibiotics; close monitoring |
| IIB — definite, moderate | Increasing systemic illness, acidosis, thrombocytopenia | Portal venous gas may occur | Intensive medical management + early surgical consultation |
| IIIA — advanced, severe, **no perforation** | Severe systemic illness, hypotension/apnea, worsening abdominal findings/ascites | Severe bowel-wall changes ± ascites | Intensive medical management + urgent surgical review; evaluate closely for perforation |
| IIIB — advanced, **perforated** | Advanced NEC with intestinal perforation | **Pneumoperitoneum / free air** | Surgical emergency: laparotomy with resection when appropriate; primary peritoneal drainage may be used in selected unstable infants |

**Prevention / feeding:** human milk is central to NEC prevention. Antenatal corticosteroids reduce prematurity-related complications. Minimal enteral/trophic feeds may be used in selected very preterm infants while nutrition is otherwise supported parenterally. The older source specifically lists enterally fed probiotics; probiotic recommendations vary by product, jurisdiction and current neonatal guideline and should not be treated as universally mandatory.


### Modified Bell's staging

|Stage|Clinical findings|Imaging |
|---|---|---|
|Stage I (suspected)|Non-specific abdominal findings such as distension, feeding intolerance, occult/gross blood in stool|Normal or nonspecific X-ray |
|Stage II A|Definite NEC; mild systemic illness, absent bowel sounds/abdominal tenderness|Pneumatosis intestinalis |
|Stage II B|Moderate systemic illness; metabolic acidosis and thrombocytopenia may occur|Portal venous gas ± ascites |
|Stage III A|Advanced NEC with severe systemic illness, hypotension/apnea|Severe bowel-wall changes ± ascites |
|Stage III B|Perforated NEC|Pneumoperitoneum |


### Laboratory findings

-   Thrombocytopenia
-   Hyponatremia
-   Metabolic acidosis

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 41

## Stage 3: severe / complicated / advanced NEC

- **Stage 3A:** definite evidence of peritonitis, erythema of the abdominal wall, induration and tenderness of the abdominal wall, with definite ascites.
- **Stage 3B:** intestinal perforation with air collecting in the peritoneal cavity; this is a surgical gastrointestinal emergency in neonates.

## Management of NEC

| Stage | Management | Treatment |
|---|---|---|
| **Till Stage 3A** | Medical management (since there is no bowel perforation) | **NPO**; **TPN + IV fluids with dextrose**; IV **ampicillin + gentamicin + metronidazole** (anaerobic coverage). Antibiotic duration: Stage 1A **3 days**, Stage 1B **7–10 days**, Stage 2A/2B/3A **14 days**. |
| **Stage 3B** | Surgical management | **Stable:** laparotomy with resection of necrotized bowel followed by anastomosis. **Unstable:** primary peritoneal drainage (PPD); after stabilization, definitive surgery. |

## Prevention of NEC

-   **Antenatal steroids**
    -   Dexamethasone/betamethasone.
    -   Help in maturation of lungs and prevent respiratory distress
        syndrome.
-   Prevent abnormal intestinal colonization.
-   **Minimal enteral nutrition (MEN)**, especially useful in small
    preterm babies.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 42

### Minimal enteral nutrition

-   In babies `<28 weeks` of gestation or hemodynamically unstable
    preterm babies:
    -   TPN is given.
    -   Small amounts of enteral feeds are given as **minimal enteral
        nutrition**.
-   Expressed breast milk can be given using a nasogastric tube.
-   MEN helps:
    -   Promote maturation of the GIT.
    -   Decrease risk of NEC.
-   Also called **trophic feeds**.

### Normal gut flora

-   *Lactobacillus* spp.
-   *Bifidobacterium* spp.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 43

## 8. Respiratory Distress in Newborn

### Page 43

## Features of respiratory distress

### CURRENT SEARCHABLE RESPIRATORY DISTRESS / TTN / RDS RECONSTRUCTION

**Definition:** respiratory distress is a clinical picture that may include **tachypnea (>60/min), chest retractions/indrawing, grunting and/or cyanosis**.

#### Silverman–Andersen score

| Finding | 0 | 1 | 2 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Upper chest retractions | Synchronous with abdomen | Lag / inward motion during breathing | Seesaw pattern |
| Lower chest retractions | None | Mild | Marked |
| Nasal flaring | None | Mild | Marked |
| Xiphoid retraction | None | Mild | Marked |
| Expiratory grunt | None | Heard with stethoscope | Heard with naked ear |

**Interpretation:** 0 = normal; higher scores indicate increasing respiratory distress. The source notes **>7** as severe/impending respiratory failure.

#### RDS severity → management

| Severity | Practical management pattern |
|---|---|
| <5 — mild | Warm/humidified oxygen as needed; target saturation; consider early CPAP |
| 5–7 — moderate | CPAP; titrate pressure/FiO₂; assess oxygen requirement and response; consider surfactant if persistent |
| >7 — severe | CPAP/ventilatory support; early surfactant; intubation/mechanical ventilation if non-invasive support fails |

#### TTN — source risk-factor pattern

- Caesarean delivery, especially without labour.
- Term or late-preterm gestation.
- Maternal diabetes / infant of diabetic mother context.
- Delayed clearance of fetal lung fluid.
- Typical X-ray: perihilar streaking / fluid in fissures.
- Usually self-limited and managed supportively; respiratory support is guided by severity.

#### Surfactant pathway

**Moderate/severe RDS → CPAP → persistent oxygen requirement despite adequate CPAP → surfactant (MIST/LISA when appropriate) → intubation/ventilation if non-invasive support fails.**


**Any 2 of the following must be present:**

-   Respiratory rate `>60/min`
-   Grunting
-   Cyanosis

### Grunting

-   Expiration against a partially closed glottis by the baby.
-   Produces positive end-expiratory pressure.
-   Prevents alveolar collapse.

## Causes of respiratory distress

| Pulmonary (more common) | Non-pulmonary |
|---|---|
| 1. TTN / transient tachypnea of newborn | 1. Cardiac failure due to certain congenital heart diseases |
| 2. Respiratory distress syndrome (RDS) | 2. Metabolic causes: hypocalcemia, hypoglycemia |
| 3. Meconium aspiration syndrome (MAS) | |
| 4. Air leaks / pneumothorax | |
| 5. Pneumonia | |
| 6. Anomalies such as congenital diaphragmatic hernia | |

## Monitoring of respiratory distress

Two important scoring systems:

1.  **Silverman-Anderson score**
2.  **Downe's score**

### Silverman-Anderson score

Useful for monitoring **preterm babies with respiratory distress**.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 44

## Silverman-Anderson score

| Symptom | 0 | 1 | 2 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Upper chest retractions | Synchronized with abdomen | Chest lags during inspiration | Seesaw respiration |
| Lower chest retractions | None | Mild | Marked |
| Nare dilation | None | Mild | Marked |
| Xiphoid retraction | None | Mild | Marked |
| Expiratory grunting | None | Heard with stethoscope | Naked ear |

### Interpretation

-   Minimum score = **0** → normal.
-   Maximum score = **10** → severe respiratory distress / impending
    respiratory failure.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 45

## Downe's / Vidyasagar score

| Symptom | 0 | 1 | 2 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Respiratory rate | <60/min | 60--80/min | >80/min or apnea |
| Cyanosis | None in room air | With O₂ support at FiO₂ <40% | With FiO₂ >40% |
| Air entry | Normal | Decreased | Barely audible |
| Retractions | None | Mild | Marked |
| Grunting | None | Heard with stethoscope | Heard with naked ear |

### Interpretation

-   Score approximately **7--10** → severe distress and impending
    respiratory failure.

## Monitoring of oxygen saturation

-   `SpO₂ <90%` → hypoxia.

### Oxygen challenge

-   Give oxygen and observe change in SpO₂.

# Transient Tachypnea of Newborn (TTN)

-   Common after **lower-segment caesarean section (LSCS)** in
    term/late-preterm babies (35--38 weeks).

### Mechanism

-   Delayed clearance of lung fluid.

In normal vaginal delivery: - Baby is squeezed/compressed during passage
through the birth canal. - Fluid is squeezed out of lungs. - Baby can
breathe freely.

In LSCS: - No squeezing occurs. - Remnant fluid remains in lungs. - This
can lead to respiratory distress.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 46

### During delivery

-   Increased expression of **ENaC (Na⁺/K⁺ ATPase channels)**.
-   Helps in sodium and water reabsorption and clearing lung fluid.
-   In LSCS, expression of these channels is impaired.

### Presentation

-   Breathing difficulty within **2 hours after birth**.

### Chest X-ray

-   Fluid in the lungs.
-   Perihilar streaks / sunburst appearance.
-   Fluid in bronchopulmonary structures.
-   Fluid in the interlobar fissure.

### Management

-   Transient condition lasting up to **72 hours**.
-   Supportive treatment with oxygen inhalation.
-   Salbutamol inhalation in early TTN is noted to increase expression
    of relevant channels.

# Respiratory Distress Syndrome

-   Occurs in **preterm babies, especially \<35 weeks**.

### Pathology

-   **Hyaline membrane disease (HMD)**
-   Reason: deficient/immature surfactant.

### Surfactant

-   Keeps alveoli open by decreasing surface tension.
-   Prevents alveolar collapse.

In RDS: - Immature/deficient surfactant → collapsed alveoli.

### Presentation

-   Usually within **6 hours**.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 47

## Chest X-ray

-   White-out lungs / ground-glass appearance.
-   **Air bronchogram sign**
    -   Air enters patent bronchi and appears black against the opaque
        lung.

### Surfactant deficiency-related disease

The notes also depict pulmonary alveolar proteinosis: - Normally, old
surfactant is removed by alveolar macrophages. - In PAP, surfactant is
not degraded and accumulates. - There is no functional surfactant.

## Tests for fetal lung maturity

Can be done in amniotic fluid/gastric juice after birth.

### 1. Lecithin : sphingomyelin ratio (L:S)

-   Normally `>2:1`
-   Phospholipids that compose surfactant.
-   Lecithin is the mature form.
-   Sphingomyelin is the immature form.
-   More lecithin → more mature surfactant.
-   The source additionally notes a threshold of `>3:1` in another
    context.

### 2. Phosphatidylglycerol test

-   Less sensitive according to the source.

### 3. Lamellar body count

-   Storage form of surfactant in type II pneumocytes.
-   Some are detected in amniotic fluid.
-   Mature lung: approximately **50,000/µL**.

### 4. Shake test

-   Gastric aspirate is mixed with alcohol.
-   Shake vigorously for **10--15 min**.
-   Leave for another **10--15 min**.
-   Full rim of bubbles indicates a positive test for lung maturation.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 48

# Management of Respiratory Distress Syndrome

-   Preterm baby `<34–35 weeks` with breathing difficulty → suspect RDS.

## Start CPAP

**CPAP --- Continuous Positive Airway Pressure**

-   Keeps alveoli open.
-   Prevents collapse.

### Very premature baby

-   At approximately `≤28 weeks` gestation:
    -   Prophylactic surfactant to prevent RDS.
-   This strategy is termed **early delivery-room CPAP with selective
    surfactant**.

### Oxygen strategy

-   Start warm humidified O₂.
-   Target SpO₂: **90--95%**
-   Excess oxygen → retinopathy of prematurity → blindness.

### RDS severity

-   Mild → warm humidified oxygen.
-   Moderate → CPAP.
-   Initial pressure: **5 cm H₂O**
-   Pressure and oxygen adjusted according to response.

### Severe RDS

-   **Intubation → surfactant → extubation**
-   Standard guideline strategy.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 49

## Newer techniques for surfactant therapy

### Non-invasive / less invasive

-   **MIST:** Minimally Invasive Surfactant Therapy
-   **LISA:** Less Invasive Surfactant Administration
-   Done using a feeding tube/tracheal catheter rather than conventional
    intubation.

## Early rescue surfactant therapy

-   Moderate RDS
-   CPAP
-   No response at high FiO₂
-   Give surfactant therapy to decrease the need for mechanical
    ventilation.

### Indications for surfactant therapy

-   Moderate RDS with no response to CPAP.

## Antenatal prevention of RDS

### Corticosteroids

Given to mothers likely to deliver at **24--34 weeks**.

  Corticosteroid   Dose
  ---------------- ------------------------------------
  Betamethasone    12 mg/dose, 2 doses 24 hours apart
  Dexamethasone    6 mg/dose, 4 doses 6 hours apart

-   Dexamethasone is commonly used in India .

### Benefits

Decreases incidence of: - Respiratory distress syndrome -
Intraventricular hemorrhage in preterm babies - Overall neonatal
mortality

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 50

# Bronchopulmonary Dysplasia (BPD) / Chronic Lung Disease of Newborn

### Risk factors

-   Preterm babies born at `<32 weeks` of gestation.
-   Babies on oxygen support for `≥4 weeks` after birth.
-   RDS.

### When to assess?

-   **36 weeks of post-menstrual age**

### BPD classification

  BPD            O₂ requirement
  -------------- -------------------------------------------------------
  Mild BPD       No O₂ required
  Moderate BPD   FiO₂ `<30%`
  Severe BPD     FiO₂ `>30%` and/or any form of ventilatory assistance

# Congenital Diaphragmatic Hernia (CDH)

-   Intestines herniate from abdominal cavity into thoracic cavity.

### Types

|Type|Location of defect|
|---|---|
|Bochdalek hernia --- most common|Posterolateral, usually left side of diaphragm|
|Morgagni hernia --- rare|Anterior aspect, usually right side of diaphragm|


### Features of CDH

-   Compression of lungs → lung hypoplasia → respiratory distress after
    birth.
-   Heart sounds on the right side due to rightward shift of thoracic
    contents.
-   Sunken/scaphoid abdomen.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 51

# Diagnosis of CDH

### Antenatal

-   USG between **18--24 weeks**.

### After birth

-   Chest X-ray.

Findings: - Air shadows in thoracic cavity indicating intestinal
shadows. - No definite diaphragm border on the affected side. - Heart
shadow on the right side. - Absent air shadows in the abdomen.

## Management

|Medical management|Surgical management|
|---|---|
|First 48 hours after birth|Repair of diaphragmatic defect|
|Promote lung expansion|Gore-Tex/appropriate patch repair if needed|
|Stabilize pulmonary hypertension due to lung compression|Subcostal approach helps better visualization|
|Conventional ventilation||
|HFOV --- High-Frequency Oscillatory Ventilation||
|ECMO --- Extracorporeal Membrane Oxygenation||


------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 52

## Poor prognostic factors of CDH

|Antenatal factors|Postnatal factors|
|---|---|
|Lung-head ratio `<1`|Degree of pulmonary hypertension --- most important prognostic factor|
|Liver in thoracic cavity|Size of defect|
|Size of defect|Early onset of respiratory distress `<24 hours`|
|Derived total lung volume (TLV) `<20 mL`|Requires ECMO|


# Meconium Aspiration Syndrome

-   Common in post-term/term babies with **intrauterine stress**.

### Pathogenesis

Intrauterine stress → increased motilin → increased peristalsis of GIT →
expulsion of meconium into amniotic cavity → swallowed and aspirated →
**MAS**

### Effects of meconium

-   Chemical pneumonitis.

### Partial airway obstruction

-   Obstructive emphysema.
-   Air trapping during exhalation → hyperinflation on chest X-ray.

### Complete obstruction of a small airway

-   Segmental atelectasis.

### Meconium in alveoli

-   Chemical pneumonitis.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 53

## Diagnostic criteria for MAS

**Diagnosis of exclusion**

-   Meconium-stained liquor (MSL) at birth + respiratory distress.
-   X-ray:
    -   Coarse, ill-defined infiltrates.
-   Exclude other known conditions.

## Complications of MAS

-   Air leak like pneumothorax.
-   Persistent pulmonary hypertension of newborn (**PPHN**).

## Management

-   Supportive management according to ventilatory requirements:
    -   Oxygen therapy
    -   CPAP
    -   Other ventilatory support as required
-   PPHN:
    -   **Inhaled nitric oxide (iNO)**

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## 9. Neonatal Hypoglycemia

### Page 54

## Neonatal hypoglycemia

The notes define hypoglycemia as **blood glucose `<45 mg/dL`**.

### Screening for neonatal hypoglycemia

## At-risk babies

### Low substrate / low glycogen

-   Preterm babies `<35 weeks` of gestation.
-   Low birth weight `<2 kg`.
-   Intrauterine growth restriction (IUGR).

### Relative hyperinsulinemia

-   Large-for-gestational-age babies.
-   Infant of diabetic mother.
-   Rh incompatibility --- transient increase in insulin.

### Sick baby

-   Neonatal sepsis
-   Birth asphyxia
-   Hypothermia

### Screening

-   Hypoglycemia is screened by **dextrose measurement / heel-prick
    blood sugar**.

### Schedule of screening for at-risk babies

At: - 2 hours - 4 hours - 6 hours - 12 hours - 24 hours - 48 hours - 72
hours

### Features of neonatal hypoglycemia

-   Earliest:
    -   **Jitteriness/tremors**
-   Other features:
    -   Lethargy
    -   Weak cry
    -   Poor feeding pattern
-   Can present with seizures.
-   Autonomic changes include:
    -   Sudden changes in heart rate
    -   Sudden pallor
    -   Episodes of hypothermia
-   Autonomic response may be greater/different in newborns compared
    with older children or adults.

### Jitteriness vs seizures

  Feature                                 Jitteriness   Seizures
  --------------------------------------- ------------- ----------
  Eye deviation                           Not seen      Seen
  Autonomic changes                       Not seen      Seen
  Sudden heart-rate/respiratory changes   Not seen      Seen

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 55

## Management of neonatal hypoglycemia

### Symptomatic hypoglycemia

-   Emergency:
    -   **10% dextrose 2 mL/kg IV bolus**
    -   Followed by IV 10% dextrose infusion at approximately **6--8
        mg/kg/min**.

### Asymptomatic hypoglycemia

-   Oral feed.
-   Feed once → recheck after **30--45 minutes**.
-   If normal:
    -   Continue breastfeeding every 2 hours or according to baby's
        requirements.
-   If `<45 mg/dL`:
    -   Start IV 10% dextrose infusion.

# Infant of diabetic mother

## Pedersen's hypothesis

Maternal hyperglycemia → fetal hyperglycemia → increased secretion of
insulin in fetus → increased growth/anabolism → macrosomia
(large-for-gestational-age baby).

## Complications of infant of diabetic mother

-   Delivery complications:
    -   Shoulder dystocia
    -   Brachial plexus injury, e.g. **Erb's palsy**
    -   Fracture of clavicle
-   Asymmetrical interventricular septal hypertrophy:
    -   Form of hypertrophic cardiomyopathy.
    -   Transient condition.
-   Increased demand for oxygen → increased RBC production →
    polycythemia → predisposition to thromboembolic manifestations due
    to hypercoagulable blood.
-   Increased insulin → interferes with surfactant maturation →
    respiratory distress syndrome.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 56

### Further complications of infant of diabetic mother

-   Increased insulin → impaired gut motility, especially colon → **lazy
    left colon syndrome** → delayed passage of meconium.
-   Increased risk of congenital anomalies.

### Congenital anomalies

-   Cardiovascular system:
    -   VSD is shown in the notes.
-   Neural tube defects.
-   The notes identify **transposition of great arteries** as a
    particularly specific cardiac defect.
-   Overall most specific anomaly mentioned: **caudal regression
    syndrome**.
    -   Example: **sacral agenesis**

### Single most common anomaly in an infant born to diabetic mother

-   **VSD** is stated in the notes.

### After birth of baby

-   Hypoglycemia
-   Hypocalcemia
-   Hypomagnesemia
-   Seizures

**Most common metabolic abnormality associated with seizure in a baby
born to a diabetic mother:**\
- Hypoglycemia

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## 10. Neonatal Jaundice

### Page 57

# NEONATAL JAUNDICE

### Definition

**Jaundice** = increased levels of bilirubin manifesting as yellowish
discoloration.

### Bilirubin metabolism

-   Heme oxygenase
-   Biliverdin reductase
-   Unconjugated bilirubin:
    -   Water insoluble
    -   Fat soluble
-   Taken to liver and conjugated.
-   Conjugated bilirubin is water soluble and excreted into bile/stool.
-   A very small portion is taken back to liver → **enterohepatic
    circulation**.

### Serum bilirubin

Visible jaundice becomes apparent around **5 mg/dL** in the notes.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 58

# Screening tests for jaundice

## 1. Transcutaneous bilirubinometer (TcB)

-   Hand-held device.
-   Sternum is a common site.
-   Useful only if baby is **≥35 weeks of gestation**.
-   Reliable only if baby is **≥24 hours old**.

## 2. Visual assessment by Kramer

### CURRENT SEARCHABLE KRAMER TABLE — approximate visual estimate only

Kramer zones are **approximate clinical estimates, not phototherapy/exchange thresholds**. The source values are:

| Zone | Distribution | Approximate bilirubin in source |
|---|---|---:|
| 1 | Head / eyes | ~6 mg/dL |
| 2 | Trunk | ~9 mg/dL |
| 3 | Lower abdomen / thighs | ~12 mg/dL |
| 4 | Upper and lower limbs | ~15 mg/dL |
| 5 | Palms / soles | >15 mg/dL |

Visual assessment should not replace serum bilirubin measurement when jaundice is clinically significant or persistent.

### CURRENT SEARCHABLE PROLONGED-JAUNDICE APPROACH

1. **Ask: is there cholestasis?** If yes, obtain fractionated/direct bilirubin and begin an etiologic work-up urgently.
2. If there is no obvious cholestasis, obtain **serum bilirubin** when jaundice is clinically significant or persistent; visual assessment alone is insufficient.
3. Source red flags include jaundice by **day 2**, bilirubin rise >5 mg/dL/day or >0.2 mg/dL/h, and persistence beyond **2 weeks in term** or **3 weeks in preterm** infants.
4. Interpret bilirubin using age-, gestation- and risk-specific treatment guidance; assess clinically for acute bilirubin encephalopathy.
5. Choose **phototherapy versus exchange transfusion** using the current age/gestation/risk-specific threshold rather than a fixed bilirubin number.
6. Review feeding, hemolysis, hypothyroidism, cholestasis, extravasated blood and inherited disorders such as Crigler–Najjar syndrome.

#### Exchange transfusion — searchable principle

- For Rh incompatibility, use appropriately cross-matched **Rh-compatible blood** according to the antibody profile and local blood-bank protocol.
- For ABO incompatibility, use compatible group O red cells / appropriate plasma strategy according to local transfusion protocol.
- Product selection should ultimately be determined by the blood bank and the exact maternal antibody/infant blood-group situation.


States that jaundice spreads in a **cephalocaudal progression**: -
Head - Trunk - Limbs

### Conjugated/unconjugated bilirubin

**Unconjugated bilirubin** - Water insoluble. - Fat soluble. - Can cross
blood-brain barrier. - Can produce brain damage.

### Zones of jaundice

> **Source figure:** The original notes contain a visual Kramer-zone diagram.
> The individual zone cut-offs are not sufficiently legible in the source scan to reproduce safely.


------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 59

# Forms of jaundice

### Physiological jaundice

-   Increase in unconjugated bilirubin.
-   Due to immature UDP-glucuronosyltransferase activity.
-   Appears by **24--72 hours** of age.
-   Peaks around **day 3--5**.
-   May persist up to **1--2 weeks**.
-   No treatment required.

### Pathological jaundice

Any jaundice that is: - Present within the first **24 hours** of life. -
Rapidly increasing. - Associated with high bilirubin levels. -
Associated with signs of bilirubin encephalopathy. - Persistent
beyond: - **2 weeks in term babies** - **3 weeks in preterm babies**

### Other clinical points

-   Jaundice in arms and legs → significant jaundice.
-   Jaundice reaching palms and soles → concerning.
-   Nomograms:
    -   Values above the **95th percentile** for age are concerning.
-   Any hemolytic condition or other significant/pathological cause may
    require:
    -   Phototherapy
    -   Exchange transfusion

### Serum bilirubin.

Compare serum bilirubin with the age of the baby in hours.

-   Percentile lines depend on:
    -   Gestational age
    -   Risk factors such as:
        -   Asphyxia
        -   Sepsis
        -   Hypothermia

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 60

# Etiology of pathological jaundice

| Cause                                   | Mechanism/examples                         |
| --------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------ |
| **Hemolysis**                           | Inherited or acquired lysis of baby's RBCs |
| ABO/Rh incompatibility                  | Hemolysis                                  |
| **UDP-GT defects**                      | Crigler-Najjar syndrome                    |
| Type I Crigler-Najjar                   | Absent UDP-GT                              |
| Type II Crigler-Najjar                  | Low levels of UDP-GT                       |
| Hypothyroidism                          | Decreased bilirubin conjugation            |
| **Increased enterohepatic circulation** | Breastfeeding/feeding-related factors      |
| **Extravasated blood**                  | Bruising, cephalohematoma                  |
| **Increased conjugated bilirubin**      | Neonatal cholestasis                       |


### Bilirubin Encephalopathy / BIND

**Bilirubin-Induced Neurological Dysfunction**

-   Brain damage due to increased levels of unconjugated bilirubin.
-   Unconjugated bilirubin is lipid soluble and can cross the
    blood-brain barrier.
-   Area affected:
    -   **Basal ganglia**
-   Pathologically called **kernicterus**.
-   May be:
    -   Acute
    -   Chronic

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 61

# Clinical manifestations of bilirubin encephalopathy

## Early phase: 0--3 days

-   Hypotonia
-   Decreased activity
-   High-pitched/shrill cry

## Later phase: after 3 days

-   Hypertonia
-   Seizures
-   Retrocollis
-   Opisthotonus

### Chronic bilirubin encephalopathy

Basal-ganglia damage → extrapyramidal cerebral palsy: - Chorea -
Athetosis - Abnormal tone/posture

Also: - Sensorineural hearing loss - Dental problems - Enamel dysplasia

### Management of neonatal jaundice

#### Step 1: Monitor

-   Visual assessment.
-   Check bilirubin every 8 hours for approximately 3--5 days after
    birth.
-   Transcutaneous bilirubinometer.

### Concerning findings

-   Jaundice in palms and soles.
-   Bilirubin above the **95th percentile** for age.

#### Step 2

-   Look for signs of bilirubin encephalopathy.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 62

### Step 2

-   Send **serum bilirubin** for further management if indicated.

### Step 3: Does baby have significant jaundice?

Concerning features include: - Jaundice in arms/legs. - Rate of rise in
bilirubin: - `>5 mg/dL/day` - or `>0.2 mg/dL/hour` - Jaundice persists
for: - `>2 weeks` in term babies - `>3 weeks` in preterm babies

### Significant jaundice

**Present → investigations**

-   Serum bilirubin in nomogram.
-   Value above cut-off → start phototherapy.
-   Value below cut-off → continue observation.

**Absent** - Continue observation.

# Treatment of neonatal jaundice

## Phototherapy

-   UV/blue light wavelength: **460--490 nm**
-   Compact Fluorescent Lamps (CFL)
-   Light-emitting diode (LED)
-   Usage: CFL \> LED .
-   Efficacy: LED \> CFL.
-   Distance between baby and light: **30--45 cm**
-   Baby should be naked except for:
    -   Eyes
    -   Gonads
-   To prevent retinal and testicular damage.

### Effective irradiance

-   At least **30 µW/cm²/nm**.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 63

## Complications of phototherapy

-   Damage to:
    -   Retina
    -   Testis
    -   Avoided by covering them.
-   Dehydration due to heat.
    -   Avoided by increasing duration/frequency of breastfeeding.
-   Hypocalcemia.
-   **Bronze baby syndrome**
    -   Seen in conjugated hyperbilirubinemia/hepatic dysfunction.
    -   Brown pigmentation all over baby's skin and body fluids.
    -   Avoidable complication .

# Exchange transfusion (ET)

### Indications

-   Signs of bilirubin encephalopathy.
-   Rh incompatibility:
    -   Cord blood bilirubin `>5 mg/dL`
    -   or hemoglobin `<10 g/dL`
    -   suggesting significant hemolysis.
-   Nomogram cut-off values in ET range → start ET.

### Principle

-   Exchange of baby's blood with approximately **twice its volume** of
    fresh blood.
-   Also called **double-volume exchange transfusion**.

### Blood volume

-   Normal volume: **80--90 mL/kg**
-   Exchange volume: approximately twice this volume.

### Type of blood

-   Cross-matched with:
    -   Baby's blood
    -   Mother's blood
-   `O negative` blood commonly used as it does not contain A/B
    antigens.
-   The notes depict use of group O red cells suspended in AB plasma.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 64

# Prolonged Jaundice in Newborn

### Persistent jaundice

-   `>2 weeks` in term babies
-   `>3 weeks` in preterm babies

## Risk factors
