| Common | Uncommon |
|---|---|
| Breastfeeding issues / decreased duration or frequency of feeds | Inherited disorders |
| Breast milk jaundice | Crigler–Najjar syndrome |
| Breast milk contains substances such as pregnanediol that interfere with conjugation | |

# Management of prolonged jaundice

### Step 1

**Does baby have features of cholestasis?**

-   Yes → evaluate for cholestasis.
-   No → proceed with bilirubin assessment.

### Step 2

Visual assessment / total serum bilirubin (TSB):

-   Below phototherapy range
-   Above phototherapy range

------------------------------------------------------------------------

### Page 65

### Management pathway

-   Continue observation.
-   Start phototherapy if above treatment range.

### Most common causes / evaluation

-   Breast milk jaundice.
-   Rh/ABO incompatibility.
-   Thyroid function test.

# Crigler-Najjar syndrome

### Type I

-   Severe.
-   May require **liver transplantation**.

### Type II

-   **Phenobarbitone**
-   Induces **UDP-glucuronosyltransferase (UDP-GT)**.

---

# Growth

### Page 66

## 11. Normal Growth

### Page 66

# NORMAL GROWTH

## Growth phases

### Periods of growth

1. **Prenatal period:** Growth before birth/intrauterine period.
   - Fertilized ovum → zygote.
   - Embryo: first 8 weeks of gestation.
   - Fetus: 8–40 weeks of gestation.
2. **Postnatal period**
   - Newborn: first 4 weeks.
   - Early newborn: first week.
   - Late newborn: next 3 weeks.
   - Infancy: first year.
   - Toddler: 1–3 years.
   - Preschool: 3–6 years.
   - School age: 6–10 years.
   - Adolescent age group:
     - Early: 10–12 years.
     - Mid: 12–14 years.
     - Late: 14–19 years.

### Pubertal changes

- Puberty occurs earlier in **females (8–13 years)** than in **males (9–14 years)**.

---

### Page 67

## Sequence of puberty

### Females

1. **Thelarche** — enlargement of breast.
2. **Pubarche** — pubic hair.
3. **Growth spurt**
4. **Menarche**

### Males

1. Increase in testicular size/volume.
2. Pubarche.
3. Growth spurt.
4. Appearance of axillary and facial hair.

### Changes during the middle part of puberty in males

- **Spermarche** — production of sperms.
- Voice cracking/deepening.

### Assessment of puberty

**Tanner's stage (Sexual Maturity Rating)**

- Done separately in males and females.
- In males:
  - Testis
  - Penis
  - Pubic hair
  - Scrotum
- In females:
  - Breast
  - Pubic hair
- From stage I (prepubertal) to stage V (adult-like appearance).

---

### Page 68

## Tanner's staging in females

### Breast appearance

- **Stage I:** No changes in the breast.
- **Stage II:** Breast bud develops; enlargement of breast beyond nipple and areola.
- **Stage III:** Further enlargement of breast and areola.
- **Stage IV:** Nipple + areola form a secondary mound over the breast.
- **Stage V:** Mature/adult-type appearance; nipple protrudes forward and areola retracts.

### Pubic hair appearance

- **Stage I:** No pubic hair.
- **Stage II:** Straight, sparse hair.
- **Stage III:** Increase in number and curling of hairs.
- **Stage IV:** Dense pubic hair; curling. Pigmentation starts increasing from stage II–IV.
- **Stage V:** Pubic hair extends into the medial part of the thigh region.

## Tanner's staging in males

- Pubic-hair changes are similar to females.
- Testicular volume is represented as a number.
- Size of the testis is mentioned.
- **Stage I:** Testis size `<2.5 cm` and volume `<4 mL`.
- **Stage II:** Testis size `≥2.5 cm` and volume `≥4 mL`; scrotum is red in colour.
- **Stage III:** Further increase in volume of testis, penis lengthens, and scrotal skin becomes dark.
- **Stage IV:** Penis increases in length and breadth.
- **Stage V:** Testicular volume `>20 mL`; adult-like appearance.

---

### Page 69

## Growth patterns

### SEARCHABLE VISUAL RECONSTRUCTIONS — growth patterns, Tanner staging and chart interpretation

#### Four classic growth patterns

| Pattern | Main tissue/system | Exam pattern |
|---|---|---|
| **Neural** | Brain/head | Rapid early growth, approaching adult size early |
| **Lymphoid** | Thymus/lymphoid tissue | Rises above adult size during childhood, then involutes |
| **Somatic** | Height/weight/body mass | Broad S-shaped pattern from infancy through puberty |
| **Gonadal** | Gonads/reproductive organs | Minimal childhood growth, marked pubertal rise |

**Memory sketch:** neural = steep early rise/early plateau; lymphoid = ~2× adult peak then involution; somatic = broad S; gonadal = flat childhood then steep puberty.

#### Tanner staging — compact searchable reference

| Stage | Girls | Boys |
|---|---|---|
| I | Prepubertal breast/pubic hair | Prepubertal genitalia/pubic hair |
| II | Breast bud/areolar enlargement; sparse lightly pigmented pubic hair | Testes/scrotum enlarge; sparse pubic hair |
| III | Further breast enlargement; darker/coarser/curlier pubic hair | Penis lengthens; testes enlarge; pubic hair darker/coarser |
| IV | Secondary mound of areola; adult-type hair not yet adult distribution | Penis/glans enlarge; scrotum becomes darker; hair adult type but not adult distribution |
| V | Mature adult breast; adult pubic hair | Adult genitalia and adult pubic hair |

#### Growth-chart interpretation

- 50th percentile ≈ median.
- 97th percentile ≈ +2 SD.
- 3rd percentile ≈ −2 SD.
- A value below −2 SD or a child crossing major percentile/Z-score channels downward should prompt assessment of **growth velocity, nutrition, chronic disease and endocrine causes**, rather than diagnosis from one measurement alone.

#### Normal-growth numbers retained from the source

- Birth weight approximately doubles by **5 months** and triples by **1 year**.
- Approximate weight 1–6 years: **2 × age + 8 kg**.
- Approximate weight 7–12 years: **7 × age − 5 kg**.


### Rules

1. Growth is in **cephalocaudal progression**.
2. Growth occurs from the **distal to proximal direction**.
3. Not all tissues in the body grow at the same rate.

### Graphs of growth

- **Graph 1:** Till 4 years there is steep increase followed by minimal growth.
- **Graph 2:** Depicts **somatic growth**; sigmoid growth.
  - Phase 1: Rapid growth.
  - Phase 2: Growth spurt.
  - Phase 3: Pubertal growth spurt.
- Pubertal growth spurt occurs early in females.
- Increase in height is around **10 cm/year in females**.
- Increase in height is around **10 cm/year in males**.
- Growth spurts:
  - Females: Tanner stage 2.
  - Males: Tanner stage 4.
- **Graph 3:** Peak around 4–8 years is approximately 4 times adult size. Some tissues come back to normal size after 8 years.
  - Examples: lymphoid tissues such as lymph nodes, tonsils and adenoids.

---

### Page 70

- Between 4–8 years: **physiological lymphoid hyperplasia**.
- **Graph 4:** Increase in size after 10–14 years; stage of gonadal growth.

## Assessment of growth

### Anthropometry

Measurement of growth parameters.

### Weight

- Average birth weight in India: **2.9 kg**.
- During the first week, there is approximately **10% weight loss in term babies**.
- For preterm babies: up to **15% weight loss**.
- This is called **physiological weight loss**.
- Regaining birth weight:
  - Term: **10–14 days**
  - Preterm: longer.
- Best way of assessing growth: **growth charts**.

### Expected weight

- Birth weight doubles by **5–6 months**.
- Birth weight triples by **1 year**.
- Birth weight quadruples by **2 years**.

### Length/height assessment

- Length is measured in the **supine posture** up to 2 years.
- Height is measured by **stadiometer** (or infantometer for length).

Approximate height:
- At birth: **50 cm**
- 1 year: **75 cm**
- 2 years: **85–90 cm**
- 4 years: **100 cm**
- 12 years: **145–150 cm**

Growth velocity:
- **4–12 years:** approximately 6 cm/year.
- **>12 years:** approximately 8–10 cm/year.

---

### Page 71

- Height reaches approximately **85–90 cm** around 2 years.
- There is a steady increase of approximately **6 cm/year till 12 years**.
- After 12 years, growth spurt occurs:
  - Females: approximately **8 cm/year**.
  - Males: approximately **10 cm/year**.
- Height doubles by **4 years**.
- Height triples by **12 years**.
- Half of adult height in a normal child (approximately 80–85 cm) is reached around **4 years (48–60 months)**.

## Body proportions

- **Upper segment (US): lower segment (LS)**
  - At birth: approximately **1.7:1**
  - At 3 years: approximately **1.3:1**
  - At 7–10 years: approximately **1:1**
  - After 10 years: approximately **0.9:1**

- Body proportions are useful in evaluation of short stature.
- In a tall child: **arm span** is also assessed.

### Arm span

- At birth: height > arm span; difference approximately **5 cm**.
- At 11 years: height ≈ arm span.
- If arms are longer than height, the difference is approximately 5 cm.

## Circumferences

### Head circumference (HC)

Also called **occipitofrontal circumference**.

- Indicates brain growth/development.
- At birth: approximately **34 cm** (33–35 cm).
- First 3 months after birth: increase approximately **2 cm/month**.
- At 3 months: approximately **40 cm**.
- Next 3 months: increase approximately **1 cm/month**; at 6 months: approximately **43 cm**.
- Next 6 months: increase approximately **0.5 cm/month**; at 12 months: approximately **46 cm**.
- Next 1 year: increase approximately **2 cm**; at 2 years: approximately **48 cm**.
- Around 12 years: approximately **52 cm**.

---

### Page 72

## Chest circumference

- Measured at the level of the nipples.
- Measured midway between inspiration and expiration.

Relationship with head circumference:
- Birth: **HC > CC** (approximately 3 cm difference).
- 1 year: **HC = CC**.
- `>1 year`: **CC > HC**.

## Mid-arm circumference

### Mid-upper arm circumference (MUAC)

- Measured in children **6 months–5 years**.
- Useful for community assessment of growth/nutritional status because it can be done easily without measuring weight.

### Shakir's tape

- Midpoint of the arm: midpoint between two bony prominences.
- **Green:** nutrition is normal.
- **Yellow:** malnutrition.
- **Red:** severe malnutrition (`<11.5 cm`).

---

### Page 73

## Skin-fold thickness measurement

- Thickness of subcutaneous fat is measured.
- Indicator of nutritional status in children.
- Done using **Harpenden's caliper**.
- `>10 mm` — normal.
- `<6 mm` — severe malnutrition.
- Skin-fold thickness charts are interpreted as percentile values.
  - `<5th percentile` → low value (malnutrition).

## Growth charts

- WHO growth charts are most preferred and commonly used.
- In India, between **0–5 years**, WHO charts are used.
- Only WHO charts are used up to 5 years; WHO or IAP charts can be used from **5–10 years**.

### Types of WHO growth charts

1. Weight/age charts.
2. Weight/length charts.
3. Height/age charts.
4. HC/age charts.
5. MUAC/age charts.
6. BMI/age charts.
7. BMI/age charts / BMI plotted against age for older children.

**Best tool for assessment in `<5 years`: Weight/Height chart.**

---

### Page 74

## Growth chart interpretation

- Growth charts use different colours for boys and girls.
- In a **height-for-age chart**, major percentiles are recorded.
- **High value (tall stature):** value above the 97th percentile.
- **Low value (short stature):** below the 3rd percentile.
- Between the 97th and 3rd percentile: normal values.

### Standard deviation / Z-scores

Deviation from the mean value.

- 97th percentile ≈ **+2 SD**
- 85th percentile ≈ **+1 SD**
- 15th percentile ≈ **−1 SD**
- 3rd percentile ≈ **−2 SD**

---

### Page 75

## 12. Abnormalities in Head Size and Head Shape

### Page 75

# ABNORMALITIES IN HEAD SIZE AND HEAD SHAPE

## Microcephaly

- **Microcephaly (small head):** based on head circumference-for-age chart.
- Head circumference `<−3 SD` is considered microcephaly in the notes.
- This is an exception to the general rule because using `<−3 SD` in the definition avoids including normal children.

### Primary/genetic microcephaly

- Seen due to any insult during brain development.
- Refers to anomalies affecting the brain, e.g.:
  - Neuronal migration disorders.
- Genetic syndromes such as **Down syndrome (trisomy 21)**.

### Familial microcephaly

- Small head size runs in the family.
- Usually inherited as **autosomal recessive**.

### Secondary microcephaly

- Normal brain development followed by acquired factors causing microcephaly.

### Acquired microcephaly

#### Prenatal factors

1. Maternal infections during intrauterine life:
   - TORCH infection.
2. Radiation affecting rapidly dividing organogenesis.
3. Toxins/drugs/chemicals taken during pregnancy.
4. Teratogens such as phenytoin or alcohol.

#### Perinatal factors

- Infection during birth.

#### Postnatal factors

- Trauma or infection in the child up to the age of 4 years can lead to microcephaly.

#### Metabolic disorders

Metabolite accumulation in the brain:
1. Phenylketonuria.
2. Lysosomal storage disorders.

---

### Page 76

## Lysosomal storage disorders causing microcephaly

### Gaucher disease vs Niemann-Pick disease

| Feature | Gaucher disease | Niemann-Pick disease |
|---|---|---|
| Enzyme deficiency | Glucocerebrosidase | Sphingomyelinase |
| Bone involvement | Can affect bones | Does not involve bones |
| Bone marrow | Gaucher cells; may cause Erlenmeyer flask deformity | — |
| Cells | Accumulation of glucocerebroside | Accumulation of sphingomyelin gives foamy appearance of cells |
| Bone marrow | Crumpled/paper-like appearance on biopsy | — |
| Eye findings | — | Cherry-red spots in eye |
| Other | Accumulation of glucocerebroside as typical cytoplasmic inclusions | — |

### Late-onset syndromes associated with microcephaly

- Rett syndrome.
- Seckel syndrome.
- Angelman syndrome.

## Rett syndrome

- X-linked dominant disorder.
- Genetic disorder characterized by defect in **MECP2** gene.
- From birth to 1 year of age, head circumference is normal; after 1 year, deceleration of brain growth results in microcephaly.
- **Developmental regression**
  - Up to 1 year, child achieves normal milestones.
  - After 1 year, child loses previously acquired milestones.
  - Child reaches a plateau with some support in later life, but not able to attain support at 15 years.
- **Stereotypies**
  - Characteristic hand wringing.
  - Purpose-less repetitive movements in the midline.
- Speech defects.

---

### Page 77

## Seckel syndrome

- Ataxia worsens over time.
- Adolescents may become wheelchair-dependent.
- **Bird-headed dwarfism**.
- **Beak-like nose**.

### Microcephaly with low IQ, seizures and hypopigmented hair

A child born out of consanguineous marriage with microcephaly, low IQ, seizures and hypopigmented hair:

- Familial microcephaly
- **Inborn error of metabolism**
- TORCH infections
- Neural tube defect

The notes identify an **inborn error of metabolism** as the likely cause.

## Phenylketonuria (PKU)

- Most common amino-acid metabolic disorder.
- Autosomal recessive inheritance.

### Pathophysiology

Normally:

**Phenylalanine → phenylalanine hydroxylase → tyrosine → melanin**

In phenylketonuria:

- Phenylalanine hydroxylase deficiency → deficient tyrosine → deficient melanin → hypopigmentation.
- Features:
  - Fair skin
  - Blonde hair
  - Blue eyes
- Increasing phenylalanine → converted to metabolites such as phenylacetate, phenylpyruvate and phenyl-lactate.
- These metabolites cause brain damage and microcephaly.

---

### Page 78

- Musty/mousy odour of urine is due to **phenylacetate excretion**.

### Management of PKU

- Restrict diet containing phenylalanine.
- Enhance conversion of phenylalanine using a cofactor via synthetic **BH4 (tetrahydrobiopterin)**, also called **sapropterin**.

## Fetal alcohol syndrome

Phenotypic appearance of infants with microcephaly may suggest fetal alcohol syndrome.

### Facial characteristics

- Small eye openings.
- Smooth philtrum.
- Thin upper lip.

### Other features

- Philtrum is normally present between nose and mouth.
- In fetal alcohol syndrome, increased risk of:
  - Cardiac defects, commonly **VSD**.
  - Cleft lip/palate.
  - Microcephaly.

---

### Page 79

## Fetal hydantoin syndrome

Due to **phenytoin (diphenylhydantoin) exposure**.

Features:
- Cleft lip/palate.
- Heart defects, commonly VSD.
- Small hands/feet.
- Mid-facial hypoplasia.
- Facial hypoplasia compared with other structures of the face.

## Macrocephaly

- Head circumference `>+2 SD` for age.

### Causes of macrocephaly

#### Increased amount of CSF

1. **Hydrocephalus**
2. **Hydranencephaly**
   - Absent brain tissue is replaced by sacs containing fluid (CSF).
   - Transillumination may be positive.

#### Increased bleeding within skull

- Subdural haemorrhage.
- Extradural haemorrhage.
- Intraventricular haemorrhage.
- Subarachnoid haemorrhage.

#### Increased size of bony compartment

Associated with primary bone disorders such as:
- Achondroplasia.
- Osteogenesis imperfecta.
- Abnormal skull expansion can also occur in disorders such as beta-thalassemia major due to increased erythropoiesis.

#### Increased intracranial volume

- Megalencephaly.

---

### Page 80

## Causes of megalencephaly

### SEARCHABLE CLINICAL-RECOGNITION MAP — megalencephaly and craniosynostosis

#### Megalencephaly classification

| Branch | Examples / clues |
|---|---|
| Syndromic | **Sotos**, fragile X and other overgrowth syndromes |
| Non-syndromic | Familial megalencephaly |
| Metabolic | Storage/leukodystrophy disorders |
| Secondary / intracranial-volume causes | Hydrocephalus, hydranencephaly, intracranial bleeding or subdural collections |
| True megalencephaly | Increased brain tissue volume itself |

#### Craniosynostosis: suture → skull shape

- **Metopic → trigonocephaly**
- **Sagittal → scaphocephaly / dolichocephaly**
- **Coronal → brachycephaly**; unilateral coronal fusion produces anterior plagiocephaly
- **Lambdoid/posterior → posterior plagiocephaly**

#### High-yield phenotype recognition

- **Rett syndrome:** developmental regression, stereotypic hand movements and evolving microcephaly.
- **Seckel syndrome:** severe growth retardation with microcephaly and characteristic “bird-headed” facies.
- **Fetal alcohol syndrome:** smooth philtrum, thin upper lip, short palpebral fissures, microcephaly and neurodevelopmental abnormalities.
- **Apert syndrome:** craniosynostosis with characteristic syndactyly.
- **Crouzon syndrome:** craniosynostosis with characteristic craniofacial features but without the classic limb syndactyly of Apert syndrome.


### Syndromic

- **Sotos syndrome**
- **Fragile X syndrome**

### Non-syndromic

- Metabolic disorders.
- Familial megalencephaly.

### Sotos syndrome (cerebral gigantism)

- Increase in head size and height are noted.
- Low IQ.
- Macro-orchidism.
- Elongated face and large ears.

### Fragile X syndrome

- Low IQ.
- Macro-orchidism.
- Elongated face and large ears.
- Features may include **large ears** and **macro-orchidism**.

### Familial megalencephaly

- Normal IQ and normal development.
- All family members have large heads.

### Metabolic disorders

- Patients have low IQ and delayed development.

### Tay-Sachs disease

- Hexosaminidase A deficiency.
- No hepatosplenomegaly.

### Sandhoff disease

- Hexosaminidase A and B deficiency.
- Hepatosplenomegaly is seen.

- Both Tay-Sachs and Sandhoff disease may show **cherry-red spots in the macula**.

---

### Page 81

# Craniosynostosis

- **Synostosis:** fusion; cranio = skull.
- Premature fusion of sutures.
- Growing brain needs space → skull expands in an irregular manner.
- Whenever a suture fuses, the skull elongates along the direction of the fused suture.
- Most common suture to undergo premature fusion: **sagittal suture**.

### Types of skull deformity

- Metopic suture → **trigonocephaly**.
- Lateral coronal suture → **brachycephaly**.
- Posterior plagiocephaly.
- Sagittal suture → **dolichocephaly/scaphocephaly**.

---

### Page 82

| Premature fusion of | Craniosynostosis |
|---|---|
| Metopic suture | Trigonocephaly |
| Lateral coronal suture | Brachycephaly |
| Sagittal suture | Dolichocephaly/scaphocephaly |
| Posterior coronal/lambdoid region | Posterior plagiocephaly |

### Brachycephaly

- Characteristic type of skull noted in **Down syndrome**.
- Broad skull with increased biparietal diameter.

## Syndromic associations with craniosynostosis

### Crouzon syndrome

- Autosomal dominant condition.
- Proptosis due to underdeveloped and shallow orbital bones.
- Prominent forehead.
- Hyperterorism.
- Maxillary hypoplasia.
- Bilateral coronal suture fusion → brachycephaly.
- Normal IQ.

### Apert syndrome

- Autosomal dominant condition.
- Not so prominent proptosis.
- Asymmetric face.
- Syndactyly or mitten-hand deformity.
- Cleft lip/palate.
- Low IQ.

---

### Page 83

### Multiple sutures prematurely fuse

- **Turricephaly / oxycephaly / acrocephaly**
- Tower-shaped/conical skull.

### Apert syndrome

- Asymmetric face.
- Syndactyly/mitten-hand deformity.
- Cleft lip/palate.

---

### Page 84

## 13. Short Stature in Children

### Page 84

# SHORT STATURE IN CHILDREN

## Introduction

- Defined as height-for-age `<3rd percentile` or `<−2 SD`.

### Types

1. **Normal variant short stature**
   - Height-for-age approximately `>3rd percentile`.
2. **Pathological short stature**
   - Height-for-age below the expected range.

### Growth velocity

- Rate of increase in height.
- Normal in normal variant short stature but may be decreased in pathological short stature.
- If growth velocity is `<5th percentile`, it is a pathological variant; if `>5th percentile`, it is a normal variant in the notes.

## Normal variant short stature

### Types

1. **Constitutional delay**
2. **Familial short stature**

---

### Page 85

## Constitutional delay vs familial short stature

| Feature | Constitutional delay | Familial short stature |
|---|---|---|
| Expected adult height | Normal | Short |
| Parent's height | Normal + history of short stature in childhood | Short |
| Puberty | Delayed | At normal age |
| Bone age | Delayed | Normal |

- Constitutional delay is the overall **most common cause of short stature**.
- Also called **constitutional delay in growth and puberty**.
- Constitutional delay is a normal variant.

## Pathological short stature

### Causes

- **Chronic malnutrition**.
- **Bone disorders**
  - Achondroplasia
  - Osteogenesis imperfecta
  - Rickets
- **Genetic syndromes**
  - Trisomy 21 (Down syndrome)
  - Turner syndrome
- **Hormonal disturbances**
  - Deficiencies of:
    - Thyroid hormone
    - Growth hormone
  - Parathormone deficiency in hypoparathyroidism.
  - Delayed puberty due to deficiency of gonadal steroids such as oestrogen and testosterone.
  - Excess hormone, e.g. thyroid hormone, can cause precocious puberty.

---

### Page 86

### Other endocrine causes

- Glucocorticoids.
- Short stature may be a prominent manifestation.
- **Precocious puberty:** excess gonadal steroids → premature fusion of epiphysis → growth arrested at an earlier age than expected.

## Approach to short stature

### SEARCHABLE SHORT-STATURE VISUAL/DECISION RECONSTRUCTION

**First split:** proportionate vs disproportionate short stature → assess US:LS/arm span/body proportions → determine growth velocity → assess bone age → target systemic/endocrine/skeletal causes.

- **Proportionate:** systemic disease, chronic malnutrition, endocrine disease and constitutional/familial variants are considered.
- **Disproportionate:** think skeletal dysplasia, rickets, spondylo-/mucopolysaccharidosis and other body-proportion disorders.

**Recognition panel:**

| Condition | High-yield clue |
|---|---|
| Constitutional delay / familial short stature | Distinguish by family history, growth velocity and bone age; use trajectory over time |
| Achondroplasia | Disproportionate short stature, rhizomelic limb shortening, trident hand and characteristic pelvic/radiographic changes |
| MPS | Coarse facies, skeletal abnormalities / dysostosis multiplex; characteristic hand/chest/spine radiographic abnormalities |


### 1. Using body proportions

Relation to:
- **Upper segment (US)**
- **Lower segment (LS)**

Helps differentiate:
- Proportionate short stature.
- Disproportionate short stature.

### Proportionate short stature

- US:LS is normal for age.

Causes:
- All normal variants:
  - Constitutional.
  - Familial.
- Malnutrition.
- Chronic systemic illness:
  - CHD
  - CLD
  - Cardiac problems.
- Growth hormone deficiency.

### Disproportionate short stature

- US:LS is abnormal for age.

#### Type 1: Short trunk / short US + small trunk

Causes:
- Spondyloepiphyseal dysplasia.
- Mucopolysaccharidoses.
- TB of the spine.
- Vertebral anomalies:
  - Hemivertebrae.
  - Butterfly-shaped vertebrae.

#### Type 2: Short limb / short LS

Causes:
- Achondroplasia.
- Rickets.
- Congenital hypothyroidism.

### 2. Bone-age determination

- Using ossification centres in bone, seen on X-ray.

---

### Page 87

## Bone-age estimation

- At approximately **1 year:** shoulder.
- **10–18 years:** left wrist/carpal bones.
- **14–18 years:** elbow and hip.

### Methods for bone-age estimation

1. **Tanner-Whitehouse method**
2. **Greulich-Pyle atlas**
   - X-rays for different age groups of males and females are available.
   - X-ray of the patient is matched with the atlas and age is determined.

### Delay in bone age vs advanced bone age

| Delay in bone age | Advanced bone age |
|---|---|
| Constitutional delay | Precocious puberty |
| Malnutrition | Excess gonadal steroids |
| Hormone deficiency, as hormones are needed for ossification-centre appearance | |

### Clinical question

A child with pathological short stature, abnormal facies and short trunk should raise suspicion of **mucopolysaccharidosis**.

---

### Page 88

# Mucopolysaccharidoses

- Lysosomal storage disorders.
- Defect in lysosomal enzymes (hydrolases) causes accumulation of **glycosaminoglycans (GAGs)**:
  - Heparan sulphate
  - Chondroitin sulphate
  - Dermatan sulphate

### Characteristic abnormal facies

Called **gargoylian/gargoylic facies**:
- Broad forehead.
- Upturned nose with copious nasal discharge.
- Macroglossia.
- Cloudy cornea (whitish cornea).

Other features:
- Low IQ.
- Macrocephaly.
- Abdominal distension with increased risk of:
  - Umbilical hernia.
  - Inguinal hernia.
- Hepatosplenomegaly.

### Radiological

### SEARCHABLE RICKETS RADIOGRAPHIC + REFRACTORY-RICKETS RECONSTRUCTION

#### Classic radiographic changes of nutritional rickets

- Loss of the **zone of provisional calcification (ZPC)**.
- Widened, irregular metaphysis.
- **Cupping**.
- **Fraying**.
- **Splaying** of the metaphyseal ends / widened growth plate.
- Looser/pseudofracture lines may occur in severe bone disease.

#### Treatment structure

- Nutritional vitamin-D deficiency: **vitamin D repletion + adequate calcium**, followed by maintenance dosing.
- Active-vitamin-D or hypophosphatemic rickets: classify using calcium, phosphate, PTH, renal function/urinary phosphate handling and disease-specific features.
- Persistent/refractory rickets: recheck phosphate, calcium, ALP, PTH, 25-OH-vitamin D, renal function and acid-base status and look for renal phosphate loss or RTA.

#### Refractory-rickets decision tree

- **Low phosphate** → check acid-base status and PTH; consider renal phosphate wasting, RTA and hypophosphatemic rickets.
- **High phosphate / reduced excretion** → consider CKD-associated disease and other phosphate-retention states.
- **Acidosis** → evaluate for proximal/distal RTA.
- **High PTH with low calcium** → vitamin-D/calcium-axis disorders and other secondary hyperparathyroid states.

### SEARCHABLE SCURVY RADIOGRAPHIC RECOGNITION

- **Frankel line:** dense metaphyseal line from provisional calcification.
- **Trümmerfeld/scorbutic zone:** central metaphyseal rarefaction.
- **Pelkan spur:** metaphyseal beak/spur from cortical changes.
- **Wimberger ring sign:** dense ring around the epiphysis.
- **Ground-glass appearance:** generalized reduction in bone density.
- Clinical recognition: bleeding/swollen gums, perifollicular haemorrhage/corkscrew hairs and painful limbs/pseudoparalysis from subperiosteal bleeding.
 findings

- Bullet-shaped metacarpals with proximal narrowing.
- Spatula/oar/paddle-shaped anterior ribs.
- Flat, straight ribs.
- Narrowing/beaking of vertebrae.
- Short trunk due to narrowing of multiple vertebrae.

---

### Page 89

- Multiple bony abnormalities in MPS are collectively called **dysostosis multiplex**.

| Type | Name | Enzyme deficiency |
|---|---|---|
| MPS I | Hurler syndrome | L-iduronidase |
| MPS I | Scheie syndrome (formerly MPS V) | L-iduronidase |
| MPS II | Hunter syndrome | Iduronate sulfatase |
| MPS III | Sanfilippo syndrome | Heparan sulfatase |
| MPS IV | Morquio syndrome | N-acetylgalactosamine-6-sulfatase |
| MPS VI | Maroteaux-Lamy | Arylsulfatase A |
| MPS VII | Sly | β-glucuronidase |

- **Hurler syndrome:** complete deficiency of L-iduronidase.
- **Scheie disease:** partial deficiency of L-iduronidase.
- All MPS are inherited autosomal recessively except **Hunter syndrome**, which is X-linked recessive.
- Hunter syndrome: **no corneal clouding**.
- Morquio syndrome: highest chance of bony deformities.
- Low incidence of intellectual disability is seen in MPS IV and VI.
- Enzyme replacement therapy (ERT) can be given for MPS I, II, IV, VI.

# Achondroplasia

- Characterized by short limbs and short stature.
- Proximal limb shortening (arm > forearm) is called **rhizomelia**.
- Mostly inherited as **autosomal dominant**.
- **FGFR3 (Fibroblast Growth Factor Receptor 3)** gene mutation, chromosome **4p**.

---

### Page 90

## Other features of achondroplasia

- Macrocephaly (large head) because of small foramen magnum causing obstructed flow of CSF leading to hydrocephalus.
- Broad and bulging forehead.
- X-ray pelvis shows **champagne-glass appearance**.

---

# Development

### Page 91

## 14. Normal Development in Children

### Page 91

# NORMAL DEVELOPMENT IN CHILDREN

## Developmental milestones

### SEARCHABLE DEVELOPMENTAL MILESTONE MEMORY AID

| Approx. age | Gross motor / adaptive | Fine motor / social-language examples |
|---|---|---|
| 3 months | Head control; regards face | Hands open; hand regard |
| 5–6 months | Rolls; tripod sitting emerges | Transfers object; palmar grasp |
| 7–8 months | Sits independently; begins crawling | Immature pincer develops later |
| 9–10 months | Pulls to stand / cruises | Neat pincer emerging |
| 12 months | Stands/cruises; may walk with support | Mature pincer; points; social/gesture skills |
| 15–18 months | Walks well; runs; climbs | Scribble; tower; increasing words |
| 2 years | Runs; stairs with help | Two-word phrases; parallel play |
| 3 years | Pedals; jumps | Copies circle / simple drawings |
| 4–5 years | Hops/skips progressively | Draws person; more complex language |

#### Pincer grasp

- **Immature pincer:** thumb + side of index finger / crude pincer.
- **Mature pincer:** tip-to-tip thumb and index finger; typically established around the end of the first year.

#### Developmental examination

| Maneuver | What to look for |
|---|---|
| Pull-to-sit | Head lag diminishes with maturation; persistent marked lag is a red flag |
| Ventral suspension | Progressive extension/control of head and trunk with maturation |
| Prone positioning | Forearm weight-bearing → hands → rolling/crawling-related transitions |


Defined according to **4 areas/domains of development**:

- **Gross motor:** major motor activities.
- **Fine motor:** use of small muscles of hands.
- **Language:** communication with others.
- **Social:** other modes of interaction.

## Gross motor milestones

### Rule

Development in a child always proceeds in a **cephalocaudal direction** (head to foot).

- **3 months:** head control/neck holding (milestone).
- **4 months:** roll over (supine to prone).
- **6 months:** sit with support (tripod position: leaning forward, trunk in centre with two arms by the side).
- **8 months:** sit without support; crawling.

### Crawling

Body is flat on the ground and uses both upper limbs and lower limbs to move from one place to another.

---

### Page 92

- **10 months:** standing with support, creeping.
- **11 months:** cruising.
- **12 months:** standing without support, walking with support.
- **15 months:** walks without support/independently.
- **18 months:** running.
- **2 years:** climbing stairs with two feet/step while holding side rails.
- **3 years:** climbing stairs with one foot/step, rides tricycle.
- **4 years:** upstairs/downstairs one foot/step, hopping.

### Creeping

Uses all 4 limbs to move, but chest and abdomen are lifted above the ground.

### Cruising

Uses support to move sideways.

---

### Page 93

## Fine motor milestones

Small movements of hands such as grasping objects, drawing, dressing skills and making towers of cubes.

- **0 months:** no fine motor milestone; baby's hands are always closed due to neonatal palmar grasp reflex.
- **4 months:** reaching out for objects with both hands (bidextrous grasp).
- **6 months:** unidextrous grasping/ulnar palmar grasping (immature); child uses palm as support and transfers objects from one hand to another.
- **8 months:** radial palmar grasping — mature.
- **9 months:** immature pincer grasp; object held by sides of fingers.
- **12 months:** mature pincer grasp; object held by fingertips.
- **15 months:** scribble.
- **18 months:** draw a vertical line.
- **2 years:** draw a horizontal line.
- **4 years:** draw a cross/plus.
- **4½ years:** draw a square.
- **5 years:** draw a triangle.
- **6–7 years:** draw a diamond/rhomboid.

---

### Page 94

## Drawing milestones

- **3 years:** circle.
- **4 years:** cross/plus.
- **4½ years:** square.
- **5 years:** triangle.
- **6–7 years:** diamond/rhomboid.

**Mnemonic:** LOX STO.

## Dressing skills

- Undressing: **2 years** (easier task than dressing).
- Dressing: **3 years**.
- Both these tasks require help of a parent.
- Dresses and undresses without any help at **5 years**.

## Tower of cubes

Placing one cube on top of another.

- **15 months:** 2 cubes.
- **18 months:** 3 cubes.
- **24 months (2 years):** 6 cubes.
- **30 months (2½ years):** 8 cubes.
- **36 months (3 years):** 9 cubes.

## Language milestones

Includes making sounds, taking words/sentences, telling stories/rhymes, etc.

- Starts from **3 months**.
- **4 months:** musical sounds when child is happy → cooing sounds.
- **4 months:** laughs aloud when happy.
- **6 months:** monosyllables.
- **9 months:** bisyllables/repeating same syllable (mama, papa).
- **1 year:** 4 words with meaning.
- **15 months:** jargon speech (words without meaning; temporary milestone).
- **18 months:** 8–10 words with meaning.
- **2 years:** 100 words; starts to speak in sentences.
- **3 years:** recognizes and tells name, age and gender.
- **4 years:** story telling or singing rhymes.

---

### Page 95

## Social milestones

- **2 months:** social smile.
- **3 months:** mother regard — interacts with mother only.
- **6 months:** stranger anxiety (cries); smiles at mirror image.
- **9 months:** waves bye-bye.
- **12 months:** playing a simple ball game with others.
- **15 months:** points to object (asking for a ball far away).
- **18 months:** domestic mimicry — child imitates actions of adults.
- **2½–3 years:** non-interactive parallel play.
- **4 years:** interactive group play.
- **5 years:**
  - Follows 3-step commands.
  - Identifies 4 colours and repeats 4 digits.

## Miscellaneous milestones

- **Mouthing:** starts around 6 months; puts objects/hand in mouth.
- **Casting:** begins by 1 year; deliberate throwing of objects.
- Both milestones are seen till approximately 18 months–2 years.
- **Object permanence:** around 9 months.
  - Feeling that a missing object is present around the child although not seen in the field of vision.
  - Example: looking and searching for a ball that fell from a child's hand.
- **Handedness:** preference of one hand over the other.
  - Starts by 3 years.
  - Firmly established by 4 years.
- **Hand regard:** appears at 3 months and persists till 5 months.

---

### Page 96

- Abnormality: persistence of **hand regard for more than 5 months** → developmental delay.

## Range of vision

- Newborn: follows objects up to **45°**.
- 1 month old: follows objects up to **90°**.
- 3 months old: follows objects up to **180°**.
- Binocular vision is fully established by **4 months**.

## Localization of sound

Turning head towards the sound source.

### Assessment

Assessed using **Murphy's sequence**.

- **Newborn:** startle response to sound.
- **4 months:** horizontal localization of sound.
- **6 months:** downward localization of sound.
- **7 months:** upward localization of sound.
- **10 months:** diagonal localization of sound.

---

### Page 97

## Assessment in development

### Pull-to-sit maneuver

- **Newborn:** complete head lag (no head control).
- **3 months:** no head lag (head control attained).

### Ventral suspension

Lift the child using the palm of a hand and turn to prone position while the palm still supports the baby's trunk.

- **Newborn:** head and limbs drop down (no control).
- **2 months:** head at level with rest of body.
- **3 months:** head is lifted above the level of body.

### Prone position

#### Newborn

Same posture till approximately 4 weeks of life:
- Legs folded and kept beneath abdomen.
- Pelvis lifted above the rest of the body.

---

### Page 98

### Prone position

- **4–6 weeks:** legs straighten and pelvis is at level with rest of body; baby will lie flat.
- **3 months:**
  - Head lifted up.
  - Elbows flexed/bent.
  - Bears weight on forearms.
- **6 months:**
  - Head lifted up.
  - Elbows straight (in extension).
  - Bearing weight on extended limbs.

---

### Page 99

## 15. Disorders of Development

### SEARCHABLE DEVELOPMENTAL-QUOTIENT / BEHAVIOURAL CLARIFICATION

> **Use this clarification when the older text conflicts with it.** DQ alone is not a diagnosis; cut-offs depend on the developmental tool and context.

**DQ = (Developmental age / Chronological age) × 100**

- **Global developmental delay:** significant delay in **2 or more developmental domains** in a young child, before a definitive intellectual-disability diagnosis is possible.
- **Regression of acquired skills** is more concerning than an isolated late milestone and warrants evaluation.

#### Tic classification 2 × 2

| | Simple | Complex |
|---|---|---|
| **Motor** | Blinking, facial movements, shoulder shrug | Jumping, touching, complex sequences |
| **Vocal** | Throat clearing, sniffing | Words/phrases and complex utterances |

#### Enuresis — compact pathway

1. Confirm **monosymptomatic vs non-monosymptomatic** nocturnal enuresis.
2. Look for constipation, UTI symptoms, daytime LUTS, diabetes and neuro/spinal clues.
3. Use behavioural measures, bladder routine and constipation management.
4. **Alarm therapy** is a key durable behavioural treatment.
5. **Desmopressin** is useful particularly for nocturnal polyuria or situations needing rapid short-term dryness; relapse after withdrawal is common.


### Page 99

# DISORDERS OF DEVELOPMENT

## Assessment of development of the child

### Developmental quotient (DQ)

**DQ = (Developmental age / Chronological age) × 100**

Example: A 4-year-old child is able to draw only a circle. Therefore, the developmental age of the child is 3 years:

**Example: DQ = (3 / 4) × 100 = 75**

- **DQ <70:** abnormal / developmental delay.
- **DQ ≥70 in 2 or more domains:** global developmental delay.

### Evaluation of a preterm baby

- Example: born at **32 weeks** of gestation.
- Premature by **8 weeks**, because normal term is 40 weeks.
- Evaluated at 3 months of age (postnatal age).
- Corrected age = postnatal age − preterm correction.
- Correction for prematurity can be calculated till **2 years of age**.

## Red-flag signs of development

When the child crosses the upper limit for attaining a milestone, it is considered a **red-flag sign**.

| Milestone | Upper limit |
|---|---:|
| Visual fixation or following | 4 months |
| Head control | 5 months |
| Vocalization | 6 months |
| Sitting without support | 10 months |
| Standing with support | 12 months |
| Standing/walking without support | 18 months |
| Single words | 18 months |

---

### Page 100

## Tests for developmental assessment

### Screening tests

**Mnemonic: Good Doctor Treats Patients**

1. **Goodenough-Harris draw-a-man test**
2. **Denver II**
3. **Trivandrum Development Screening Test**
4. **Phatak's Baroda screening test**

### Definitive tests

1. **Bayley III scale** — infant and toddler development.
2. **Stanford-Binet intelligence scale**
3. **Wechsler Intelligence Scale**
4. **Vineland Adaptive Behaviour Scale II**

## Developmental delay in a child

Causes can be classified as:

| Cause | Examples |
|---|---|
| Chromosomal | Down syndrome |
| Genetic syndrome | Fragile X syndrome, Rett syndrome |
| Congenital hypothyroidism | |
| Brain anomalies | |
| TORCH infections | |
| Severe head trauma | |
| Meningitis | |
| Perinatal asphyxia | |

### Variants of developmental disorders

#### Developmental dissociation

- Variation in attainment of milestones between 2 or more domains.
- Example: isolated language delay due to hearing deficit.

#### Developmental deviance

- Child develops milestones out of the normal sequence.
- Example: early rolling over due to increased extensor tone in cerebral palsy.

---

### Page 101

## Regression

- The child loses previously acquired milestones.

Examples:
- Rett syndrome.
- Subacute sclerosing panencephalitis (**SSPE**).
- Leukodystrophy.

---

### Page 102

## 16. Behavioural Disorders in Children

### Page 102

# BEHAVIOURAL DISORDERS IN CHILDREN

## Breath-holding spells

- A reflex event.
- **Age:** starts after 6 months.
- Peaks by **2 years** and persists till 5 years.

### Sequence of events

Provocative factor:
- Anger
- Frustration
- Pain

↓

Cry

↓

Starts to hold breath

↓

Breath holding takes place in **full expiration**

### Types

1. **Most common: cyanotic type**
   - Due to increase in parasympathetic activity.
   - Cyanosis develops.
   - Child becomes normal after a few seconds.
   - May develop a tonic-clonic movement.

2. **Pallid type**
   - Increase in sympathetic activity.
   - Child becomes pale/white, mimicking a syncope-like attack.
   - Usually lasts for a few seconds.
   - ECG may be done to rule out long-QT syndrome.

### Management

- Reassurance and inform parents about benign nature and spontaneous resolution.
- During tonic-clonic episode:
  - Turn child to one side (prevents aspiration).

---

### Page 103

- Never pick up a child during the event because this can abruptly decrease cerebral perfusion.
- Work-up for **iron-deficiency anaemia** and treat accordingly.
- **Atropine:** useful in cases of long-duration pallid spells.

## Bruxism

- Teeth grinding.
- Common in up to **30% of children**.
- Age:
  - `<5 years` — normal.
- Commonly occurs during sleep (normal phenomenon).
- Daytime bruxism in children `>5 years` may be associated with anxiety disorders.

### Long-term problems

- Significant dental malocclusion.
- Jaw pain / temporomandibular joint pain.

### Management

- Older children (`>5 years`) may benefit from behavioural therapy.

## Pica

- Consumption of inedible/non-nutritive substances such as chalk, mud or paint by a child for at least **1 month**.
- Should be inappropriate for developmental or cultural practices.
- Age: `<5 years`.

### Risk factors

- Malnutrition.
- Iron-deficiency anaemia.
- Low socioeconomic status.
- Psychosocial stress:
  - Parental neglect.
  - Maternal deprivation.
- Developmental delay, e.g. cerebral palsy.

### Complications

- Lead poisoning (paint).
- Parasitic infestations.

### Management

- Behavioural therapy.
- One dose of deworming with **albendazole**.
- Low-dose iron supplements (**5–10 mg/kg/day**).

---

### Page 104

## Thumb sucking

- Self-soothing behaviour for a child.
- Noted in the age group after **6 months**.
- Peaks at **18–24 months**.
- Till **4 years**, it is normal; reassurance is required.
- `>4 years`: may indicate emotional stress/social insecurity.

### Management

For `>4 years`:
- Behavioural modifications.
- Positive reinforcement by praising the child when he/she is not sucking the thumb.
- Negative reinforcement, such as applying oil, is not recommended.

## Temper tantrums

- Starts at **18–24 months**.
- Persists till approximately **5 years**.
- Response of a child to physical or emotional challenges.

### Manifestations

- Crying.
- Kicking.
- Pushing.
- Head banging.
- Attention-seeking tactic.

### Management

- Reassure parents and ask them to remain calm during the episode.
- Child should be left alone in a place where he/she is unlikely to get harmed.
- Distraction: take the child away from that place.
- Time-out technique: leave the child alone for some time to settle down.

## Tics

- Fast, involuntary, paroxysmal, non-rhythmic, repetitive motor or vocal manifestations.
- Abrupt onset.

---

### Page 105

## Types of tics

### Motor tics

- **Simple motor tics:**
  - Eye blinking.
  - Neck jerking.
  - Shoulder shrugging.
- **Complex motor tics:**
  - Echopraxia — repeating movements/postures or imitating others.

### Vocal tics

- **Simple vocal tics:**
  - Throat clearing.
  - Cough.
  - Sniffing.
- **Complex vocal tics:**
  - Echolalia — repeating other's speech.
  - Palilalia — repeating one's own words/sentences.

### Age

- Starts by approximately **4–6 years**.
- Peaks by **10–12 years**.
- Persists till **18–20 years** .

### Tourette syndrome

- Age of onset `<18 years`.
- Both motor and vocal tics for **≥1 year**.

### Management

- Behavioural therapy.
- Long-lasting tics:
  - Neuroleptics such as **haloperidol**.
  - **Clonidine**.

## Nocturnal enuresis

- Urination at night after the age of **5 years** (age of bladder maturation ).
- Nearly complete evacuation of bladder at a wrong place/time for at least a month, for at least twice a month for at least 3 months.
- Males > females.

### Types

- **Primary:** present since birth.

---

### Page 106

## Nocturnal enuresis

### Secondary

- Child was previously dry at night for at least a period of the preceding 6 months.
- Causes:
  - UTI.
  - Diabetes insipidus.
  - Diabetes mellitus.
  - Stress.
  - Bowel/bladder dysfunction.

### Other types based on symptoms

1. **Monosymptomatic nocturnal enuresis**
2. **Non-monosymptomatic nocturnal enuresis**
   - Nocturnal enuresis with lower urinary tract symptoms (LUTS), e.g. hesitancy, urgency or dribbling of urine.

- Most common variety: **primary monosymptomatic nocturnal enuresis**.

### Management

#### Behavioural modifications

- Voiding before going to bed.
- Restrict fluids in the evening:
  - 40% fluids in morning.
  - 40% at noon.
  - 20% after 8 PM in the evening.
- Avoid caffeinated drinks:
  - Tea.
  - Coffee.
  - Soda.

#### Motivational therapy

- Verbal praising.
- Rewards for being dry at night.

#### Alarm therapy

- Conditioning the child to the sensation of a full bladder.
- Sensors are attached to underwear or sleeping mat.
- Motivational therapy + alarm therapy: approximately **10–70% success rate**.

#### Drug therapy

If the above therapies fail:
- **Short term:** oral desmopressin.
- **Long term:** oxybutynin/tolterodine — decrease uninhibited bladder contractions.
- **Imipramine** is not used because of adverse cardiovascular side effects.

---

# Nutrition

### Page 107

## 17. Breast Feeding

### SEARCHABLE LATCH / FOREMILK–HINDMILK RECONSTRUCTION

#### Correct attachment

- Baby close to mother; chest/abdomen aligned with the mother.
- Mouth opens **wide**; tongue down and forward.
- More areola is generally visible above the baby's mouth than below.
- Chin touches breast; nose is free or only minimally touching.
- Deep, rhythmic sucking with audible swallowing; no persistent clicking.
- Mother should not have persistent nipple pain or pinching.

#### Foremilk vs hindmilk

- Fat content generally **increases across a feed**; later milk tends to be higher in fat.
- Avoid treating foremilk and hindmilk as rigidly separate stored fluids or assigning an exact “minute” at which hindmilk begins.
- **Feeding on cue and effective milk removal** are more important than timing a feed to obtain “hindmilk.”


### Page 107

# BREAST FEEDING

## Breastfeeding basics

- Breastfeeding should be started as soon as possible.
- Exclusive breastfeeding should be continued till **6 months**.
- Top feeding should not be practised.
- Breast milk contains **88% water**.
- After 6 months, complementary feeds (semisolids → solid) are started.
- Breastfeeding should be continued till **2 years of age** along with complementary feeds.

## Maternal reflexes

### Prolactin reflex

- Prolactin helps in production of breast milk.
- Galactopoiesis.
- Active at night time; mother should not skip night feeds to maintain prolactin reflex.

### Oxytocin reflex

- Oxytocin acts on myoepithelial cells causing contraction.
- Helps in expression of milk → **galactokinesis**.
- Active at all times.
- Affected by maternal emotions.

## Neonatal reflexes

### Rooting reflex

- When the newborn baby's mouth touches the nipple at the start of breastfeeding, newborn turns the mouth towards the stimulus, helps in initiation of feeding.

### Sucking and swallowing reflex

- When the nipple region touches the baby's palate, baby starts the process of sucking.
- Once the baby's mouth gets filled with milk, swallowing reflex is activated and baby swallows the milk.

### Suck–swallow

- One breath cycle during breastfeeding is approximately **1 second**.
- Correct infant latch and position are important.

---

### Page 108

## Features of good attachment (latching)

- Baby's mouth is wide open.
- More areola is visible in baby's mouth **above the areola**; nipple is visible but lower part should be completely covered by baby's mouth.
- Lips are everted.
- Chin of the baby touches the mother's breast.

## Properties of breast milk

### Immunological advantages

- Predominant immunoglobulin: **IgA (secretory IgA)**.
- Others: immunoglobulins.
- Low levels of **para-aminobenzoic acid (PABA)** — protects against normal flora.
- Lactoferrin → protects from iron.
- IgA.
- **Bifidus factor** → protects from *E. coli* infection.
- **Bile-salt-stimulated lipase** → protects against giardiasis.

### Bioactive factors

- Epidermal growth factor (**EGF**).
- Transforming growth factor (**TGF**).
- Help enhance the integrity or maturity of the gastrointestinal epithelium.
- Decrease risk of allergy in later life.

---

### Page 109

## Nutritional properties

### Nutritive value

- Approximately **67 kcal/100 mL** (same for cow's milk).

### Carbohydrates

- **6–7 g/100 mL**; high compared with cow/buffalo milk.
- Majority of carbohydrate is **lactose**.

### Lactose

- Lactase breaks lactose into glucose and galactose.
- Galactose is further broken down into glucose.
- Lactose helps in production of lactoferrin → protects against *E. coli* infection.
- Lactose helps in colonization of GI tract by good bacteria like **Lactobacillus**.

### Galactooligosaccharides

- Important for nutrition of oligodendrocytes.
- Helps in myelination and brain growth.

### Fat

- Approximately **3.5–4 g/100 mL**.
- Almost same as cow's milk.
- Rich in polyunsaturated fatty acids.
- Contains **DHA (docosahexaenoic acid)**.
- DHA favours brain growth.

### Protein

- Approximately **0.9–1 g/100 mL**.
- Approximately 3 times less than cow's milk.
- Quality of breast-milk protein is superior.

| Feature | Breast milk | Cow's milk |
|---|---|---|
| Major protein | Whey predominant | Casein predominant |
| Ratio of whey:casein | Approximately 60:40 | Approximately 20:80 |
| Digestion | Whey protein is easily digestible | Casein is poorly digestible |
| Effect | Less likely to cause intestinal coagulation/constipation | Casein undergoes coagulation in intestine and may cause constipation |

### Examples of whey proteins in breast milk

- Lactoferrin.
- Lactoglobulin.

---

### Page 110

### Other nutritional components

- Amino acids promoting brain growth:
  - Taurine.
  - Cysteine.
- Increased risk of allergy with cow's-milk protein → cow's-milk protein allergy.

## Other benefits of breastfeeding

- Protection from illness/infection:
  - 14 times less likely to die of diarrhoea.
  - 4 times less likely to die of respiratory infection compared with a non-breastfed baby.
- Decreased risk of allergy.
- Decreased risk of diabetes and heart disease in later life.
- Higher IQ compared with babies who are not breastfed.

## Benefits for mother

- Helps in **uterine involution**.
- Decreased incidence of postpartum haemorrhage.
- Lactational amenorrhoea for around 6 months.
- Decreased risk of carcinoma of breast and ovary.
- Helps shedding weight after pregnancy.

## Deficiencies in breast milk

- Micronutrients:
  - Vitamin K.
  - Vitamin D.
  - Vitamin B12.
  - Vitamin E/A in pure vegan/vegetarian families.
- Iron:
  - Iron levels are low, but iron has good bioavailability.
  - Baby has sufficient iron stores from mother.
  - Routine iron supplementation is not required .

## Composition of breast milk

### Colostrum

- Milk secreted in the first **3–4 days**.
- Thick, yellowish.
- High fat content and immunoglobulins (especially IgA).

### Transitional milk

- Milk secreted till approximately **4 days after birth**.
- Protein content and immunoglobulins decrease.
- Fat and sugar content increase.

### Mature milk

- After **4 days**.

---

### Page 111

## Parts of breast milk

- **Foremilk:** whitish.
  - Rich in water.
  - Satisfies thirst of the child.
- **Hindmilk:** yellowish.
  - Rich in fat.
  - Satisfies satiety/hunger demand of the child.

## Preterm milk

- Milk secreted by a mother who has delivered a preterm baby.
- Contains more:
  - Sodium.
  - Iron.
  - Protein.
  - Calories/sugars.

## Contraindications of breastfeeding

### Absolute

- Lactose intolerance (congenital lactase deficiency) as listed in the notes.
- Galactosemia.
- Mother on chemotherapy/radiotherapy.

### Relative / infections in mother

- HIV:
  - Mother has to take antiretroviral treatment.
  - Baby has to take nevirapine prophylaxis.
- Tuberculosis if mother is not on antitubercular treatment or has completed `<2 weeks` of treatment.
- Herpes simplex and herpes zoster if mother has lesions on the breast.
- COVID infection in mother is **not** a contraindication for breastfeeding.

### Expressed breast milk storage

- Room temperature: **6–8 hours**.
- Refrigerator: **24 hours**.
- Freezer (`−20°C`): **3 months**.

---

### Page 112

## 18. Malnutrition

### CURRENT WHO 2023 WASTING / NUTRITIONAL-OEDEMA OVERLAY — exam-facing update

> **This current WHO section supersedes conflicting legacy Marrow admission, RUTF, antibiotic and feeding rules below.** The 2023 WHO guideline updates the older SAM framework.

#### Definitions in children <5 years

| Category | WHO 2023 criteria |
|---|---|
| **Acute malnutrition** | WHZ/WLZ <−2 and/or nutritional oedema |
| **Severe acute malnutrition (SAM)** | Nutritional oedema and/or WHZ/WLZ <−3 and/or **MUAC <115 mm** |
| **Moderate acute malnutrition (MAM)** | WHZ/WLZ −3 to <−2 and/or **MUAC 115 to <125 mm**, without oedema |

#### Who needs inpatient care?

Immediate inpatient referral is indicated with **danger signs, an acute medical problem, severe (+++) oedema, poor appetite or a failed appetite test**, or other illness requiring inpatient investigation/treatment. Further assessment is required for persistent failure to improve, significant nutrition-related comorbidity or recurrent severe episodes.

**Key exam point:** not every child with SAM needs admission. Uncomplicated severe wasting with **good appetite and no danger signs/acute complication** can generally be managed as an outpatient when a functioning programme is available.

#### Outpatient RUTF

- Initial therapeutic energy provision: approximately **150–185 kcal/kg/day** until anthropometric recovery and/or resolution of oedema according to the clinical pathway.
- Once the child is no longer severely wasted and has no oedema but still needs nutritional rehabilitation: approximately **100–130 kcal/kg/day**.
- MAM is managed with an appropriate nutrient-dense diet/programme plus assessment for underlying disease and growth failure.

#### Updated inpatient principles

1. Triage and treat urgent medical problems.
2. **Hypoglycaemia:** treat promptly and avoid prolonged fasting.
3. **Hypothermia:** rewarm and evaluate for infection.
4. **Dehydration:** assess carefully; avoid routine rapid fluid boluses and avoid over-hydration.
5. **Electrolytes:** potassium and magnesium need correction; avoid excess sodium.
6. **Infection:** signs may be subtle; evaluate and treat clinically.
7. **Micronutrients:** provide appropriately; routine iron is generally delayed until stabilization/return of appetite and early recovery unless specifically indicated.
8. Start cautious therapeutic feeding (e.g. F-75 in inpatient stabilization), then transition to higher-energy rehabilitation/RUTF when appropriate.
9. Monitor oedema, intake, weight trajectory and complications.
10. Transition to recovery/home nutrition and follow-up after stabilization.

#### Antibiotics — current principle

- Uncomplicated severe wasting managed as an outpatient may receive an oral antibiotic such as **amoxicillin** according to the WHO/local programme.
- Inpatient antibiotics are based on clinical status/local protocol.
- Do **not** apply a blanket rule that every child with malnutrition automatically receives the same prolonged antibiotic course.

**Reference:** WHO, *WHO guideline on the prevention and management of wasting and nutritional oedema (acute malnutrition) in infants and children under 5 years*, 2023.


### Page 112

# MALNUTRITION

## Classification of malnutrition

### Based on duration

- **Acute**
  - Weight is reduced.
  - Wasting.
  - Weight-for-height chart (WH).
- **Chronic**
  - Height is affected.
  - Stunting.
  - Height-for-age chart (HA).

### Based on severity (WHO charts)

- Moderate: approximately **−2 SD to −3 SD**.
- Severe: `<−3 SD`.

| Feature | Moderate malnutrition | Severe malnutrition |
|---|---|---|
| Symmetrical edema (bilateral pedal edema) | No | Yes |
| Weight-for-height | −2 to −3 SD | <−3 SD |
| Height-for-age | −2 to −3 SD | <−3 SD |

## Extreme forms of malnutrition (PEM/PCM)

- **Kwashiorkor**
- **Marasmus**

---

### Page 113

# Kwashiorkor

- Main sign is **pitting edema**.
- Predominant protein deficiency → albumin reduced → decreased oncotic pressure → edema.
- Caused by free-radical damage.
- Decompensated malnutrition/dysadaptative malnutrition.
- Onset: **1–4 years**.

### General appearance

- Dull/lethargic appearance.
- Poor appetite.
- Muscle wasting is present but masked by edema.
- Also called **fat-sugar baby** .

### Skin changes

- Areas of hyperpigmentation and areas of depigmentation called **flaky-paint dermatosis**.
- Cracks on skin surface: **crazy-pavement dermatosis**.
- Enamel spots/isolated spots.

### Hair changes

- Patchy pigmentation.
- Few areas of pigmentation with areas of no pigmentation → **flag sign**.
- Brittle, lustreless, easily pluckable hair.
- Loss of curls.

### Liver

- **Fatty liver** is present.

# Marasmus

- More common compared with kwashiorkor.
- Caused by severe deficiency of **calories** (predominant).
- Prominent and visible wasting is the main sign.
- Wasting is due to adaptation to starvation; hence called **adaptive starvation**.
- Mobilization of fat results in fat loss.
- Skin-and-bone appearance.
- **Simian facies** (monkey-like face).
- **Baggy-pants appearance** due to loss of fat and muscle.
- General appearance: alert, good appetite.
- Outcome is better because appetite is good.

---

### Page 114

## Kwashiorkor vs marasmus

| Feature | Kwashiorkor | Marasmus |
|---|---|---|
| Predominant deficiency | Protein | Calories |
| Onset | 1–4 years | <1 year |
| Edema | ++++ | Absent |
| Muscle wasting | ++ (hidden) | +++ visible/severe |
| Appetite | Poor | Good |
| General appearance | Lethargic, apathetic | Alert |
| Nutritional features | Hair changes, flag sign, skin changes, fatty liver | — |

# Severe Acute Malnutrition (SAM)

## Definition

Any one of the following criteria is sufficient:

1. **WHZ <−3 SD**
2. **Bipedal edema** (diagnosis of exclusion)
3. **Mid-arm circumference <11.5 cm** in children aged 6 months–5 years.

### Uncomplicated vs complicated SAM

| Feature | Uncomplicated SAM | Complicated SAM |
|---|---|---|
| Appetite test | Good | Poor |
| Generalized edema (anasarca) | Absent | Present |
| Medical complications | Absent | Present |
| Management | Supervised home management | Hospital admission |

## Home management in SAM

- Nutrition: **175 kcal/kg/day**.
- Protein: **4–6 g/kg/day**.
- Given by home-based diet or **RUTF**.

---

### Page 115

## RUTF — Ready-to-use therapeutic food

Oil-based paste made of:
- Sugars.
- Milk solids.
- Peanut paste.
- Vegetable oil.
- Added nutrients, minerals and antioxidants.

### Nutritive value

- Approximately **543 kcal + 15 g protein per 100 g**.
- Stable and can be stored unrefrigerated for many months.

### Antibiotics

- Oral **amoxicillin for 5 days**.

### Deworming

- Albendazole single dose:
  - `>2 years`: **400 mg**
  - `<2 years`: **200 mg**

### Vitamin A

- `<6 months`: **50,000 IU**
- 6–12 months: **100,000 IU**
- `>1 year`: **200,000 IU**

### Age-appropriate vaccination

- Give according to age.

### Sensorineural stimulation

- Emotional attachment.
- Taking care of the child.

### Follow-up

- Home visits by healthcare workers:
  - ASHA.
  - Anganwadi.
  - ANM.

## Hospital management in SAM

Consists of **10 steps** divided into:

1. **Stabilization phase:** first week in hospital.
2. **Rehabilitation phase:** second week onwards.

- Average duration of hospital stay: **6–8 weeks**.
- First 6 steps are management of complications.
- Next 4 steps are feeding with stimulation.

---

### Page 116

## Stabilization and rehabilitation

| Stabilization | Rehabilitation |
|---|---|
| Day 1–3 | Days 4–7 |
| Hypoglycemia | |
| Hypothermia | |
| Dehydration | |
| Electrolytes | 4–6 weeks |
| Infection | |
| Micronutrients | |
| Cautious feeding | With iron |
| Catch-up growth | |
| Sensory stimulation | |
| Prepare for follow-up | |

## Hypoglycemia

- Blood glucose `<54 mg/dL` in neonates and `<45 mg/dL` in the notes' treatment pathway.

### Treatment

- **Asymptomatic:** give oral sugars, e.g. 50 mL of 10% dextrose in infants, followed by expressed breast milk or feed.
- **Symptomatic/severe:** intravenous 10% dextrose.
- In a neonate, approximately 2 mL/kg of 10% dextrose → 50 mL of 10% dextrose via nasogastric tube → oral feeds.

## Hypothermia

- Rectal temperature `<35.5°C` or axillary temperature `<35°C`.
- Rapid rewarming should be avoided because it may lead to **dysequilibrium syndrome**:
  - Child becomes drowsy/comatose.
  - Seizures may occur.

### Management

- Term baby: cover the baby.
- Preterm baby: over-wrapping / **kangaroo mother care**.

## Infection

- Always suspect and look for infection.
- Usual signs may not be present; instead infection may manifest with hypothermia.
- Majority are caused by gram-negative bacteria.

---

### Page 117

### Antibiotics

- Start empirical antibiotics:
  - **Penicillin + aminoglycoside**.
  - Third-generation cephalosporin such as **cefotaxime** may be used.
- Meningitis: initial antibiotic **cefotaxime + amikacin**.

## Electrolyte imbalance

### Sodium

- Sodium values may appear low because of dilutional effect of edema → **dilutional hyponatremia**.
- Avoid excess salt/sodium in diet.

### Potassium

- The notes give potassium supplementation during treatment.

- Electrolyte imbalances must be corrected over approximately **2 weeks**.

## Dehydration

- Skin-pinch testing is unreliable because it depends on subcutaneous fat.

### Signs

- Dry oral mucosa.
- Urine output decreased (oliguria).
- Increased thirst.

It is safe to assume that all children with SAM have some dehydration.

### Correction of dehydration without shock

- Oral fluids over **12 hours**.
- First 2 hours: **5 mL/kg every 20 minutes**.
- Next 10 hours: **5–10 mL/kg/hour**.

### ReSoMal

**Rehydration Solution for Malnutrition**

- 1 packet of WHO ORS + **1 L water** + **50 g sucrose** + mineral mix, or approximately 45 mL of additional solution as indicated in the source.

### With shock

IV fluid:
- Ringer lactate with 5% dextrose, or
- ½ normal saline with 5% dextrose.

---

### Page 118

### Fluid response

- Give approximately **15 mL/kg over 1 hour**.
- Check for response.
- If response:
  - Repeat 15 mL/kg in 1 hour.
  - Oral fluids.
- If no response:
  - Treat like septic shock.

## Micronutrient deficiencies

- Definite and significant micronutrient deficiency is present in all children with SAM.
- Recommended to give **twice the RDA**.

### Iron

Iron is **not given in the first week** of management because:
1. It promotes free-radical damage to intestine.
2. It promotes bacterial proliferation.

- From the second week, iron is given at **3 mg/kg/day** for **2 months**.

### Vitamin A

- `>1 year but <8 kg`: ½ dose is given.
- If features of xerophthalmia are present:
  - 3 doses given on Day 0, Day 1 and Day 14.

### Vitamin K

- **2.5 mg IM** single dose.

## Principle of feeding in SAM

### First week

- **F-75 diet**
- Approximately **75 kcal + 0.9 g protein/100 mL**.

### Initial goal

- Approximately **80 kcal/kg/day + 0.8 g protein/kg/day**.

### Second week onwards

- Energy-dense **F-100 diet**:
  - Approximately **100 kcal + 2.9 g protein/100 mL**.
- Then RUTF → home-based diet.

### Sensory stimulation

- Structured play activity for at least **15–30 min/day**.

### Criteria for discharge

- No edema for at least **2 weeks**.
- WHZ reaches **−2 SD** or better.
- MUAC reaches **12.5 cm**.
- Deworming before discharge.

---

### Page 119

## Treatment failure

### Primary

#### Day 4 criteria

- Failure to regain appetite.
- Failure to start losing edema.

#### Day 10 criteria

- Presence of edema.
- Failure to gain at least **5 g/kg/day**.

### Secondary

- Failure to gain at least **5 g/kg/day for 3 consecutive days**.

### Reasons

- Latent infection.
- Malabsorption.

## Problems encountered during nutritional rehabilitation

### Pseudotumor cerebri

- High-energy feeds can result in increased ICP in the cerebrum.
- May require treatment.

### Refeeding syndrome

- Occurs due to calorie-rich feeds in the beginning rather than gradual increase.
- **Hypophosphatemia** is characteristic.

### Other electrolyte abnormalities

- Hypokalemia.
- Hypomagnesemia.
- Hypophosphatemia.

### Nutritional recovery syndrome

- Due to excess protein in the diet.
- Associated with:
  - Development of hepatosplenomegaly.
  - Abdominal distension due to ascites.
  - Parotid swellings.
  - Gynaecomastia.

### Encephalitis-like syndrome

- Child becomes drowsy and comatose.
- Seen during 3–4 days of treatment.
- Postulated to be due to high protein.
- Very rare and self-limiting.

---

### Page 120

## 19. Rickets and Scurvy

### Page 120

# RICKETS AND SCURVY

- Micronutrient disorders of **vitamins D and C**.

# Vitamin D deficiency

## Etiology

### Diet

- Low levels of vitamin D, calcium and phosphate.
- Vitamin D deficiency in diet is rare except in preterm babies because they require increased phosphate for growth.

### Sunlight

- Inadequate exposure to sunlight.
- Vitamin D is called the **sunshine vitamin**.

### Malabsorption

- Fat-soluble vitamins.
- Chronic liver disease.

### Drugs

- Long-term use of:
  - Anticonvulsants.
  - Antitubercular drugs, especially isoniazid.
- These can interfere with vitamin D metabolism.

## Pathophysiology

Vitamin D deficiency

↓

Impaired absorption of calcium and phosphate

↓

Decreased mineralization of bones (**osteomalacia**)

↓

- Decreased strength → increased risk of fractures.
- Disproportionate short stature due to short limbs.
- Development of deformities.

---

### Page 121

## Clinical features of rickets

### 1. Deformities

#### Head

- **Soft skull / craniotabes**
  - Ping-pong-ball consistency.
  - Not diagnostic.
- Craniotabes is also seen in:
  - Hydrocephalus.
  - Congenital syphilis.
  - Osteogenesis imperfecta.
- Widely separated sutures.
- Wide-open fontanelle with delayed closure.

#### Chest region

- **Rachitic rosary**
  - Widening/enlargement of costochondral junction.
  - String-of-beads appearance.

### Growth plate changes

- End of metaphysis is called the **growth plate**.
- Chondrocytes are normally present in the physial plate.
- After deposition of calcium and PO₄³⁻, apoptosis occurs.
- Replaced by osteocytes.
- Differentiation occurs into osteoblasts and osteoclasts.
- This process is called **endochondral ossification or calcification**.
- Calcification requires phosphorus.
- Apoptosis is inhibited by deficiency of PO₄³⁻, leading to accumulation of hypertrophic chondrocytes and swelling in the bone region.

### Harrison's groove/sulcus

- Due to pull of diaphragm against weak rib cage during each breath.

### Limbs

- Wrist widening due to chondrocyte hypertrophy.
- Lower limbs:
  - Bowing of legs due to medial deviation.
  - **Genu varus**.
  - Knock knees due to lateral deviation.
  - **Genu valgus**.

---

### Page 122

### Lower-limb deformities

- Bowing of legs:
  - Medial deviation of tibia.
  - **Genu varus**.
  - Common in lower limbs.
- Knock knees:
  - Lateral deviation of tibia.
  - **Genu valgus**.
- **Wind-swept deformity** may occur.

## Investigations

### Serum

- **Calcium:** decreased/normal.
- **Phosphate:** decreased.
  - Impaired absorption from intestine.
  - Increased excretion of phosphate under PTH influence.
- **Parathyroid hormone:** increased.
  - Causes osteoclast activation leading to bone resorption.
  - Hence, in rickets calcium tends to be normal.
- **Alkaline phosphatase (ALP):** increased.
  - Osteoblast activity and bone formation.
- **25-hydroxycholecalciferol (25-OH-D₃):**
  - Active form is 1,25-dihydroxycholecalciferol and cannot be estimated in the lab according to the source.

### Radiological

- Preferred for diagnosis.
- X-ray changes:
  - Loss of normal zone of provisional calcification (ZPC).
  - Irregularity at the end of metaphysis.
  - **Cupping**
  - **Fraying**
  - **Splaying** of growth plate/lateral deviation.

---

### Page 123

## Treatment

# Vitamin D therapy

### Oral (preferred)

#### High dose, short duration — Stoss therapy

- **60,000 IU daily or on alternate days for 10 days**.
- Total dose approximately **6 lakh IU**.

#### Low dose, long duration — preferred

- **2,000–6,000 IU/day for 12 weeks**, depending on age.

#### Maintenance

After deficiency is corrected:
- **400 IU/day** for `<1 year`.
- **600 IU/day** for `>1 year`.

### Calcium

- **50–75 mg/kg/day**.

## Response to treatment

- Follow up at 4 weeks.
- X-ray showing healing of rickets due to deposition of calcium at physiological levels.
- If no response, it is called **refractory rickets**.

# Refractory rickets

### Disorders

1. Hypophosphatemic rickets.
2. Vitamin D-dependent rickets.
3. Vitamin D-resistant rickets.
4. Chronic kidney disease-associated rickets.

### X-linked hypophosphatemic rickets

- X-linked dominant inheritance.
- Most common inherited form of rickets.
- Due to mutation in the **PHEX gene** (phosphate-regulating gene with homologies to endopeptidases on the X chromosome).

---

### Page 124

## X-linked hypophosphatemic rickets

### Normal mechanism

- **PHEX gene** codes for an endopeptidase that inactivates phosphatonin (**FGF-23, fibroblast growth factor-23**).

### Mutation in PHEX gene

PHEX mutation

→ absent/reduced endopeptidase

→ continued activation of phosphatonin (FGF-23)

→ inhibition of renal tubular reabsorption of PO₄³⁻

→ increased urinary phosphate excretion

→ **hypophosphatemia**

### Clinical features

- Presents like vitamin D deficiency rickets but is diagnosed with biochemical investigations.
- Lower-limb deformities are observed.
- Dental abnormalities:
  - Pulp defects.
  - Increased risk of dental abscess.

### Treatment

- Oral phosphate replacement (e.g. phosphate solution).
- FGF-23 decreases active vitamin D.
- Calcitriol or a calcitriol analogue is used as appropriate.
- **Burosumab** — a humanized monoclonal antibody directed against FGF-23.

# Vitamin D-dependent rickets (VDDR)

---

### Page 125

## Types of VDDR

| Type I VDDR | Type II VDDR |
|---|---|
| Congenital absence of 1α-hydroxylase | Resistance of vitamin D receptors to its action |
| Thus, active form of vitamin D is not formed | Autosomal recessive |
| Autosomal recessive | Alopecia |
| Presents like rickets | Ectodermal dysplasia/defects |
| Treatment: Calcitriol or α-calcidiol | Oligodontia/absence of teeth |
| | Formation of epidermal cysts |
| | Treatment: response is unsatisfactory; high dose of calcium can give some relief |

## Algorithmic approach to refractory rickets

### Serum phosphate

- **Low**
  - Blood pH/acid-base status.
  - Renal tubular acidosis (RTA) associated rickets.
  - Proximal/distal RTA.
- **High**
  - CKD-associated rickets.
  - Rickets due to decreased phosphate excretion.

If serum phosphate is low:
- Check **PTH**.
- High PTH → VDDR/other causes as shown in the algorithm.
- Normal PTH → hypophosphatemic rickets.

---

### Page 126

# Scurvy

- Caused by **vitamin C deficiency**.

## Role of vitamin C

- Maintenance of connective-tissue collagen.
- Collagen is formed by hydroxylation of **proline and lysine**.
- Vitamin C is needed for the process of hydroxylation.
- Wound healing (similar to zinc).
- Absorption of iron from intestine.
- Osteoid formation.

### Vitamin-C-rich foods

- Citrus fruits:
  - Orange.
  - Lemon.
- Green vegetables.

## Risk groups

- Children consuming only pasteurized cow's milk.
  - Breast milk has sufficient vitamin C.
- Children consuming a diet lacking vitamin C.

## Features of scurvy

### Early features

1. Irritability.
2. Decreased appetite.
3. Anaemia.
4. Petechiae due to increased fragility of capillaries.
5. Bleeding manifestations:
   - Gums.
   - Perifollicular bleeds → corkscrew appearance of hair follicles.
   - Subperiosteal bleed → pseudoparalysis due to pain with movement.
6. **Scorbutic rosary**
   - Tenderness.
   - Angulated appearance.

---

### Page 127

## Scurvy vs rickets

| Feature | Scurvy (vitamin C deficiency) | Rachitic rosary (vitamin D deficiency) |
|---|---|---|
| Tenderness | Present | — |
| Appearance | Angulated rosary | Rounded rosary |

## Radiological findings in scurvy

On X-ray:

1. **White line of Frankel**
   - Well-calcified cartilage.
2. **Scorbutic/Trummerfeld's zone**
   - Central zone of rarefaction.
   - Represents poorly formed bony trabeculae.
3. **Pelkan's spur**
   - Lateral zone of rarefaction.
4. **Ring epiphysis / Wimberger's ring**
   - Ring around the epiphysis.
5. **Ground-glass appearance**.

### Diagnosis

- Diagnosis is made by clinical appearance of the child.

### Investigations

- Leukocyte vitamin C levels using **Burby/biochemical assay** as shown in the notes.
- Serum vitamin C level (best).

### Dietary treatment

- Approximately **100–200 mg/day** of vitamin C (equivalent to approximately 100 mL of orange juice or tomato pulp) as shown in the notes.

---

# Genetic Disorders

### Page 128

## 20. Genetic Disorders

# GENETIC DISORDERS

## Aneuploidies

- **Aneuploidy:** Difference in the normal number of chromosomes.
- **Trisomy:** An extra chromosome makes a total of 3 instead of a pair, e.g. trisomy 21.
- **Monosomy:** Only one chromosome instead of a pair, e.g. monosomy X, with only one X chromosome in a female.

---

## Down syndrome — Trisomy 21

It is the most common trisomy, the most common trisomy in live babies, and the most common genetic cause of low IQ/intellectual disability.

### Genetic basis

- **Maternal meiotic nondisjunction of chromosome 21** is the most common cause; seen in **95%** of cases.
- **Robertsonian translocation:** Translocation between two acrocentric chromosomes; seen in **4%** of cases.
- **Mosaicism:** A mixture of trisomy 21 cells and normal cells in a child; seen in **1%** of cases.

### External features

#### Dysmorphic facies

- Mongoloid/upslanting slant of eyes: eyes are oriented upwards.
- **Brushfield's spots:** White spots on the iris.
- **Epicanthal folds:** Extra fold of skin that increases the apparent space between the eyes and nose.

### Head and face

- Brachycephalic skull with a flat nose, occiput and face.
- Low-set ears: the entire ear is below the imaginary horizontal line drawn from the outer canthus.
- Protruding tongue due to hypoplastic mandible.

### Hands and feet

- **Simian crease:** Single transverse palmar crease (normally 3 creases are present).
- **Clinodactyly:** The little finger is curved.
- **Sandal gap:** Wide gap between the first and second toe.

### Internal defects/anomalies

- Low IQ and hypotonia.
- **Cardiac defects**
  - They are the most common cause of mortality and morbidity in children with Down syndrome.
  - The heart is the most common organ affected.
  - Seen in **50%** of children with Down syndrome.
  - Common order:
    - Endocardial cushion defect / atrioventricular septal defect
    - VSD
    - ASD
- **Gastrointestinal defects**
  - Most common: duodenal atresia.
  - Hirschsprung disease.
  - Annular pancreas.
- Increased risk of hypothyroidism is seen in **30%** of cases. Hence, regular screening is to be done.
- **Increased risk of cancers**
  - ALL: overall most common leukemia.
  - AML (M7, megakaryocytic type) is the most common in children less than 3 years of age.
- **Presenile Alzheimer's disease**
  - Seen with onset at around 40 years.
  - Chromosome 21 has a specific gene coding for APP (amyloid precursor protein).
  - Extra chromosome 21 → increased production of beta-amyloid → accumulation in the brain → predisposes to presenile Alzheimer's disease.
- **Atlanto-axial subluxation** is seen with a short neck.
  - Screening neck X-ray by 3–5 years of age is to be done.
  - These children are advised to avoid contact sports.

---

## Antenatal screening tests

### Antenatal USG

- **Nuchal translucency** in the 1st trimester or **nuchal fold thickness** in the 2nd trimester.
- Nuchal translucency/fold thickness **>3 mm** is suggestive.

### Markers from maternal blood

- **1st trimester: Double test**
  - Pregnancy-associated plasma protein A (**PAPP-A**)
  - **β-hCG**
- **2nd trimester: Triple test**
  - β-hCG
  - Alpha-fetoprotein (**AFP**)
  - Unconjugated estriol
- **Quadruple test:** Triple test + **inhibin A**.

### Interpretation of markers

- β-hCG and inhibin A are elevated.
- Other markers are reduced.

### Combined and integrated tests

- **Combined test:** 1st-trimester USG + double test.
- **Integrated test:** 1st-trimester combined test + 2nd-trimester quadruple test.

### Other tests

- **Cell-free fetal DNA testing (cffDNA):** Some fetal DNA enters maternal circulation and can be detected in blood. It is also called **non-invasive prenatal testing (NIPT)**.
- **CVS:** Chorionic villous sampling at 11–13 weeks.
- **Amniocentesis:** Done around 15 weeks of gestation.

A confirmatory test is done only after the screening test shows high risk because invasive tests carry a possibility of inducing abortion/termination.

### Recurrence risk of Down syndrome

- General risk: **1%**
- Advanced maternal age (>35 years): **4%**
- Robertsonian translocation in parents:
  - t(14,21) in father: approximately **3–5%**
  - t(14,21) in mother: approximately **10–15%**
