  - t(21,21) in father or mother: **100%**

---

## Trisomy 18 — Edwards syndrome

- Most commonly associated with spontaneous abortion.
- **Prominent occiput**
- Increased risk of cleft lip/palate
- Closed fists with overlapping fingers
- Short neck
- **Rocker-bottom foot:** Prominent heels; appearance resembles the bottom of a rocking chair.
- Limited hip abduction.
- Malformed ears.

### Associated organ defects/anomalies

- Cardiac defects: **>90%** of cases.
  - VSD > PDA > ASD
- Renal defects:
  - Horseshoe kidney

---

## Trisomy 13 — Patau syndrome

### External characters

- **Cleft lip and/or palate** — up to 80% of cases.
- Dysplastic or malformed ears.
- **Aplasia cutis:** Absence of skin in areas of the scalp.
- Fingers:
  - Unseparated or fused fingers.
  - Postaxial polydactyly.
  - Clinodactyly.
- Undescended testis / cryptorchidism.

### Associated defects

- Heart defects: up to 90% of cases.
  - VSD > PDA > ASD
- Absent or missing ribs.
- **Holoprosencephaly / arrhinencephaly**

---

## Monosomy X — Turner syndrome

- Represented as **45,XO** — 0 for the absent X chromosome.
- Genetic basis: loss of the X chromosome during paternal gametogenesis.

Among cases after karyotype analysis:
- **57%** have complete monosomy X.
- **29%** have mosaicism.
- **14%** have partial monosomy X; the X chromosome is not completely deleted and may be a ring chromosome.

### External features

- Short stature (**<145 cm even as adults**).
- Triangular face.
- Neck:
  - Loose skin folds.
  - Webbed neck.
  - Low posterior hairline.
- Chest:
  - Flat/shield chest.
  - Widely spaced nipples.
- Increased carrying angle (cubitus valgus).
- Short 4th metacarpals.

### Increased risk of organ defects

- **Heart**
  - Bicuspid aortic valve
  - Coarctation of the aorta
- **Gonads**
  - Underdeveloped ovaries (streak ovaries)
  - Reduced fertility and amenorrhea

### Presentation in neonatal period or infancy

- Dorsal lymphedema of feet/hands due to lymphatic obstruction.
- In the neck, it is called **cystic hygroma**.

### Other features

- Normal IQ, but learning disabilities.
- Turner karyotype 45,XO is the most common chromosomal abnormality seen in spontaneous abortions.
- The trisomy most commonly associated with spontaneous abortions in pregnancy is **trisomy 16**.

### Management

- Estrogen replacement until secondary sexual characters are acquired.
- Recombinant growth hormone (rGH) to attain height.

---

## Noonan syndrome / Pseudo-Turner syndrome

- Not an aneuploidy.
- Seen in females as well as males.
- Features are similar to Turner syndrome.
- Autosomal dominant.
- Chromosome **12q24.1**.
- **PTPN11** gene: Protein Tyrosine Phosphatase Non-Receptor type 11.

### Features similar to Turner syndrome

- Triangular face due to small chin.
- Flat chest.
- Widely spaced nipples.
- Cubitus valgus.
- Webbing of the neck.

### Differences between Turner and Noonan syndrome

| Feature | Turner syndrome | Noonan syndrome |
|---|---|---|
| Karyotype | 45,XO (female only) | 46,XX (female), 46,XY (male) |
| IQ | Normal | Low IQ may occur |
| Gonadal defects | Streak ovaries | Cryptorchidism in males; delayed puberty and low fertility |
| Cardiac defects | Bicuspid aortic valve; coarctation of aorta | Pulmonary stenosis; hypertrophic obstructive cardiomyopathy (HOCM); atrial septal defect |

---

## Klinefelter syndrome

- Karyotypic abnormality: **47,XXY**.
- Due to nondisjunction of the X chromosome during meiosis of gametogenesis.
- Hence, paternal and maternal defects equally contribute.
- Risk factor: **increased paternal age**.
- Klinefelter syndrome is the most common genetic cause of infertility/hypogonadism in males.

### Clinical features

- Taller than average height; legs are unusually long.
- Cryptorchidism leads to low levels of testosterone.
- **Eunuchoid body habitus:** Absence of axillary and pubic hair, delayed appearance of secondary sexual characters.
- Small penis and testes.
- Atrophy of testes and seminiferous tubules at late stages.
- Increased incidence of infertility.

### Puberty in Klinefelter syndrome

- Starts at a normal age.
- Progression is very delayed.

### Gynecomastia

- Due to increased activity of the enzyme aromatase, which converts testosterone into estrogenic metabolites (estradiol).
- An increase in estrogen metabolites increases the risk of male breast cancer.

### Other features

- Low IQ and learning disabilities (verbal skills affected).
- Behavioural problems.
- Increased risk of extragonadal germ cell tumors, especially in the **anterior mediastinum**.

---

## Microdeletion syndromes

- Small deletions which are not detectable in karyotyping.
- **FISH (fluorescent in situ hybridization):** Used for diagnosis.

### DiGeorge syndrome

- **22q11.2 microdeletion.**
- Abnormalities in the development of the 3rd and 4th pharyngeal pouches.
- The thymus and parathyroid are affected.

### Mnemonic for clinical features: CATCH-22

**C — Cardiac**
- Conotruncal defects:
  - Tetralogy of Fallot
  - Interrupted aortic arch
  - Right-sided aortic arch

**A — Abnormal facies**
- Hypertelorism: widely separated eyes.
- Thin upper lip/small mouth.
- Small jaw.
- Low-lying/notched ears.

**T — Thymic hypoplasia**
- T-cell deficiency due to thymic hypoplasia/aplasia.

**C — Cleft palate**
- Cleft lip/palate.

**H — Hypocalcemia**
- Due to low parathyroid hormone.
- Can result in seizures during infancy.

DiGeorge syndrome is also called **velocardiofacial syndrome**.

### Microdeletion 7q11.23 — Williams syndrome

#### Facial appearance

- Wide mouth.
- Prominent forehead.
- Puffiness in eyes and cheeks.
- These features resemble elves: **elfin facies**.
- These children have happy and friendly personalities.

#### Other features

- Hypercalcemia.
- Low IQ and learning disabilities.

---

## Genomic imprinting disorders

Each gene has two alleles, paternal and maternal. Out of them, one is normally silenced or imprinted and only one is active.

In these disorders:
- Deletion of the active allele (most common), or
- Disomy of the inactive allele.

### Examples

- Prader-Willi syndrome
- Angelman syndrome

### Prader-Willi syndrome

- Deletion of the paternal allele in chromosome **15q11-q13** is the common cause, or paternal allele is absent due to **maternal uniparental disomy**.

#### Clinical features

- Obesity due to increased intake of food (hyperphagia), related to increased **ghrelin**, which stimulates appetite.
- Shorter compared to children of the same age.
- Small almond-shaped eyes.
- Small hands and feet.
- Hypogonadism: gonads are not properly developed, resulting in delayed puberty.
- Low IQ.

### Angelman syndrome

- Chromosome **15q11-q13**.
- Deletion of the active maternal allele is the common cause.
- Disomy of paternal allele.

#### Clinical features

- Inappropriate laughter.
- Ataxia.
- Very low IQ.
- Increased incidence of seizures.
- Hypotonia.

---

## Other genetic syndromes

### Treacher Collins syndrome

- Autosomal dominant disorder.
- Also known as **mandibulofacial dysostosis**.

#### Clinical features

- Slant of eyes: downward slant (antimongoloid slant).
- Coloboma of lower eyelids.
- Sunken cheeks.
- Underdeveloped or small mandible.
- Dental malocclusion (malaligned teeth).
- Abnormal hair distribution.
- Increased risk of conductive hearing loss due to malformation of ossicles and defective development.

### Pierre Robin sequence

- It is not a condition by itself but is associated with other conditions.
- It is a sequence or chain of events.

#### Order of the sequence

1. Severely underdeveloped mandible in utero (common starting point).
2. The tongue is pushed back (**retroglossoptosis**).
   - It interferes with development of the palate, resulting in a big **U-shaped cleft palate** corresponding to the shape of the tongue.
   - Can predispose to aspiration of feeds after birth.
3. Results in airway obstruction after birth because the tongue is pushed back.

### Associations with other syndromes

- Fetal alcohol syndrome.
- Trisomy 13.
- In Treacher Collins syndrome, a severely affected mandible results in Pierre Robin sequence.

# Infectious Disorders

### Page 141

## 21. Common Childhood Infections

### Mnemonic for day of appearance of rash in a febrile child

**Very Sick Persons must Take Rest**

- **Varicella** — 1st day
- **Scarlet fever** — 2nd day
- **Roseola / exanthema subitum** — 3rd day
- **Measles** — 4th day
- **Typhus (epidemic)** — 5th day
- **Rickettsia** — 6th day

---

## Varicella — Chickenpox

- Causative agent: **Varicella-zoster virus** (double-stranded DNA virus).
- Incubation period: **10–21 days**.
- Period of infectivity: **24–48 hours before onset of rash until all lesions are crusted**.
- Highly infective; secondary attack rate for household contacts is approximately **80%**.

### Rash

- High-grade fever.
- Rash appears on the **1st day of fever**.
- Initially macular → papular → vesicular rash.
- Characteristic vesicular rash is intensely pruritic.
- **Centripetal rash:** Starts in the trunk and spreads to limbs.
- Rash lasts **3–5 days** and leaves behind brown pigmentation when it goes away.
- Different stages of lesions can be seen simultaneously.
- **Dewdrop on a rose petal:** Clear fluid-filled vesicle surrounded by erythema.

### Complications

- Secondary bacterial infection by:
  - *Staphylococcus aureus*
  - *Streptococcus pyogenes*
- Neurological:
  - Meningoencephalitis
  - Cerebellar ataxia
- Risk of **Reye's syndrome**: avoid aspirin.

### Diagnosis

- **Tzanck smear:** Scraping of lesions.
- Anti-IgM antibody demonstration against varicella virus.

### Treatment

- **Acyclovir:** Antiviral of choice; **20 mg/kg/dose**.
- To be started within **48 hours**; not of much benefit later.
- The child must not attend school until no new lesion appears and all existing lesions have crusted.

### Prevention

- Varicella vaccine:
  - 1st dose: **15–18 months**
  - 2nd dose: **4–6 years**

### Post-exposure prophylaxis

- **Varicella-zoster immunoglobulin**
- Recommended only for high-risk individuals:
  - Pregnant women.
  - Neonate whose mother developed chickenpox within 5 days before to 5 days after delivery.
  - Immunocompromised patients.

---

## Scarlet fever

- Etiology: **Group A β-hemolytic streptococci (Streptococcus pyogenes)**.
- Age group: School-going children, approximately **5–15 years**.

### Features

- Fever with rash on the **2nd day**.
- Rash:
  - Starts from the face.
  - Fine maculopapular rash.
  - Spreads to the trunk.
- **Sandpaper rash:** Skin appears rough.
- **Pastia's lines:** Accentuation of rash in skin creases.
- Sore throat: pus in the throat.
- **Strawberry tongue:** Due to intense inflammation/hypertrophy of papillae.

### Investigation

- Culture of throat swab.

### Treatment

- **Penicillin for 10 days** is the treatment of choice.

### Complications

- Acute rheumatic fever.
- Post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis.

---

## Measles

- Viral infection: **single-stranded RNA virus** of the family Paramyxoviridae.
- Incubation period: **6–18 days**.
- Secondary attack rate: **>90%**. Highly infective.
- Infectivity period: 4 days before appearance of rash to 5 days after appearance of rash.

### Characteristic features

- **Prodromal phase:** Fever with cough, coryza and conjunctivitis.
- **Koplik spots:** Pathognomonic of measles.
  - White spots surrounded by redness.
  - Rice-grain appearance surrounded by erythema.
  - Location: buccal mucosa opposite the second lower molar.
- **Maculopapular rash**
  - Starts from the face, behind the ear.
  - Spreads to trunk and then extremities.
  - Duration of rash: 4 days.
  - Disappears leaving brownish hyperpigmentation.

### Complications

- Most common acute complication: **otitis media**.
- Common complication: **bronchopneumonia**.
- Giant cell pneumonia / Hecht's pneumonia:
  - Etiological agent is the virus itself.
- Bacterial pneumonia may occur due to *Staphylococcus*.

### Subacute sclerosing panencephalitis (SSPE)

- Chronic complication.
- Occurs **7–13 years after infection**.
- Decrease in school performance.
- Behavioural problems.
- Myoclonic seizures.
- Fatal condition.

### Investigations

- CSF testing for measles-specific antibodies (IgG).
- EEG: **Rademacher complexes** — periodic high-voltage discharges on a slow background.

### Treatment

- Isoprinosine along with interferon gamma (intrathecal route).
- Supportive treatment only: IV/oral fluids.
- **Vitamin A:** Single dose orally; reduces mortality and morbidity.
  - <1 year: **1 lakh IU**
  - >1 year: **3 lakh IU**

### Prevention

- **MR vaccine:** Measles-rubella vaccine.
- Included in the National Immunisation Schedule.

---

## Exanthema subitum

- Also called **sixth disease** or **roseola infantum**.
- On the **4th day of fever**, the rash appears.
- Etiology: **Human herpesvirus 6 (HHV-6)**.

### Features

- Fever with viral prodrome:
  - Lethargy
  - Cough
  - Running nose
- Maculopapular rash appears on the 4th day.
- It has to be differentiated from measles.
- After the rash starts, fever decreases.
- No residual pigmentation after the rash disappears.
- **Nagayama spots:** Ulcers at the uvulopalatal-glossal junction.
- Symptomatic treatment; prognosis is excellent.

---

## Rubella

- Etiology: **single-stranded RNA virus**, family Togaviridae.
- Incubation period: **14–21 days**.
- Infectivity period: approximately 5 days before to 5 days after the rash.
- Other names: **3-day measles / German measles**.

### Features

- Rash is similar to measles: maculopapular and starts from the face.
- Total duration of rash: **3 days**.
- Prodromal features may be present or absent.
- Rash:
  - Starts from the face and spreads to the trunk.
  - Duration: 3 days.
  - No residual pigmentation or desquamation after the rash disappears.

### Other features/complications

- **Forchheimer spots:** Tiny rose-coloured lesions in the oropharynx.
- Post-thrombocytopenia: approximately 2 weeks after the rash.
- Arthritis: adolescents/adults.
- Most severe: **progressive rubella panencephalitis** — fatal disease.

### Prevention

- MR vaccine.

---

## Parvovirus B19 infection

- Etiology: **Parvovirus B19**, single-stranded DNA virus.
- Incubation period: **4–18 days**.
- Age group: School-going children.

### Features

- Fever with prodrome for 1–2 days.
- **Slapped-cheek appearance:** Intense redness of cheeks.
- Starts at the face and spreads to the trunk.
- Central clearing is seen in the rash, producing a **lacy/reticulated** appearance.
- Arthralgia/arthropathy: older children.
- In children with chronic haemolytic anaemia, it can predispose to **transient aplastic crisis** with pancytopenia.
- If the condition occurs in pregnancy, it can lead to **hydrops fetalis** and may be associated with intrauterine death or stillbirth.

---

## Epidemic typhus

- Triad:
  - Fever
  - Rash
  - Headache
- Seen in approximately **50%** of patients.
- Rash on **day 5 of fever**.
- Purpuric rash involves palms and soles.

### Treatment

- **Doxycycline** for 5–7 days and for at least 3 days after the patient becomes afebrile to prevent relapse.

---

## Hand-foot-mouth disease (HFMD)

- Common viral illness in children **<5 years**.
- Usually seen as outbreaks in preschools.
- Causative agents:
  - Coxsackie A16
  - Enterovirus 71

### Transmission

- Direct contact or use of infected fomites.

### Features

- Fever.
- Ulcers or blisters in the oral cavity.
- Classical blisters are seen around the mouth.
- On palms and feet: papulovesicular lesions.
- Lesions resolve in **4–5 days**.
- Does not usually cause complications.

### Treatment

- Symptomatic treatment.
- Isolation of affected children at home until all lesions are resolved.

### Page 151

# 22. TORCH Infections

## Intrauterine infections

- Transmitted from mother to baby in intrauterine life.
- Congenital infections:
  1. Toxoplasmosis
  2. Others (syphilis, Zika virus)
  3. Rubella
  4. Cytomegalovirus (most common)
  5. Herpes (herpes simplex, herpes zoster)

### Clinical features

- Primary infection of the mother that affects the baby.
  - Exception: in syphilis, even latent infection can affect the baby.
- Congenital anomalies are especially associated with infection during the **1st trimester (organogenesis)**.

### Common manifestations of intrauterine infections

- Intrauterine death.
- Preterm baby.
- Abortions.
- Recurrent abortions (syphilis).
- If the baby is severely affected:
  - Anemia.
  - Thrombocytopenia → petechiae.
  - Hepatosplenomegaly.
  - Jaundice / abnormal LFTs.
  - May present as neonatal sepsis.

---

## Congenital Rubella syndrome

- Severe intrauterine infection.
- Triad:
  - **Cataract**
  - **Deafness** (sensorineural hearing loss)
  - **Cardiac defects**
- Heart defects:
  - **PDA**
  - Pulmonary artery stenosis
- Most distinctive ocular manifestation: **salt-and-pepper retinopathy**.
- Most distinctive manifestation: sensorineural deafness.
- Virus survives for a long time in the brain → progressive rubella panencephalitis (PRP).

### Features of PRP

- Seizures.
- Intellectual impairment.

### Other features

- Increased risk of diabetes.
- Retinopathy.
- Increased risk of hypothyroidism.

### Diagnosis

- Demonstration of **IgM rubella antibodies** (may be repeated after a few days if suspicion is high but test is negative).

### Treatment

- No treatment; severe/fatal complications may occur.

### Prevention

- Live attenuated vaccine (**RA 27/3 strain**).

---

## Congenital cytomegalovirus (CMV) infection

- **Most common TORCH infection.**
- Risk of transmission from mother to baby is high in the **3rd trimester**.

### Transmission

- Congenital/intrauterine transmission.
- Most infected babies are asymptomatic (around **90%**).

### Congenital cytomegalic inclusion disease

Affects a minority of babies.

#### Triad

- Microcephaly
- Chorioretinitis
- Intracranial calcifications

**Calcifications:** Periventricular / around the ventricles.

### Diagnosis

- **PCR testing** should be done within **2 weeks after birth**, as infection acquired intrauterine versus postnatally may be difficult to distinguish later.
- Samples:
  - Urine — best
  - Blood
  - Saliva — screening
- IgM test.

### Treatment

- **Drug of choice: Ganciclovir**
- Used if the baby has progressive neurological disease or sensorineural hearing loss.
- IV ganciclovir for **6 weeks** → oral ganciclovir for **6 months**.

### Postnatal transmission

- Transmission through breast milk.
- Baby is usually asymptomatic.
- Preterm / very-low-birth-weight baby may present like sepsis-like illness or pneumonia with respiratory distress.

---

## Congenital toxoplasmosis

- Risk of transmission is high in the **3rd trimester**.
- Mostly asymptomatic.
- If infection occurs in the 1st trimester, the baby may present with the following clinical features.

### Triad

- Chorioretinitis
- Intracranial calcifications
- Hydrocephalus / microcephaly

**Calcifications:** Diffuse intracranial calcifications scattered throughout the brain, unlike the periventricular calcifications of CMV.

### Diagnosis

- Serology:
  - Double-sandwich ELISA testing: IgM/IgA ELISA.
  - IgA ELISA: more sensitive.
  - IgM-ISAGA (immunosorbent agglutination assay).

### Treatment

- All babies should be treated.
- **Pyrimethamine + sulfadiazine** for 1 year.
- Add supplementation:
  - Folinic acid / leucovorin.

### Prevention

- Fruits and vegetables should be washed.
- Pregnant women should avoid eating uncooked food.
- If the mother is already affected, she can be given **spiramycin**.

---

## Herpes zoster / Varicella infection

### Modes of transmission

1. **Perinatal**
   - More common, less severe.
   - At-risk period: when the mother gets infection just 5 days before / 5 days after delivery.
   - Administer varicella-zoster immunoglobulin or IV Ig to the baby after birth to prevent lesions.
   - If lesions develop → acyclovir.
2. **Intrauterine / congenital**
   - More severe.
   - Risk of transmission is more in the **2nd trimester**.
   - Cerebral atrophy → microcephaly.
   - Hypoplasia of limbs (short limbs).
   - Scars similar to burns → **cicatricial scarring**.

---

## Herpes simplex infection

- Usually transmitted in the perinatal period when the baby comes in contact with vesicles during delivery.
- Therefore, **cesarean section** is more preferred in affected mothers.
- HSV-2 is more common.

### 3 patterns of presentation

1. **Classical pattern (most common)**
   - Development of vesicles in skin, eyes and mouth.
2. **CNS manifestations**
   - Meningoencephalitis with seizures.
3. **Disseminated disease**

### Diagnosis

- PCR testing.

### Treatment

- **Acyclovir for 3 weeks**.

---

## Congenital syphilis

- Rare.
- Only intrauterine infection which is associated with **recurrent abortions**.
- Usually transmitted after 4 months of gestation (2nd and 3rd trimesters).

### Early onset

- Within 2 years after birth.
- Vesiculobullous rash (can involve hands and feet).
- X-rays:
  - Intense inflammation of bone (**periostitis**).
- Intense inflammation in limbs → severe pain → **Pseudoparalysis of Parrot** (child does not move limbs).
- Discharge from nose and mouth similar to rhinitis → **snuffles**.

### Late onset

- After 2 years of birth.
- **Hutchinson's triad**
  - Hutchinson's teeth (notching / peg-shaped incisors)
  - Interstitial keratitis
  - Deafness (sensorineural hearing loss)
- **Mulberry molars:** Irregular surface; not part of Hutchinson's triad.
- Deformed nose:
  - Intense inflammation → destruction of nasal septum and bone.
  - Later → complete destruction of nose.
- **Rhagades:** Presence of fissures around mouth.
- **Saber shin:** Bowing of tibia.
- **Clutton's joints:** Bilateral knee joint effusions.

### Diagnosis

- VDRL test.
- Compare VDRL titers.
- If there is a **4-fold increase** in baby → congenital syphilis.
- Rapid plasma reagin (**RPR**).

### Treatment

- IV procaine penicillin / crystalline penicillin for **10 days**.
- Ceftriaxone if penicillin is not available.

---

## Congenital Zika infection

- Transmitted by mosquito (**Aedes aegypti**).
- Other modes:
  - Sexual
  - Blood-borne
  - Materno-fetal transmission
- Risk of transmission is high in the **1st trimester**.

### Features

1. **Skull deformities**
   - Partially collapsed skull.
   - Overlapping sutures.
   - Prominent occiput.
2. **Brain abnormalities**
   - Thinning/atrophy of cerebral cortex, especially white matter.
   - Sensorineural abnormalities.
   - Extrapyramidal manifestations (chorea, athetosis).
   - Subcortical calcifications.
3. **Musculoskeletal findings**
   - Contractures in lower/upper limbs → deformed limbs.
   - Lots of contractures → **arthrogryposis multiplex congenita**.
4. **Eyes**
   - Pigmentary retinal mottling.
   - Macular scarring.
   - Thinning of retina.
   - Coloboma/cataract.
   - Microphthalmia.

### Diagnosis

- **RT-PCR (real-time):** Investigation of choice.
- IgM testing; if IgM positive, confirm with plaque reduction neutralization testing (PRNT).

### Treatment and prevention

- No treatment.
- No prophylaxis/vaccine.
- Avoid exposure; prevent travel to endemic areas.

### Page 159

# 23. COVID-19 in Children

- MOHFW (Ministry of Health & Family Welfare), Government of India, updated the guidelines in **January 2022**.

## COVID-appropriate behaviour

- 3Ws & 2Vs.
- Watch your distance >2 metres.
- Wear mask.
- Wash your hands frequently.
- Ventilation: open-space ventilation decreases spread more than a poorly ventilated place.
- COVID-19 vaccination for children of **12–18 years** had been started.
- In India, vaccination for **15–18 years** started on **3 January 2022** and for **12–14 years** on **16 March 2022**.

## COVID-19 symptoms in children

### Features

- Fever, cough, rhinorrhoea.
- Sore throat/irritation: seen commonly with the **Omicron** variant.
- Myalgia/headache/malaise.
- Diarrhoea.
- Loss of sense of smell/taste: seen commonly with the **Delta** variant.

COVID-19 is mostly mild/asymptomatic in children. Moderate/severe disease is rare in children.

---

## Severity grading

| Feature | Asymptomatic (incidental detection or history of contact) | Mild (URTI) | Moderate | Severe |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Respiratory rate | Normal | Normal | Increased | Increased |
| SpO₂ in room air | ≥94% | ≥94% | 90–93% | <90% |
| Danger signs/symptoms | Absent | Absent | Absent | Usually present |

### Danger signs & symptoms

- Grunting
- Lethargy
- Seizures
- Somnolence
- Severe chest retractions

- Asymptomatic patients are children in whom COVID-19 was an incidental finding or who have a history of contact with a COVID-19 patient.
- Mild COVID refers to upper respiratory tract symptoms.

### Criteria for fast breathing

| Age | Respiratory rate |
|---|---:|
| <2 months | >60/min |
| 2–12 months | >50/min |
| 1–5 years | >40/min |
| >5 years | >30/min |

---

## Investigations

- CT chest is not indicated in paediatric COVID.
- Exception: if the child is terminally ill.

| Severity of disease | Investigations |
|---|---|
| Asymptomatic | Not required |
| Mild | Not required |
| Moderate | CBC, ESR, chest X-ray, blood glucose |
| Severe | CBC, ESR, chest X-ray, blood glucose, CRP/ferritin, D-dimer, LFT/RFT for multi-organ dysfunction |

---

## Management

|  | Asymptomatic | Mild | Moderate | Severe |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Place of care | Home isolation | Home isolation | Ward in a COVID-19 hospital or DCHC (dedicated COVID health centre) | ICU / high-dependency unit of COVID-19 hospital |
| Treatment | No specific treatment | Symptomatic treatment, e.g. paracetamol for fever or warm saline for throat irritation | O₂ inhalation (goal SpO₂ 94–98%), fluid & electrolyte balance; steroids only if progressive disease course is noted; antimicrobials if strong evidence of bacterial infection | O₂ support, fluid & electrolytes, corticosteroids |

## Severe COVID management

- Patients need to be admitted in ICU or high-dependency unit (HDU) of COVID-19 hospital.
- **O₂ support:** SpO₂ 94–98%.
- Fluid and electrolytes.
- **Corticosteroids:** Indicated in all severe cases.
- Steroids should be used at the **right time, right dose, right duration**.

### Steroid therapy

#### Right time

- Steroids are avoided in the first **3–5 days** after start of symptoms as they prolong viral shedding.

#### Right dose

- **Dexamethasone:** 0.15 mg/kg to a maximum dose of 6 mg once daily.
- **Methylprednisolone:** 0.75 mg/kg to a maximum dose of 30 mg/day.

#### Duration

- Steroids need to be given for **5–7 days** and then tapered over **10–14 days**.

### Anticoagulants

- Not usually indicated in management of all COVID cases.
- Indicated only in cases of confirmed thrombosis with elevated D-dimer.
- **Low-molecular-weight heparin (LMWH, enoxaparin):** 1 mg/kg twice daily.

### Antimicrobials

- Indicated only when there is strong clinical evidence/suspicion of bacterial infection.
- Exception: if the child has septic shock, antimicrobials can be given.

### Antivirals/monoclonal antibodies

- Remdesivir and favipiravir were listed as not recommended because efficacy was not known in children.

---

## Multisystem inflammatory syndrome in children (MIS-C)

- Usually presents **2–4 weeks after acute COVID**.

### Risk factors

- Age <1 year.
- Comorbidities:
  - Asthma
  - Chronic lung disease
- Obesity.
- Children with immunodeficiency.

### Diagnostic criteria (WHO)

- Children and adolescents **0–18 years** of age with fever (temperature **≥38°C**) for **≥3 days**.
- Any of the following should be present:
  1. Evidence of mucocutaneous inflammation like rash / bilateral non-purulent conjunctivitis.
  2. Hypotension or shock.
  3. Myocardial dysfunction, myocarditis, valvulitis, coronary involvement (e.g. aneurysms), elevated troponin.
  4. Evidence of coagulopathy (elevated D-dimers).
  5. Evidence of severe gastrointestinal involvement (uncontrolled abdominal pain/diarrhoea).

### Additional findings

- Increased inflammatory markers:
  - CRP >5 mg/dL.
  - ESR >40 mm/h.
  - Procalcitonin.
- Evidence of recent COVID-19:
  - RT-PCR positive, or
  - Contact with a confirmed COVID-19 case.
- No evidence of serious bacterial infection.

### Management of MIS-C

- Hospitalisation is required in ICU.
- **Methylprednisolone:** 2 mg/kg/day; tapered over **2–3 weeks**.
- **IV immunoglobulin (IVIG):** Indicated in all cases of MIS-C.
  - Dose: **2 g/kg** as a slow IV infusion.
  - Maximum dosage: **100 g**.

---

# Neurology

### Page 164

## 24. Congenital Anomalies and Hydrocephalus

# CONGENITAL ANOMALIES AND HYDROCEPHALUS

## Anomalies of the nervous system

### Neural tube defects (NTD)

- Most common congenital malformations of the CNS.
- Incidence: **1.5 per 1000 live births**.
- Increased risk in subsequent pregnancies: **3.5–4 per 1000 live births**.

### Neural tube development

- Neural tube develops from neuroectoderm between approximately **day 24 and day 28 of gestation**.
- Neural plate → neural groove → neuropore → neural tube.
- Anterior neuropore gives rise to structures like the brain; posterior neuropore gives rise to the spinal cord.
- Basic defect in NTD: **failure of closure of the neuropore**.

### Risk factors

- Maternal deficiency of **folic acid**.
- Maternal use of **valproate and carbamazepine** during pregnancy.
- Maternal intake of alcohol during pregnancy.
- Maternal overt/uncontrolled diabetes.
- Genetic defects, including mutation of the **MTHFR gene**.

## Types of neural tube defects

- **Cranial/upper NTD**
- **Caudal NTD** — more common.
  - **Spina bifida occulta** (hidden).
  - **Spina bifida cystica/aperta**, of two types:
    - Meningocele
    - Meningomyelocele

### Spina bifida occulta

- Some part of the posterior vertebral arch is missing.
- The defect is hidden because the skin covers it and it is not visible externally.
- Caused by failure of formation of the posterior vertebral arches.
- Common site: **L5–S1**.
- Usually asymptomatic in children because the spinal cord and meninges remain well covered.

#### Clues for detection

- Tuft of hair over the defect.
- Swellings such as a lipoma or hemangioma over the defect.

### Spina bifida cystica / aperta

#### Meningocele

- Defect in the vertebral arches with protrusion of the meninges through the defect, forming a cyst.
- More common in the **lumbosacral region**.
- CSF-filled lesion.
- Shows transillumination positivity.
- Soft and fluctuant mass.

#### Meningomyelocele

- Spinal nerve roots also protrude through the defect in the vertebral bone.
- Usually associated with neurological deficits.
- Lower limbs: decreased muscle tone.
- Severe cases: flaccidity of lower limbs.
- Deep tendon reflexes may be absent.
- Bowel and bladder incontinence may occur.
- Associated with **Arnold–Chiari type II malformation**.
- Seen in approximately **80%** of cases.
- Hence, hydrocephalus is commonly associated.

## Cranial neural tube defects

### Anencephaly

- Absence of brain/cerebral tissue.
- Absent skull vault (calvarium).
- Brainstem, cerebellum and spinal cord remain intact.

#### Subtypes

1. **Holoanencephaly** — whole brain is absent.
2. **Partial anencephaly / meroanencephaly** — part of the brain is present.

### Encephalocele

- Part of brain tissue protrudes through a skull defect and forms a sac/cavity.
- Most common location: **occipital region**.

### Iniencephaly

- Retroflexion of the head due to a defect in the occipital bone with fusion of the cervical spinal vertebrae.
- Antenatal ultrasound may show the **star-gazer posture**.

- Cranial NTDs are invariably associated with severe neurological deficits.

## Antenatal diagnosis of NTD

### Ultrasound

- Neural tube defects can be identified antenatally.
- **Anencephaly** is among the earliest NTDs to be identified on antenatal ultrasound.

### Maternal serum markers

- **Alpha-fetoprotein (AFP):** sensitive marker.
- **Acetylcholinesterase:** specific/best marker.

## Prevention of NTD

### Folic acid supplementation

- Usual dose: **0.4 mg/day (400 micrograms/day)**.
- If the mother has previously had an affected child: **4 mg/day (4000 micrograms/day)**.
- Periconceptional supplementation should begin **1 month before conception** and continue until **12 weeks of gestation**.

## Neuronal migration defects

### Lissencephaly

- “Lisso” refers to smooth.
- Smooth brain with absent/simplified gyri and sulci.
- Also called **agyria**.

### Schizencephaly

- “Schisis” refers to split.
- Split-brain defect with clefts in the cerebral hemispheres.

### Porencephaly

- Presence of cystic spaces in the brain due to cerebral infarction occurring in intrauterine life.

## Agenesis of corpus callosum

- Corpus callosum is the major interhemispheric connection.
- Defect results from injury/insult to the commissural plate.

### Features

- Microcephaly.
- Low IQ/intellectual disability.
- Increased risk of refractory seizures.

### Imaging

- Normal lateral ventricles: **bow-tie appearance**.
- In agenesis of the corpus callosum: parallel lateral ventricles, producing the **racing-car/moose-head appearance**.

## Aicardi syndrome

- X-linked; lethal in males.

### Triad

- Agenesis of corpus callosum.
- **Chorioretinal lacunae:** punched-out lesions in the retina/choroid.
- **Infantile spasms** with EEG showing hypsarrhythmia.

### Other abnormalities

- Coloboma of the optic disc.
- Retinal pits.
- Vertebral defects, e.g. hemivertebrae.

## Holoprosencephaly

- Defect: failure of division of the forebrain.
- Genetic defect involving the **Sonic hedgehog (SHH) gene**.

### Midline facial defects

- Fused eyes: **cyclopia**.
- Malformed nose: **proboscis**.
- Midline cleft lip/palate.

# HYDROCEPHALUS

- Increase in CSF volume results in enlargement of the ventricles.
- Normal CSF volume:
  - Approximately **50 mL in infants**.
  - Approximately **150 mL in adults**.

## CSF pathway

- CSF is formed by ultrafiltration/secretion at the **choroid plexus**.
- Lateral ventricle → **foramen of Monro** → third ventricle → **aqueduct of Sylvius** → fourth ventricle → foramen of **Magendie/Luschka** → subarachnoid space.
- CSF is absorbed through the **arachnoid villi**.

## Causes of hydrocephalus

### Non-obstructive / communicating hydrocephalus

#### Increased production

- Choroid plexus papilloma.

#### Reduced absorption

- Blood in the subarachnoid space, e.g. subarachnoid hemorrhage, interferes with CSF absorption.
- Pus in the subarachnoid space, as in meningitis.

### Obstructive / non-communicating hydrocephalus

- More common type.
- Overall most common cause of **congenital hydrocephalus: aqueductal stenosis**.

#### Other associated anomalies

- Dandy–Walker malformation.
- Arnold–Chiari type II malformation.
- Vein of Galen malformation.

## Dandy–Walker malformation

- Defect in development of the roof of the fourth ventricle.
- Leads to expansion of the posterior fossa.

### Features

- Prominent/bulging occiput.
- Transillumination may be positive.
- Cerebellar hypoplasia causing ataxia, dysarthria, nystagmus and vertigo.

### Brain imaging

- Characteristic fluid-filled lesion in the posterior fossa.
- Enlarged ventricles.

## Arnold–Chiari malformation

### Type I

- Herniation of cerebellar tonsils through the foramen magnum.
- Not associated with hydrocephalus.
- Seen predominantly in adults.
- Presents with headache and neck pain.

### Type II

- Associated with hydrocephalus.
- Herniation of the cerebellar vermis, pons, medulla and fourth ventricle.
- Kinking/obstruction of the fourth ventricle causes hydrocephalus.
- Associated with meningomyelocele in approximately **80%** of cases.

## Vein of Galen malformation

- Persistence of the **median prosencephalic vein**, precursor of the vein of Galen.
- Abnormal dilatation produces a vascular malformation.
- May cause obstruction of the CSF pathway and hydrocephalus.
- Increased venous pressure in the brain may produce a **cranial bruit** and prominent carotid pulsations.
- High-pressure venous blood returning to the right atrium can result in **cardiac failure**.

### Imaging

- MRI/MR angiogram (venogram) demonstrates the dilated vein of Galen.

## Clinical features of hydrocephalus

### Macrocephaly

- Large size of the head.
- Head circumference **> +2 standard deviations** for age.

### Features of raised intracranial pressure

- Rapidly increasing head circumference.
- Dilated scalp veins.
- Bulging anterior fontanelle.
- Wide separation of sutures.
- **Sunset sign:** eyeballs are rotated downward and the upper sclera is visible.
- Dilatation of the suprapineal recess.

### Cracked-pot / Macewen sign

- After 3 months of age, gentle tapping on the skull produces a sound resembling the cracking of a pot.

## Investigations

- **Screening:** neurosonogram/USG through the fontanelle.
- **Definitive/gold-standard investigation:** MRI brain.

## Treatment of hydrocephalus

### Drugs

- **Acetazolamide** (carbonic anhydrase inhibitor) to reduce CSF production.
- Osmotic diuretics may also be used in selected situations.

### Surgical treatment

- **VP shunt:** ventriculo-peritoneal shunt.
- **VA shunt:** ventriculo-atrial shunt.

### Complication of VP shunting

- Infection/shunt meningitis, commonly due to **coagulase-negative Staphylococci**, e.g. *Staphylococcus epidermidis*.

### Shunt tapping

- A catheter/reservoir system may be tapped to obtain CSF when shunt-associated infection is suspected.
- Preferred over lumbar puncture in a patient with a VP shunt when indicated.

### Aqueductal stenosis

- **Endoscopic third ventriculostomy (ETV)** can be performed.
- A communication is created between the third ventricle and the subarachnoid space, allowing CSF to bypass the obstruction.

### Page 174

## 25. Seizure Disorders and Epilepsy in Children

# SEIZURE DISORDERS AND EPILEPSY IN CHILDREN

## Definitions

- **Seizure:** abnormal, excessive neuronal electrical activity producing motor, sensory, autonomic or behavioral manifestations.
- **Epilepsy:** recurrent unprovoked seizures.
- Seizures are detected/characterized by **electroencephalography (EEG)** when appropriate.

### Common causes/types mentioned

- Most common cause of seizures in neonates: **hypoxic-ischaemic encephalopathy (HIE)**.
- Most common cause in older children: **febrile seizures**.
- Most common type in older children: **generalized tonic-clonic seizure (GTCS)**.

## Febrile seizures

- Seizure associated with fever in a child from approximately **6 months to 5 years** of age.
- Fever: approximately **>38.4°C (101°F)**.
- No evidence of CNS infection or electrolyte/metabolic abnormality.
- No history of preceding afebrile seizures or major preceding CNS insult.

### Types

#### Simple febrile seizure

- Generalized tonic-clonic seizure.
- Lasts **<15 minutes**.
- Does not recur within **24 hours**.

#### Complex febrile seizure

Any of the following:

- Focal seizure.
- Duration **>15 minutes**.
- Recurs within **24 hours**.

### Other definitions

- **Febrile status epilepticus:** prolonged febrile seizure/status lasting approximately 30 minutes or more.
- **Febrile seizures plus (FS+):** febrile seizures occurring beyond the usual age range and/or afebrile seizures.
- **GEFS+:** genetic epilepsy with febrile seizures plus; commonly familial with autosomal-dominant inheritance.

## Investigations in febrile seizure

- Simple febrile seizures generally do not require routine investigations.
- Investigations are considered with complex febrile seizures or febrile status epilepticus.
- Blood glucose/calcium and serum electrolytes when clinically indicated, especially in febrile status.
- Urine examination may be considered in infants without an obvious focus of infection.
- **MRI:** considered in complex febrile seizures and febrile status when indicated.
- **EEG:** may be performed in selected children with complex/recurrent seizures.
- **Lumbar puncture:** when there is suspicion of meningitis/CNS infection.

### Lumbar puncture considerations

Consider LP in:

1. Young/unimmunized children, especially those not fully immunized against *Streptococcus pneumoniae* and *Haemophilus influenzae*.
2. Children pretreated with antibiotics.
3. Children with clinical suspicion of meningitis or febrile status epilepticus.

## Treatment of febrile seizures

### Control fever

- **Paracetamol:** approximately 10–15 mg/kg/dose at appropriate intervals.
- Tepid sponging when required.

### Control seizures

- If the seizure lasts **>5 minutes**, administer an anticonvulsant.
- Intravenous benzodiazepines: **lorazepam/midazolam**, approximately 0.1 mg/kg/dose when appropriate.
- Alternative routes:
  - Hospital: intranasal/buccal midazolam.
  - Community: rectal diazepam.

### Prevention of recurrence

#### Intermittent prophylaxis

- Oral **clobazam** for the first few days of fever, or intermittent oral diazepam.

##### Indications

- Recurrent febrile seizures.
- Significant parental anxiety.
- Residence far from a medical facility.

#### Continuous prophylaxis

- Daily **sodium valproate** in selected high-risk children.
- Consider in frequent complex febrile seizures, febrile seizures with neurodevelopmental delay, and FS+/GEFS+ as appropriate.

## Absence seizures

- Also called **petit mal seizures**.
- Typical age of onset: **>5 years**, usually around 5–8 years.

### Features during the seizure

- Vacant stare.
- Unresponsiveness.
- Eye fluttering.
- Brief duration, usually a few seconds and generally <30 seconds.
- May be precipitated by hyperventilation.

### Diagnosis

- EEG: **3-Hz spike-and-wave pattern**.
- Hyperventilation is often used during EEG to provoke typical absence seizures.

### Treatment

- **Sodium valproate**.
- Alternative: **lamotrigine**.

### Atypical absence seizures

- Myoclonic component may be present.
- EEG may show slower spike-and-wave activity.
- Treated with sodium valproate as appropriate.

## Juvenile myoclonic epilepsy (JME)

- Typical onset in adolescence, approximately **12–18 years**.
- Polygenic inheritance.

### Genetic associations mentioned

- Chloride-channel related genes.
- Calcium-channel/calcium-sensing receptor related genes.

### Features

- **Myoclonus:** sudden, brief, shock-like contractions.
- Consciousness is usually preserved.
- Can have other generalized seizure patterns, including GTCS and absence seizures.

### Precipitating factors

- Sleep deprivation.
- Alcohol consumption.

### Diagnosis

- EEG: generalized **polyspike-and-wave** pattern.

### Treatment

- **Sodium valproate**.
- Alternatives: **lamotrigine/levetiracetam**.

## Epilepsy syndromes

- Severe conditions with brain abnormalities predisposing to low IQ, developmental delay and difficult-to-control seizures.

### Idiopathic/cryptogenic

- Previously normal child.
- Tend to have better outcome.

### Symptomatic

- Associated brain disorder.
- Worse outcome.

## Infantile spasms / West syndrome

### Features

- Spasms: sudden, sustained contraction of muscles.
- Jack-knife or Salaam-spell seizures.
- Repeated flexion of the neck/trunk; extension of the neck may also occur.

### Triad

1. Onset **<1 year of age**.
2. Low IQ/developmental delay.
3. EEG: **hypsarrhythmia** (high-voltage, multifocal spikes with disorganized background).

### Treatment

- **ACTH** is a first-line treatment.
- **Vigabatrin** is especially useful when infantile spasms are associated with **tuberous sclerosis**.

## Dravet syndrome / severe myoclonic epilepsy in infancy

- Very severe form of epilepsy with onset during infancy, usually in the first year.
- Associated with **SCN1A** defect involving the alpha-1 subunit of a sodium channel.

### Clinical features

- Febrile seizures beginning in the first year of life.
- Seizures may be focal/unilateral and clonic.
- Later development of afebrile seizures.
- Difficult to treat.

### Treatment

- **Stiripentol** is an important treatment option.

## Lennox–Gastaut syndrome

- Onset: approximately **2–10 years**.

### Triad

1. Developmental delay.
2. Multiple refractory seizure types.
3. EEG: typical **1.5–2.5 Hz spike-and-wave** pattern.

- Drug of choice/commonly used: **sodium valproate**.

## Status epilepticus

- Prolonged seizure activity, classically **≥30 minutes**, with treatment initiated much earlier in practice.

### Time frames

- **T1:** 5 minutes or more — impending status epilepticus; this is the point after which treatment is initiated to control the seizure.
- **T2:** 30 minutes or more — established status epilepticus, with increased risk of neurological sequelae.

## Management of status epilepticus

### First 5 minutes

- Establish/secure:
  - Airway
  - Breathing — provide oxygen when indicated.
  - Circulation.
- Turn the head to one side to reduce aspiration risk.
- Establish IV access.
- Check blood glucose.
- If hypoglycaemia is present, give approximately **5 mL/kg of 10% dextrose** as a bolus, according to local protocol.

### After 5 minutes

- Start benzodiazepines.
- IV lorazepam/midazolam approximately **0.1 mg/kg/dose**.
- Reassess after about 5 minutes and repeat when appropriate.

### Persistent seizure

- **Fosphenytoin** is preferred because of a lower adverse-effect burden than phenytoin in the notes.
- Dose approximately **20 mg/kg** as an infusion.

### Ongoing seizure activity

- IV **sodium valproate or levetiracetam**, approximately **20–40 mg/kg**, as appropriate.

### Further persistence

- IV **phenobarbital**, approximately **20 mg/kg**, as an infusion.

## Refractory status epilepticus

- If seizures persist after the above therapies, induce a barbiturate/anaesthetic coma with continuous infusion of an appropriate agent such as:
  - Midazolam
  - Thiopentone
  - Phenobarbitone

- If seizures still persist, this is **super-refractory status epilepticus**.
- Other therapies such as IV immunoglobulin may be considered in selected refractory cases according to the underlying cause.
