### Page 183

## 26. Cerebral Palsy and CNS Infections

# CEREBRAL PALSY AND CNS INFECTIONS

## Cerebral palsy

- Most common cause of motor disability in childhood.
- Permanent, non-progressive damage due to a static (one-time) insult/injury to the developing brain, resulting in abnormalities of **tone, posture and movement**.
- Irreversible condition.
- Also known as **static encephalopathy**.

### Etiology

- Intrauterine infection (TORCH).
- Prematurity/low birth weight.
- Trauma and birth trauma.
- Teratogen/drug exposure during pregnancy.
- Toxin-mediated intracranial damage.
- Intracranial hemorrhage.

- More than **50%** of cases are due to prenatal etiology; less than **10%** are due to perinatal etiology .

## Clinical presentation

### Global developmental delay

Delay in at least 2 developmental domains, including:

- Gross motor milestones.
- Fine motor milestones.
- Language milestones.
- Social milestones.

### Other findings

- Persistence of neonatal/primitive reflexes because of immature brain development.
  - Example: Moro reflex persisting beyond about 4–6 months.
- Development of spasticity.
  - Clenched fist.
  - **Cortical thumb:** thumb held over the palm; persistence beyond about 4 months is abnormal.
- **Scissoring of legs** due to lower-limb spasticity.
- Abnormal posture.

## Comorbid conditions

- Intellectual disability/low IQ.
- Increased incidence of seizures.
- Microcephaly due to poor brain growth.
- Speech and swallowing abnormalities; aspiration can cause death.
- Increased risk of blindness/deafness.
- Increased risk of behavioural problems.

## Classification of cerebral palsy

### Based on limbs affected

1. **Spastic quadriplegia**
2. **Spastic diplegia**
3. **Spastic hemiplegia**

### Dyskinetic type

- Also called extrapyramidal variety.
- Findings resemble extrapyramidal damage:
  - Dystonia/dyskinesia/rigidity.
  - Chorea/athetosis.
- Commonly due to birth asphyxia affecting the **basal ganglia**.
- Intellectual function may be relatively preserved.

### Ataxic type

- Due to cerebellar damage.
- Features include ataxia, slurring of speech, nystagmus and incoordination.

### Mixed type

- Spastic and extrapyramidal features occur together.

## Spastic quadriplegia

- Overall most common type in India in the notes.
- All four limbs are affected.
- Usually seen in term babies with a history of birth asphyxia.
- Severe type.
- High incidence of comorbidities and poor outcome.

### MRI findings

- Immediate neonatal period: **parasagittal infarction**.
- Older children: **multicystic encephalomalacia**.

## Spastic diplegia

- Common type of cerebral palsy in the Indian population.
- Seen commonly in preterm babies with a history of birth asphyxia.
- During development, **commando crawling** may be seen: the infant uses the trunk and upper limbs while dragging the lower limbs.
- IQ is usually normal and prognosis is relatively good.
- MRI finding: **periventricular leukomalacia (PVL)**.
- White matter surrounding the ventricles is affected.

## Spastic hemiplegia

- One half of the body is affected, with upper and lower limb involvement on the same side.
- Usually due to a vascular insult causing perinatal stroke.
- Early hand preference may be seen before 1 year.
- Because one side is weak, the opposite hand is preferentially used.
- MRI may show focal changes, including **porencephalic cysts** due to infarction.

## Management of cerebral palsy

- Permanent damage cannot be reversed; treatment is symptomatic and multidisciplinary.
- Team may include paediatrician, physiotherapist, ENT surgeon and ophthalmologist.

### Spasticity

- Diazepam.
- Baclofen.
- Botulinum toxin type A.

### Contractures

- Tendon-release procedures.

### Dystonia

- Trihexyphenidyl.
- Levodopa in selected patients.

# CNS INFECTIONS

## Bacterial meningitis

### Etiology by age

- **0–2 months:** Gram-negative organisms such as *Klebsiella* and *E. coli*.
- **2–24 months:** *Streptococcus pneumoniae* and *Haemophilus influenzae*.
- **2–12 years:** *Streptococcus pneumoniae* and *Neisseria meningitidis*.

- Overall, the most common bacterial cause after the neonatal period in the notes: **Streptococcus pneumoniae**.

### Special situations

- Complement system defects (C5–C8): **meningococci** are particularly important.
- T-lymphocyte defects (e.g. DiGeorge syndrome, SCID, HIV): **Listeria monocytogenes** is highlighted in the notes.
- Splenic dysfunction/asplenia due to chronic hemolytic anaemia: increased risk from encapsulated organisms, particularly *S. pneumoniae*, *N. meningitidis* and *H. influenzae*.
- CSF leak (rhinorrhoea/otorrhoea): *S. pneumoniae* is a common cause.

## Clinical features of meningitis

- Fever.
- Generalized seizures/GTCS.
- Irritability.
- Headache.
- Photophobia.
- Signs of raised intracranial pressure, including bulging anterior fontanelle in infants.

> Suspect meningitis in a highly irritable child <1 year old with fever and a bulging anterior fontanelle.

### Signs of meningeal irritation

- Neck stiffness/neck rigidity.
- **Brudzinski sign:** passive neck flexion causes reflex flexion of the hips and knees.
- **Kernig sign:** hip and knee are flexed; passive extension of the knee produces resistance/pain.
- Meningeal signs are uncommon in children younger than about 2 years.

## Investigations in suspected meningitis

- **Gold standard:** CSF analysis by lumbar puncture.
- In a patient with a ventriculoperitoneal shunt and suspected shunt-associated meningitis, **shunt tapping** is performed.
- If lumbar puncture is contraindicated because of raised intracranial pressure and risk of herniation, neuroimaging is obtained first.
- CT may show meningeal enhancement.
- CSF culture can be performed to isolate the organism.
- CSF PCR may be used to detect bacterial nucleic acid/antigens depending on the test available.

## Complications of bacterial meningitis

- Most common acute complication: **seizures**.
- Subdural effusion/empyema.
- Hydrocephalus.
- Brain abscess, especially in immunodeficient children.
- **Sensorineural hearing loss**, particularly with *S. pneumoniae* and *H. influenzae* infection.

## Treatment of bacterial meningitis

- Empirical IV antibiotic therapy according to age and local protocol.
- Third-generation cephalosporins such as **cefotaxime/ceftriaxone** are used in appropriate age groups.
- Add **vancomycin** when resistant pneumococcal disease is a concern.
- Duration is approximately **10 days** for many bacterial meningitis syndromes; neonates generally require a longer course, around **3 weeks**, .

## Tuberculous meningitis

### Pathophysiology

- Tuberculous infection can involve the meninges and produce inflammatory exudates at the base of the brain.
- Progression through stages is described in the notes; higher stages are associated with poorer prognosis.
- Neurological sequelae may include obstructive hydrocephalus and cranial nerve deficits.

### CSF findings

- Raised opening pressure.
- Lymphocytic pleocytosis.
- Protein may be markedly elevated.
- Glucose is reduced.
- Classic **cobweb clot** may form on standing.

### Diagnosis

- CSF testing including smear/culture and nucleic-acid amplification tests such as **CBNAAT/GeneXpert** when available.
- Neuroimaging may show basal exudates and hydrocephalus.

### Treatment

- Anti-tubercular treatment according to pediatric TBM protocol.
- **Dexamethasone/corticosteroids** are used as adjunctive therapy to reduce inflammatory complications.

## Viral infections of the CNS

- Viral CNS infections can present as meningitis or encephalitis.
- Typical CSF pattern in viral infection:
  - Lymphocyte predominance.
  - Protein normal or mildly raised, sometimes up to about 200 mg/dL.
  - CSF glucose usually normal.

### Diagnosis

- CSF nucleic-acid testing/PCR is an important diagnostic tool in suspected viral encephalitis.
- CSF antibody testing may also be useful for selected viruses.

## Japanese encephalitis

- A viral encephalitis caused by **Japanese encephalitis virus**.
- Diagnosis is supported by detection of specific antibodies in appropriate clinical settings.
- Treatment is mainly supportive.

## Brain abscess

- Brain abscess may occur in children with conditions predisposing to hematogenous spread or contiguous spread.

### Predisposing factors

- Cyanotic congenital heart disease.
- Chronic ear/sinus infections.
- Other systemic infections.
- Immunodeficiency.

### Location

- **Parietal lobe abscess** may occur in association with congenital cyanotic heart disease.
- **Temporal lobe abscess** may be associated with chronic otitis/mastoid infection.

### Organisms

- Aerobic and anaerobic organisms may be involved, including streptococci and other organisms depending on the source.

### Treatment

- Prolonged IV antibiotics directed at the likely organisms.
- Neurosurgical aspiration/drainage may be required.
- Lack of response or multiloculated lesions may require more definitive surgical intervention.

---

### Page 194

## 27. Neuromuscular Disorders

# NEUROMUSCULAR DISORDERS

## Classification

### Motor neuron disorders

- Disorders of the anterior horn cell/motor neuron.
- Example: **Spinal muscular atrophy (SMA)**.

### Peripheral nerve disorders

- Disorders of the motor nerve/peripheral nerves.
- Example: **Guillain–Barré syndrome (GBS)**.

### Neuromuscular junction disorders

- Disorders of transmission at the neuromuscular junction.
- Example: **Myasthenia gravis**.

### Primary muscle disorders

- Disorders affecting skeletal muscle.
- Examples: muscular dystrophies.

## Spinal muscular atrophy (SMA)

- Usually **autosomal recessive**.
- Due to defective **SMN** protein resulting from mutation/deletion involving the **SMN1 gene**.
- SMN protein is essential for survival of motor neurons.
- Deficiency causes degeneration of motor neurons in the anterior horn of the spinal cord.

### Classification based on age of onset

- Prenatal onset → severe disease and possible fetal death.
- **Type I:** 0–6 months.
- **Type II:** approximately 6–18 months.
- **Type III:** later onset, with children achieving independent sitting/walking to varying degrees.

### Clinical features of classic severe SMA

- Death of motor neurons causes weakness and hypotonia.
- Decreased muscle tone.
- Reduced spontaneous/fetal movements may be observed.
- Weakness is more marked proximally.
- Frog-leg posture.
- Head lag.
- Poor resistance to passive movements.
- Tongue fasciculations may be present.
- Decreased or absent deep tendon reflexes.
- Electromyography shows evidence of denervation.
- Sensation is relatively preserved.

### Treatment

- Symptomatic/supportive care.
- Disease-modifying therapies target **SMN protein production/function**, including therapies such as nusinersen and gene-replacement approaches in appropriate settings.

## Guillain–Barré syndrome (GBS)

- **Acute inflammatory demyelinating polyradiculoneuropathy (AIDP)** is the classic form.
- Demyelination of peripheral nerves leads to slowed nerve conduction.
- Often occurs after a respiratory or gastrointestinal infection.

### Common associations

- *Campylobacter jejuni*.
- *Mycoplasma pneumoniae*.
- Other post-infectious triggers.

### Pathogenesis

- Immune-mediated/autimmune injury to peripheral nerve myelin.

### Features

- Acute onset of weakness.
- Often begins distally and progresses proximally.
- **Ascending paralysis** is typical.
- Hypotonia.
- Reduced/absent deep tendon reflexes.
- Weakness may involve respiratory muscles.
- Cranial nerve involvement may occur, especially facial weakness.
- Sensory symptoms such as tingling/paraesthesia can occur.
- Bladder symptoms are uncommon.
- Fever is usually absent once neurological symptoms begin.

### Investigations

- **Nerve conduction studies:** slowed conduction and features of demyelination in AIDP.
- **CSF:** albuminocytological dissociation — elevated protein with a relatively normal/low cell count.

### Treatment

- IV **immunoglobulin (IVIG)** or **plasmapheresis**.
- Treatment is used to reduce progression and shorten the course in appropriate patients.
- Corticosteroids are not effective in classic GBS.

### Variants

- **Acute motor axonal neuropathy (AMAN):** predominantly motor nerves are affected.
- Other axonal variants may also occur.

## Neuromuscular junction disorders

### Myasthenia gravis

- Autoimmune disorder of the neuromuscular junction.
- Usually involves antibodies directed against the **acetylcholine receptor (AChR)** or related components of the postsynaptic membrane.
- Antibody-mediated reduction in functional ACh receptors leads to fatigable weakness.

### Features of juvenile myasthenia gravis

- Fluctuating/fatigable weakness.
- Ocular muscles are commonly involved:
  - Ptosis.
  - Diplopia.
- Facial and bulbar weakness may occur.
- Weakness can involve respiratory muscles, producing respiratory failure in severe cases.
- Symptoms worsen with activity and improve with rest.

### Investigation

- Repetitive nerve stimulation may show a **decremental response**.
- Other electrodiagnostic tests can support the diagnosis.
- AChR antibodies may be detected in appropriate cases.
- Chest imaging may be performed to assess the thymus when indicated.

### Treatment

- Acetylcholinesterase inhibitors such as **pyridostigmine**.
- Immunotherapy, including corticosteroids and other steroid-sparing agents, when indicated.
- IVIG or plasmapheresis may be used for significant exacerbations/crisis.
- Thymectomy may be considered in selected patients.

### Neonatal myasthenia gravis

- Transient condition due to passage of maternal antibodies across the placenta.
- Occurs in a minority of infants born to mothers with myasthenia gravis.
- Usually begins within the first few days after birth.

#### Features

- Feeding difficulty.
- Weak cry.
- Facial weakness.
- Bulbar/respiratory muscle weakness.
- Usually complete recovery within several weeks.

## Muscular dystrophies in children

### Duchenne muscular dystrophy (DMD)

- **X-linked recessive** disorder.
- Almost exclusively affects males; females are typically carriers.
- Due to mutation/deletion in the **DMD gene** on the short arm of the X chromosome.
- DMD gene encodes **dystrophin**, an important protein for muscle membrane stability.
- Frameshift/nonsense mutations usually produce severe Duchenne disease; in-frame mutations may produce the milder Becker phenotype.

### Clinical features

- Onset usually around **2–5 years**.
- Delay in attainment of motor milestones may occur.
- Progressive **proximal muscle weakness**.
- Difficulty running, jumping and climbing stairs.
- **Calf pseudohypertrophy**.
- **Gowers sign:** child uses the hands to push on the thighs and rise from the floor because of proximal muscle weakness.
- Respiratory muscle involvement develops with disease progression.
- **Cardiomyopathy** is an important complication.

### Investigations

- Serum **creatine kinase (CK)** is markedly elevated.
- Genetic testing confirms the diagnosis.

### Treatment

- No curative therapy.
- **Corticosteroids** slow progression and preserve function.
- Supportive physiotherapy and respiratory/cardiac care are important.
- Disease-specific molecular therapies may be considered in selected patients depending on the mutation and availability.

## Other muscular dystrophies

### Facioscapulohumeral muscular dystrophy

- Usually an inherited muscular dystrophy with characteristic distribution.

#### Typical presentation

- Facial weakness.
- Inability to fully close the eyes.
- Difficulty whistling/smiling.
- Scapular winging.
- Predominant upper-limb involvement with relative preservation of some forearm muscles.
- "Popeye arm" appearance may be seen.
- Lower-limb involvement may occur.

### Limb-girdle muscular dystrophy

- Group of muscular dystrophies affecting proximal limb-girdle muscles.
- May have autosomal-recessive or autosomal-dominant inheritance depending on subtype.
- Different subtypes result from different protein defects, including **calpain**-related disease.

## Pattern of weakness in muscular dystrophies

- Upper limb: predominantly proximal muscles.
- Lower limb: predominantly proximal muscles.

---

### Page 206

# Gastrointestinal Disorders in Children

### GI anomalies

#### Esophageal Atresia

Discontinuity in the esophagus because part of the esophagus is underdeveloped/not formed.

### Associations of Esophageal Atresia

- **TEF:** Tracheoesophageal fistula.
  - Seen in 85% of cases of esophageal atresia.
- **VACTERL association:**
  1. Vertebral defects
  2. Anorectal malformation
  3. Cardiac defects
  4. Tracheoesophageal fistula (TEF)
  5. Renal anomalies
  6. Limb defects

### Clinical presentation of esophageal atresia

- Recurrent episodes of vomiting (non-bilious).
- Profuse drooling of frothy saliva in newborn.
  - Not able to swallow.
- During antenatal period, mother has a history of **polyhydramnios**.
  - During intrauterine life, the fetus is unable to swallow the amniotic fluid.

**Complication:** Repeated vomiting resulting in aspiration of feed, causing **aspiration pneumonitis**.

### Classification of esophageal atresia with TEF

Gross classification involves 5 types of tracheoesophageal fistula:

- **Type A:** Isolated esophageal atresia.
- **Type B:** Proximal TEF with distal esophageal atresia.
- **Type C:** Proximal esophageal atresia with distal TEF — **most common type**.
- **Type D:** Proximal and distal TEF — least common.
- **Type E:** TEF without esophageal atresia — **H-shaped TEF**.
- **Type F:** Esophageal stenosis.

---

# Esophageal Atresia

- **Type D:** Proximal esophageal atresia with distal tracheoesophageal fistula.
- **Type E:** Also called an **H-shaped TEF**.

### Diagnosis

- During routine check-up of the baby, while inserting a nasogastric tube, resistance is felt.
- On X-ray, classical coiling of the tube can be seen.
- **Diagnostic finding:** Coiling of the NG tube in the upper esophagus.

### Treatment

Surgery is the definitive treatment:

- Posterolateral thoracotomy
- Repair of defects

## Idiopathic Hypertrophic Pyloric Stenosis

Hypertrophy of circular muscle fibres in the pyloric region, causing narrowing at the pylorus.

- More common in males than females (4:1 incidence).
- Risk of IHPS in a first-born male child is higher.
- Usage of macrolides (erythromycin), either by mother during pregnancy or by the newborn in the first 2 weeks, is associated with IHPS.

### Features

- Non-bilious, forceful projectile vomiting after feeds.
- Vomiting is never immediately after birth; it appears later.
- Epigastric olive-shaped swelling is usually noted after the vomiting episode.
- Visible gastric peristalsis due to increased force exerted by the smooth muscles to overcome the obstruction.
- Usual direction of peristalsis is from left to right.

---

### Esophageal Atresia

- **Type D:** Proximal esophageal atresia with distal tracheoesophageal fistula.
- **Type E:** Also called an **H-shaped TEF**.

### Association

**Jaundice:** Seen due to decreased activity of the enzyme **UDP-GT**, resulting in increased unconjugated bilirubin and jaundice.

**Icteropyloric syndrome:** Jaundice + IHPS.

### Investigations for IHPS

#### Contrast X-ray

1. **String sign:** Narrow pyloric channel is seen on contrast X-ray.
2. **Double-track sign.**
3. **Shoulder sign:** The hypertrophic muscle bulges into the gastric antrum.
4. **Mushrooming:** Bulging of the hypertrophic muscle into the 1st part of the duodenum.

**Investigation of choice:** USG.

- Thickness of the pyloric muscle should be **≥ 4 mm**.
- Length of the pyloric channel should be **≥ 16 mm**.

### Treatment

- Manage dehydration and electrolyte imbalances before surgery for IHPS.
- Hypochloremia with metabolic alkalosis and hypokalemia is seen as HCl is vomited out.
- Presence of hyponatremia in this patient initiates conservation of sodium by reabsorption. Kidney excretes H⁺ and K⁺; therefore, **paradoxical aciduria** is seen.
- **Hypokalemic hypochloremic metabolic alkalosis with paradoxical aciduria.**
- Dehydration: ½ NS + K⁺ or Ringer lactate used.
- Surgical treatment: **Ramstedt's pyloromyotomy**.

---

## Duodenal Atresia

Due to failure of recanalization of the duodenal lumen during development.

- Genetic association for duodenal atresia is seen in **Down syndrome**.
- **20%** of Down syndrome patients are associated with duodenal atresia.
- Other associations:
  - Trisomy 21.
  - Congenital heart defects are seen in 30% of cases.

### Features of duodenal atresia

- Bilious vomiting seen soon after birth.
- Abdomen: Not distended.
- No abdominal distention.
- Maternal history will be significant for **polyhydramnios**.

### Investigation

**X-ray: Double-bubble sign**

This sign is also seen in:

- Annular pancreas
- Duodenal web

### Diagnostic modality

- USG/CT imaging.

### Treatment

- Surgery: **Duodeno-duodenostomy**

## Meckel's Diverticulum

Most common GI anomaly, seen in approximately **2% of the population**.

- Remnant of omphalomesenteric duct or vitellointestinal duct.
- This is a **true diverticulum** (includes all layers of the GI tract).

### Features

- Present in about 2% of the population.

---

### Meckel's Diverticulum

- Length of Meckel's diverticulum is **2 inches (5 cm)**.
- It is **2 feet from the ileocecal junction** at the antimesenteric border of the ileum.
- Lined by ectopic/heterotopic mucosa:
  1. Gastric mucosa
  2. Pancreatic mucosa
- 4 times more common in males than in females.
- Most cases are asymptomatic.
- If the child has symptoms, they usually appear before 2 years of age and present as painless rectal bleeding or bleeding at the time of stool passage.
- Bleeding is due to deep ileal ulcers caused by ectopic gastric mucosa.
- Bleeding may be described as:
  - Currant-jelly stools
  - Melanotic stools
  - Maroon stools

### Other rare forms of presentation

1. Intestinal obstruction due to Meckel's diverticulum acting as a lead point in intussusception.
2. Volvulus due to twisting occurring by the connection between Meckel's diverticulum and the umbilicus.
3. **Littre's hernia:** When Meckel's diverticulum appears as a part of the hernia sac.

### Diagnosis

- **Meckel's scan:** Technetium-99m (⁹⁹ᵐTc) pertechnetate scintigraphy.

---

### Diagnostic investigation for Meckel's

**Meckel's scan:** Technetium-99m (⁹⁹ᵐTc) pertechnetate scintigraphy.

This radionucleotide has high affinity for gastric mucosa; therefore, stomach and ectopic Meckel's gastric mucosa show increased uptake.

### Treatment

Asymptomatic Meckel's is discovered as an incidental finding.

Treatment is done if:

1. The Meckel's diverticulum has a narrow mouth or narrow base, then diverticulectomy is done.
2. Patient is symptomatic.

## Hirschsprung Disease

Also known as **aganglionosis** (absence of ganglion cells in the intestinal submucosal and muscular layers — Meissner's and Auerbach's plexuses).

Occurs due to failure of migration of neurons during development and is known as a **neurocristopathy**.

Due to absence of ganglion cells, there is failure of relaxation of the intestine. Therefore, stool is unable to pass through the aganglionic segment of the intestine and gets stuck proximal to the affected area.

- More common in males (4:1 male:female).
- Most commonly affects the **rectosigmoid area (60% cases)**.
- Named as short-segment disease because only a short segment is affected.
- 15% cases appear as long-segment disease, where areas proximal to the sigmoid are affected (e.g. transverse colon).
- 5% of cases involve the entire colon (**total colonic disease**).

### Associations

- Down syndrome.
- Mutations in **RET proto-oncogene**.

---

## Hirschsprung Disease

### Clinical Features

Rectosigmoid area is always in a constricted state.

In newborns:

1. Delay in passage of meconium, even after 48 hours of birth.
2. Abdominal distention.
3. Rectum feels empty because of lack of stools.
4. Withdrawal of finger results in sudden expulsion of meconium.
5. In older children, constipation is seen.

### Investigation

- **Rectal manometry:** Absence of rectoanal inhibitory reflex.
- **Best investigation for diagnosis:** Rectal suction biopsy.

### Findings

1. Hypertrophy of nerve fibres.
2. Absence of ganglion cells.
3. Increased staining/activity of acetylcholinesterase.

### Treatment

Previously, resection and anastomosis was done.

Nowadays, **pull-through procedure** is done:

- Normal part of intestine is pulled through the aganglionic part and attached to the last part of intestine, i.e. anus.
- **Duhamel procedure**
- **Soave procedure** — preferred procedure.

In this procedure, the rectal mucosa is stripped while leaving behind the muscular part of rectum (**muscular cuff**). This muscular cuff provides support to the proximal part of intestine that is joined to the distal part.

---

### Page 213

# Diarrhoea

Diarrhoea is one of the important causes of under-5 mortality.

### Common definitions

| Type | Etiology | Duration |
|---|---|---|
| Acute diarrhoea | Infection | < 14 days |
| Persistent diarrhoea | Infection | > 14 days |
| Chronic diarrhoea | Non-infectious, often associated with malabsorption | > 14 days |

### Acute diarrhoea

It is defined as loose stools/change in consistency of stool:

- Child who used to pass normal-consistency stool now passes watery stool.
- Increase in frequency of stools >3/day.

### Etiology

- Commonest infections causing diarrhoea are viral and bacterial.
- Overall most common cause: **Rotavirus**.
- Other viruses:
  - Norwalk virus
  - Adenovirus
  - Astrovirus
  - Calicivirus
- Overall most common bacterial cause in children: **E. coli**.
- Other bacterial causes:
  - Salmonella
  - Shigella
  - Campylobacter
- Parasite-induced diarrhoea is predisposed in children with immunodeficiency.
- Most common parasite causing diarrhoea: **Giardia**.
- Other parasites:
  - Entamoeba
  - Cryptosporidium

---

### Dysentery

It is the presence of loose stools along with blood in stools.

- Almost always bacterial in origin.
- Most common bacteria associated with dysentery in children: **Shigella flexneri**.

A child with diarrhoea: most common cause is rotavirus, so there is no need to start antibiotics.

However, if the child has dysentery, it is almost always due to bacteria and should start antibiotics.

## Dehydration

First step in management of diarrhoea is to assess the child for dehydration.

Based on presence or absence and severity of dehydration, there are 3 types.

| Feature | No dehydration | Some dehydration | Severe dehydration |
|---|---|---|---|
| Fluid loss | 0–100 mL/kg | 100–200 mL/kg | >200 mL/kg |
| General condition | Active, alert | Irritable | Lethargic |
| Skin pinch | Fast, <1 sec | Slow, 1–2 sec | Very slow, >2 sec |

---

## Plan of Management

|  | Plan A | Plan B | Plan C |
|---|---|---|---|
| Replacement of ongoing loss | **<6 months:** 50 mL ORS or ¼ glass for every loose stool. **6 months–2 years:** 50–100 mL ORS or ¼–½ glass for every loose stool. **>2 years:** 200 mL ORS or 1 glass for every loose stool. | Rehydration: ORS **75 mL/kg over 4 hours**. Replacement of ongoing loss by ORS similar to Plan A. Maintenance/daily fluid requirements calculated by Holliday-Segar formula. | Rehydration with IV fluids. |
| Maintenance | — | Holliday-Segar formula (24 hours): **0–10 kg:** 100 mL/kg; **10–20 kg:** 1000 mL + 50 mL/kg for every kg above 10 kg; **>20 kg:** 1500 mL + 20 mL/kg for every kg above 20 kg. | — |

**Example for Plan B:** For a child of 22 kg, fluid required = 1500 + 40 = **1540 mL**.

### Plan C

1. Rehydration is with IV fluids (since child is lethargic):
   - 100 mL/kg fluid is given (30 mL/kg + 70 mL/kg).

| Age | 30 mL/kg | 70 mL/kg | Total |
|---|---|---|---|
| <1 year | 1 hour | 5 hours | 6 hours |
| >1 year | ½ hour | 2½ hours | 3 hours |

2. Replacement of ongoing loss.
3. Daily fluid maintenance.

---

Fluids are the cornerstone of treatment in diarrhoea.

- Fluid can be given orally as ORS or as IV fluids if the child is not able to take orally.
- In addition to fluids, **zinc** is recommended in the management of diarrhoea.
- Zinc helps in epithelialization of the GIT and decreases severity of loose stools as well as future recurrence of diarrhoea in a child.

### Zinc dose

- **<6 months:** 10 mg/day
- **>6 months:** 20 mg/day
- **Duration:** 14 days irrespective of age.

- No role of antimotility agents like **loperamide**.
- No recommendations for antisecretory agents like **racecadotril**.
- No guidelines/recommendation for probiotics.

## ORS

- WHO recommended low-osmolarity ORS is being used now.
- Total osmolarity = **245 mOsm/L**.

### ORS composition

| Component | Amount |
|---|---:|
| Sodium | 75 |
| Glucose | 75 |
| Potassium | 20 |
| Chloride | 65 |
| Citrate | 10 |

Both glucose and sodium are kept in the same concentration as in the intestine because the **SGLT1 sodium-glucose cotransporter** transports 1 molecule of sodium and 1 molecule of glucose.

Therefore, both are kept at a 1:1 ratio.

## Indications for antimicrobial treatment

- **Dysentery:** 3rd-generation cephalosporin, e.g. cefixime.
- Other medications that can be useful:
  - Ciprofloxacin
  - Azithromycin

---

- **Severe malnutrition:** Ampicillin + Gentamicin.
- **Cholera:** Doxycycline.
- **Giardiasis/amoebiasis:** Metronidazole.

## Persistent diarrhoea in children

It starts as an acute diarrhoea but persists for >14 days and is also due to infection.

### Risk factors

- Age <1 year.
  - May be due to immaturity of GIT as well as immune system to overcome the infection.
- Low birth weight or small-for-gestational-age (SGA) babies or malnutrition:
  - Delayed mucosal healing of GIT results in unusually prolonged/persistent diarrhoea.
- Some organisms:
  - E. coli:
    - Enteroadherent E. coli
    - Enteroaggregatory E. coli
  - Salmonella
  - Shigella
- Lactose intolerance:
  - Both a risk factor and a consequence of persistent diarrhoea.
- Cow's milk protein allergy:
  - β-lactoglobulin
  - Casein

### Consequences

It can predispose to nutritional deficiency, especially micronutrients like vitamin A.

- Imperative to give 1 dose of vitamin A while treating persistent diarrhoea.

## Lactose intolerance

Acquired/secondary due to persistent diarrhoea.

- Lactose enters the GIT from milk consumed.
- Lactose is digested with the help of the enzyme lactase.
- Lactase is produced from the tip of gastrointestinal villi.
- In persistent diarrhoea, there is loss of the tip of villi leading to loss of lactase activity.
- There is no digestion of lactose.

---

The lactose is now made to pass down the GIT in its undigested form.

Once it reaches the colon, the colonic bacteria convert it into lactic acid and hydrogen.

- Lactic acid can be excreted in the stools causing perianal excoriations/rash.
- Hydrogen, being gaseous, when excreted in stool causes explosive/gaseous stools.

### Management

- ORS after assessing dehydration.
- Zinc — same dose and duration as in acute diarrhoea.
- Vitamin A supplementation (single dose):
  - **<6 months:** 50,000 IU
  - **6–12 months:** 1 lakh IU
  - **>12 months:** 2 lakh IU
- Decrease or completely avoid lactose in the diet. Done in 3 steps:

| Diet | Composition |
|---|---|
| Diet A | Low-lactose diet; diluting/decreasing amount of milk given in diet |
| Diet B | No lactose, normal starch; monosaccharide-based diet such as rice-milk gruel, sooji-milk gruel, rice-curd mixture |
| Diet C | No lactose, no starch; completely avoid milk and dairy products along with reduced amount of starch; cereal/glucose, egg/chicken protein recipes |

---

### Page 219

# Miscellaneous Topics in GIT

## Gastroesophageal Reflux Disease

Reflux of gastric contents to esophagus.

- Child presents with recurrent vomiting.
- Recurrent vomiting causes malnutrition.
- Recurrent vomiting can also lead to aspiration pneumonitis.

### Sandifer syndrome

Age at onset: <6 months (4 months).

Associated syndrome with GERD.

Characteristically presents with sudden episodes of spasm of muscles involving trunk and limbs and **opisthotonus posturing** (arching of the back), seen similarly in tetanus.

### Diagnosis

1. **24-hour esophageal pH monitoring** if pH <4.
2. Best diagnostic test: **multichannel intraluminal impedance-pH monitoring**.

### Management

Usually resolves spontaneously by 2–3 years.

Therefore, first choice includes non-pharmacological measures:

- Thickening of feed.
- Small, frequent feeds.
- Positioning:
  - If baby is awake: prone position.
  - If baby is asleep: supine position.

---

### Pharmacological measures

Tried only if non-pharmacological measures fail.

- Drug of choice: **Proton pump inhibitors**.
- If refractory GERD does not respond to treatment:
  - Surgical correction in the form of **fundoplication** is done.

## Foreign Bodies in GIT

Once in the stomach, foreign bodies generally pass without difficulty throughout the remainder of the GIT and are excreted in feces.

Hence, it is important to recognize any foreign body that is above the stomach, i.e. in the esophagus.

- Age: 6 months–6 years.
- Most common objects: Coins and small toys.
- Most dangerous: **Disc-shaped button batteries**; they can cause caustic damage and coagulative necrosis of the esophagus.

### Diagnosis

#### Investigations

- X-ray: 90% of foreign bodies are radiopaque.
- Remaining 10%:
  - Barium study
  - Esophagogram
- Radiolucent foreign bodies appear as a filling defect on contrast X-ray.

### Sites of impaction

1. At the level of cricopharynx.
2. At mid-esophagus (at level of tracheal bifurcation).
3. Just above the lower esophageal sphincter.

### Presenting features

- Dysphagia
- Odynophagia
- Drooling of saliva
- Chest pain

### Management

If object is blunt and the child is asymptomatic: observe for 24 hours.

---

### Indications for immediate removal

1. Impacted for >24 hours.
2. Sharp objects.
3. Button batteries.
4. Magnets.
5. Symptomatic at any time.

Removal is done by **flexible endoscopic removal**.

### Foreign body in stomach and beyond

Mostly conservative management as they pass out through the intestine in the next 4–6 days.

### Indications of endoscopic removal

1. Battery >20 mm diameter, age <5 years: cannot pass through the pylorus.
2. Double-sided sharp objects like fish bones, toothpicks.
3. Open safety pin.
4. Size of object, longer than **>5 cm**: cannot pass through intestinal flexures.
5. Multiple magnets.

## Celiac Disease

Gluten hypersensitivity (a kind of food allergy).

### Pathogenesis

- T-cell-mediated autoimmune disorder.
- Autoantibody-mediated small intestinal destruction.

### Foods with gluten (BROW)

1. Barley
2. Rye
3. Oats
4. Wheat

### Genetic predisposition

- HLA DQ2
- HLA DQ8

In these patients, gluten exposure can lead to celiac disease, especially in Northern parts of India where wheat is the major diet.

### Features / Clinical profile

1. Malabsorption leads to failure to thrive.
2. Chronic diarrhoea (>2 weeks).

---

### Celiac Disease

3. Anemia refractory to iron treatment. Iron, vitamin B12 and folic acid are absorbed in the small intestine.
4. Age at onset: >6 months after birth, with commencement of complementary feed, as breast milk does not have gluten.

### Associations

1. Hashimoto's thyroiditis
2. Type 1 diabetes mellitus
3. Down syndrome
4. Turner's syndrome
5. Dermatitis herpetiformis — papulovesicular eruption on extensor surfaces/joints.

### Increased risk of tumors in adult life

- Adenocarcinoma
- Non-Hodgkin lymphoma — T-cell type

### Diagnosis

Revised European Society of Pediatric Gastroenterology and Nutrition (ESPGHAN):

1. Compatible clinical profile.
2. Biopsy — investigation of choice in Indian setup:
   - Villous atrophy/short villi
   - Crypt hyperplasia
   - Increased intraepithelial lymphocytes
3. Antibodies — all are IgA antibodies:
   - Anti-tissue transglutaminase antibody (anti-tTG) — screening test.
   - Anti-endomysial antibody — best diagnostic.
   - Anti-gliadin antibody.
   - Anti-reticulin antibody.
   - High false positive; little role in testing for CD.
4. Unequivocal response in 12 weeks to gluten-free diet.

### Management

- Lifelong gluten-free diet.
- Follow-up to check compliance:
  - Post 6 months: fall in titre levels of tTG antibody.
- Repeat jejunal biopsy for follow-up is not recommended.

---

### Page 223

# Neonatal Cholestasis

Cholestasis = stasis of bilirubin.

There is elevation of conjugated bilirubin levels manifesting as jaundice.

### Definition

If total serum bilirubin (TSB) is:

- <5 mg/dL: increase in conjugated bilirubin to ≥1 mg/dL.
- >5 mg/dL: increase in conjugated bilirubin to >20%.

It is suspected in a baby when jaundice is persisting.

### Clinical features

- Color of stools: Pale/clay/white stools.
  - Core of the stools is assessed because outer aspect may appear yellowish due to bile staining of epithelial cells of the intestine.
- Color of urine: Dark yellow.
- Associated with hepatomegaly.

### Etiology

#### Extrahepatic causes / obstructive jaundice

Due to obstruction to the flow of bile.

- **Biliary atresia (EHBA, extrahepatic biliary atresia):**
  - Overall most common cause.
- Choledochal cyst

#### Intrahepatic causes

Characterized by impaired excretion of bilirubin into the bile canaliculi.

- Idiopathic neonatal hepatitis — most common.
- Infections:
  - Neonatal sepsis
  - UTI
- Metabolic causes:
  1. Galactosemia

---

### Metabolic causes of neonatal cholestasis

1. Tyrosinemia
2. α1-antitrypsin deficiency — most common inherited cause worldwide
3. Cystic fibrosis
4. PFIC (Progressive Familial Intrahepatic Cholestasis), a genetic disorder characterized by impaired bile salt secretion.

### PFIC subtypes

- Type 1 — **ATP8B1** gene defect (Byler disease).
- Type 2 — **ABCB11** gene defect.
- Type 3 — adult onset.

Type 1 and type 2 have low levels of **GGT (gamma-glutamyl transpeptidase)**.

### Genetic conditions

1. Down syndrome / Trisomy 21
2. Edwards syndrome / Trisomy 18
3. Alagille syndrome

## Alagille Syndrome

- Autosomal dominant inherited disorder.
- Gene defects:
  - **JAGGED1** — most common
  - NOTCH2 defects

### Pathology

- Paucity of intrahepatic bile ducts, probably due to immune-mediated destruction of the bile ducts.
- Number of bile ducts keeps decreasing with age.

### Physical examination findings

- Jaundice.
- Abnormal facies:
  - Broad forehead
  - Deep-set, widely spaced eyes
  - Long straight nose
  - Underdeveloped mandible
  - Triangular facies
- Vertebral anomalies:
  - Butterfly-shaped vertebrae
  - Hemivertebrae

---

### Alagille Syndrome

- Eye:
  - Microcornea
  - Posterior embryotoxon — ring-shaped structure due to prominence of the Schwalbe's ring.
- CVS anomalies:
  - Peripheral pulmonary stenosis — most common cardiac finding.
  - Tetralogy of Fallot (TOF)
  - Ventricular septal defect (VSD)
- Pruritus
- Tendon xanthomas due to high cholesterol.

## Extrahepatic Biliary Atresia

Biliary atresia is not associated with cyst formation.

Also called **non-cystic obliterative cholangiopathy**.

### Pathology

- Complete obliteration of the extrahepatic bile ducts.
- Obliteration of intrahepatic ducts at the level of the hilum.

### Classification of biliary atresia

Based on the presence of associated anomalies:

- **Perinatal type (10% cases):**
  - Not associated with any other anomalies.
- **BASM syndrome (15% cases):**
  - Associated with:
    1. Biliary atresia
    2. Splenic malformations + polysplenia
    3. Situs inversus
    4. Cardiac defects
    5. Malrotation of GIT
  - Poor prognosis.
- Other 15%:
  - Renal anomalies
  - Choledochal cyst

### Investigations

1. Liver function tests:
   - Increased conjugated bilirubin.
2. Markers of cholestasis:
   - 5'-nucleotidase
   - Alkaline phosphatase (ALP)
   - GGT
     - Normal is up to 40 U/L.
     - Amount of rise in GGT can give clues to the cause of neonatal cholestasis.
     - 4× elevation: intrahepatic causes.

---

### Neonatal Cholestasis — Investigations

- **10× elevation of GGT:** extrahepatic causes.
- Exception: GGT levels are low in PFIC type 1 and type 2.

### 3. Ultrasound

High-frequency US (HUS) is more helpful in evaluation of neonatal cholestasis.

USG is also helpful in detecting associated anomalies.

#### Characteristic USG findings in EHBA

- **Triangular cord sign:**
  - Hyperechoic areas corresponding to fibrous/atretic portion of the bile duct.
- Non-visualized gallbladder / microgallbladder / gallbladder ghost.

### HIDA scan

Shows decreased excretion of tracer from the liver due to obstruction.

Phenobarbital loading/priming has to be done before HIDA scan to increase uptake of radionuclide tracer by the liver.

- Used only as a screening test.
- Not a specific investigation.

### Best investigation

**Liver biopsy**

|  | Intrahepatic/idiopathic neonatal cholestasis | EHBA |
|---|---|---|
| Hepatic architecture | Disarray of hepatic lobules | Lobules preserved/altered |
| Giant cells | + | + |
| Portal reaction | Inflammation with minimal fibrosis | Marked fibrosis |
| Ductular proliferation | Rare | Evidence of proliferation |
| Portal bile duct | — | Plugging along with bile lake formation |

---

# Neonatal Cholestasis: Management

### Medical management

| Clinical impairment | Management |
|---|---|
| Malabsorption of dietary long-chain triglycerides | Medium-chain triglyceride (MCT) oil supplementation |
| Fat-soluble vitamin malabsorption | Vitamin A, D, E, K supplementation |
|  | Vitamin A: 10,000–25,000 IU/day |
|  | Vitamin D: 800 IU/day |
|  | Vitamin E: 25–200 units/kg/day |
|  | Vitamin K: 2–5 mg repeated every 4 weeks |
| Micronutrient deficiency | Ca²⁺ phosphate and zinc supplementation |
| Deficiency of water-soluble vitamins | Twice the RDA |
| Retention of biliary constituents such as cholesterol (pruritus) | UDCA (ursodeoxycholic acid), cholestyramine |

### Surgical management

#### Algorithm followed in surgical management of EHBA

**Exploratory laparotomy**

↓

**Peroperative cholangiogram**

↓

**Temporary procedure: Kasai procedure / hepaticojejunostomy**

↓

**Definitive management: Liver transplantation**

### Kasai procedure

- It is a temporary procedure.
- Communication is created between the liver and the small intestine (jejunum) for bile to drain out.
- Also known as **hepaticojejunostomy**.
- It should be done within **8 weeks after birth**.

---

### Liver Transplantation

- **Most common indication for liver transplantation in children:** Biliary atresia.

---

### Page 229

# Metabolic Liver Disorders

## α1-Antitrypsin Deficiency

Most commonly inherited metabolic liver disorder.

- Less common in India compared to the West.
- Inheritance: Autosomal recessive.
- Gene defect: **SERPINA1** gene on chromosome 14q.
- Production of a misfolded form of α1-antitrypsin (α1AT).

### Normal role of α1-antitrypsin

- Protects lung tissue (acinus) from neutrophil elastases.

### In α1-antitrypsin deficiency

- Misfolded α1AT accumulates in the liver.
- Cannot enter the lungs.
- Toxic to liver cells.
- Destruction of lung tissue by neutrophil elastases.

### Phenotypic expression

- Normal phenotype: **Pi MM**
  - PI = protease inhibitor
  - MM = normal allele
- Abnormal phenotype: **Pi ZZ**
  - ZZ = abnormal allele

### Features

- Liver damage:
  - In 80–85% cases, liver recovers completely.
  - Only 15–40% cases develop chronic liver disease.

---

### α1-Antitrypsin deficiency

- Pan-acinar emphysema:
  - Does not develop in childhood.
  - Develops in adults by 30–40 years of age.
  - Smoking increases the risk of development of pan-acinar emphysema.

### Investigations

- α1AT level: Low (50–80 mg/dL).
- Genetic testing: **SERPINA1 gene**.
- Liver biopsy:
  - Misfolded α1AT as intrahepatic globular inclusions in the liver.
  - PAS-positive.
  - Diastase resistance is also seen.

### Treatment

- Supportive treatment.
- Limiting exposure to tobacco smoke.
- In adults:
  - Infusion therapy with normal α1AT collected from healthy individuals.
- If chronic liver disease develops:
  - Liver transplant.

## Wilson Disease

- Gene defect: **ATP7B** on chromosome 13.
- This gene codes for an ATPase involved in copper metabolism.

### Normal role

- Excretion of excess Cu²⁺ into bile.
- Incorporation of Cu²⁺ with ceruloplasmin (Cu²⁺-binding protein).
- Bound Cu²⁺ in blood is nontoxic.

### Wilson disease

- Impaired excretion of Cu²⁺ in bile leading to accumulation of Cu²⁺ in the liver → liver damage.
- Increased levels of unbound/free Cu²⁺ in blood → toxic.

---

## Wilson Disease

### Clinical features

- Chronic liver disease, cirrhosis of liver:
  - Age of presentation is usually 7–12 years.
- Eye damage:
  - **Kayser-Fleischer ring** in cornea due to deposition of Cu²⁺ in Descemet's membrane.
- Cataract:
  - Sunflower cataract.
- Neurological manifestations:
  1. Basal ganglia damage → abnormal movements, tremors, seizures.
  2. Psychiatric manifestations.
- Feature of brain damage noted in children with Wilson disease:
  - Deterioration of scholastic performance.

### Childhood vs adult onset

- Childhood-onset Wilson disease: classically presents with chronic liver manifestations.
- Adult-onset Wilson disease: presents with neurological manifestations.

### Other manifestations

- Cu²⁺-mediated hemolysis of RBCs (negative Coombs test).
- Arthralgia.

### Investigations

#### Screening investigations

- Urine Cu²⁺: Increased.
- Serum ceruloplasmin: Decreased.

#### Liver biopsy

- Macrovesicular steatosis.
- Mallory-hyaline bodies (not very specific).

Assessment of Cu²⁺ levels in liver tissue: increased
(approximately 250 μg/g dry weight of liver tissue).

### Treatment

Decrease Cu²⁺ levels in the body by limiting the amount of Cu²⁺ in the diet.

---

### Wilson Disease

#### Cu²⁺ chelating agents

- **D-penicillamine:** DOC in children.
- **Trientine**.

### Zinc

Zinc induces production of **metallothionein**, which inhibits absorption of Cu²⁺ from the intestine.

### Chronic liver disease/cirrhosis

- Liver transplantation.

Chronic usage of D-penicillamine leads to deficiency of vitamin B6.

Therefore, D-penicillamine should always be prescribed along with **vitamin B6** to prevent development of optic neuritis.

# Glycogen Storage Disorders (GSD)

### Types

| Liver GSD | Muscle GSD |
|---|---|
| GSD I: Von Gierke's disease — glucose-6-phosphatase deficiency | GSD II: Pompe's disease — acid maltase deficiency |
| GSD III: Cori's/Forbes disease — debranching enzyme deficiency | GSD V: McArdle's disease — muscle phosphorylase deficiency |
| GSD IV: Anderson's disease — branching enzyme deficiency | GSD VII: Tarui's disease |
| GSD VI: Hers disease — liver phosphorylase deficiency |  |

### Common features of liver GSD and muscle GSD

| Liver GSD | Muscle GSD |
|---|---|
| Fasting hypoglycaemia / hypoglycaemic seizures | Exertional fatigue |
| Hepatomegaly | Recovery of fatigue with rest (“second-wind” phenomenon) |

---

## GSD I — Von Gierke's Disease

- Deficiency: **Glucose-6-phosphatase enzyme**.
- Overall most common type.

### Biochemical consequences

Glucose-6-phosphate cannot be converted to free glucose.

- Hypoglycemia
- Hepatomegaly
- Hyperuricemia
- Lactic acidosis
- Hyperlipidemia

### Features

- Characteristic round facies (doll-like facies).
- Abdominal distension due to hepatomegaly.

### Biochemical findings

- Hyperuricemia
- Lactic acidosis
- Hyperlipidemia

### Treatment

- Frequent feeding (every 2–3 hours) to prevent fasting hypoglycemia.
- Night time:
  - Nasogastric feeds.
- Uncooked cornstarch in every meal because it releases small amounts of glucose at frequent intervals.
- Older children:
  - Waxy maize heat-modified starch to prevent overnight episodes of hypoglycemia.

---

## GSD III — Cori's/Forbes Disease

- Deficiency: Debranching enzyme.

### Features

- Some cases can also have muscle involvement.
- No biochemical abnormalities.
- No renomegaly.
- Splenomegaly is present.

## GSD IV — Anderson's Disease

- Deficiency: Branching enzyme.
- Liver damage and **early-onset cirrhosis**.
- Worst prognosis.

## GSD II — Pompe's Disease

**Muscle GSD**

- Deficiency: Acid maltase (lysosomal enzyme).
- Also a lysosomal storage disorder.
- Skeletal muscles and cardiac muscles are affected.
- Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy leading to cardiac failure.
- Liver is also involved (hypoglycemia).
- Macroglossia.
- Severe hypotonia (floppy infant).
- Poor prognosis.
- Treatment: **ERT (enzyme replacement therapy)**.

## Tyrosinemia

Defect in tyrosine metabolic pathway.

- 3 types; Type I is more common.

### Type I Tyrosinemia

- Autosomal recessive.
- Deficiency of **Fumarylacetoacetate hydrolase (FAH)**.

---

## Tyrosinemia

### Metabolic pathway of tyrosine

Tyrosine → 4-hydroxyphenylpyruvate → Homogentisate → Maleylacetoacetate → Fumarylacetoacetate → Fumarate + Acetoacetate

Fumarylacetoacetate → **Succinylacetone**

### Increased levels of fumarylacetoacetate and succinylacetone lead to

#### Liver

- Chronic liver disease
- Cirrhosis
- Increased AFP (alpha-fetoprotein)

#### Kidney

- Damage to renal proximal convoluted tubule
- Fanconi-like syndrome

Type I tyrosinemia is also known as **hepatorenal tyrosinemia**.

### Neurological manifestations

- Recurrent acute episodes of neuropathy involving limbs.
- Severe pain in the limbs associated with vomiting.
- Urine odour: **boiled cabbage odour** due to accumulation of methionine metabolites.

### Diagnosis

- Succinylacetone levels in urine: Increased.

### Treatment

- Reduced intake of phenylalanine and tyrosine in diet.
- **Nitisinone (DOC)**:
  - Inhibits 4-HPPD (4-hydroxyphenylpyruvate dioxygenase) enzyme.

### Tyrosinemia Type II

Deficiency of **TAT (tyrosine aminotransferase)**.

### Features

- Corneal ulcers.
- Palmoplantar keratosis.

---

### Tyrosinemia Type II

- Keratoderma (thick, rough skin).
- Painful, non-pruritic lesions.
- Also called **oculocutaneous tyrosinemia**.

### Tyrosinemia Type III

- Deficiency of **4-HPPD**.
- Neurological manifestations:
  - Developmental delay
  - Ataxia
  - Seizures

In both type II and III tyrosinemia, liver and renal functions are normal.

# Galactosemia

- Autosomal recessive.

### Galactose metabolism

**GALT:** Galactose-1-phosphate uridyltransferase.

**UGP:** UDP-glucose/galactose pyrophosphorylase.

**GALE:** UDP-galactose 4′-epimerase.

**Gal-1-P:** Galactose-1-phosphate.

### Important points

- Most common enzyme deficiency: **GALT**.
- Accumulation of galactose-1-phosphate.

### Clinical features

Starts in neonatal period after initiation of breastfeeds, because breast milk contains lactose which can be converted to galactose.

- Galactose is not converted to glucose → hypoglycemia, seizures.
- Liver damage:
  - Hepatomegaly
  - Acute/chronic liver disease
  - Cirrhosis

---

### Galactosemia

- Kidney damage:
  - Fanconi-like syndrome (PCT affected).
- Eye:
  - Accumulation of galactitol, which gets converted to dulcitol and is deposited in the lens leading to cataract (**oil-drop cataract**).
- Cause of death: **E. coli sepsis**.

### Late complications

- Speech defect
- Delay in development of milestones
- Apraxia
- Ovarian failure in female adolescents (delayed puberty)

### Investigations

- Benedict's test of urine: Positive (reducing substances).
- Confirmatory test: Assay of GALT in RBCs.

### Treatment

- Eliminate galactose from diet.
- Breast milk is absolutely contraindicated.
- No-lactose formula and soy-based formula.
- Supplementation:
  - Calcium
  - Vitamin D
- Assessment of compliance by levels of galactose-1-phosphate in RBCs:
  - Normal → compliant
  - Elevated → non-compliant

# Hereditary Fructose Intolerance

- Autosomal recessively inherited.
- Defect: **Fructose-1-phosphate aldolase B enzyme deficiency**.
- Accumulation of fructose which is not converted to glucose → hypoglycemia.
- Onset of symptoms: ~6 months.
- Characteristic feature: Self-restriction/aversion to sweets.
- Liver and kidney dysfunction is seen.
- No cataract formation.

### Investigations

- Urine analysis for fructose.
- Confirmatory test: Aldolase B enzyme assay in liver tissue.

### Treatment

- Avoid sucrose, fructose and sorbitol in the diet.
- Drugs or vitamins dispersed in sucrose base should be avoided.

---

# Respiratory System

### Page 238

# Upper Airway Disorders in Children

## Upper Airway Obstruction

Upper airway refers to the larynx.

**Feature of upper airway obstruction:** **Stridor** (respiratory sound).

### Conditions leading to stridor

- Anomalies of the larynx.
- Infection in larynx.
- Foreign body in larynx.

## Congenital Anomalies of Larynx

### Laryngomalacia

- Most common anomaly of larynx.
- Softness/laxity of larynx.

### Structures affected

- Epiglottis
- Arytenoids
- Aryepiglottic folds

Abnormality in shape of structures in larynx → omega-shaped epiglottis.

Increased compliance during inspiration (in supine posture) → folding (epiglottis pushed downward into airway) → obstruction of upper airway → intermittent stridor.

Stridor occurs when child is:

- In supine position
- Crying/agitated
- Feeding

Stridor disappears in prone posture.

- Long-standing stridor.
- Starts soon after birth or within two weeks after birth.

---

## Laryngomalacia

- Diagnosed clinically.
- No investigations are needed.

### Treatment

- Reassurance: improves with age and resolves spontaneously.
- Severe stridor → **supraglottoplasty** (rare).

# Croup

**Acute Laryngotracheobronchitis (ALT)**

- Age: 6 months to 5 years.
- Peak: 2 years.

### Etiology

- Parainfluenza virus.

### Presentation

- Stridor, associated with low-grade fever in an otherwise well child.
- Cough:
  - Barking
  - Seal-like
  - Brassy cough

### Grading

#### 1. Mild croup

- Stridor only upon coughing/exertion.
- SpO₂ normal.

#### 2. Moderate croup

- Stridor at rest.
- Respiratory distress:
  - Tachypnea/fast breathing
  - Chest retractions
- SpO₂ normal.

#### 3. Severe croup

- Stridor at rest.
- Respiratory distress:
  - Tachypnea/fast breathing
  - Chest retractions
- Hypoxia:
  - SpO₂ <92% in room air.

### X-ray

Narrowing of airway, with maximum narrowing in the subglottic area → **steeple sign**.

---

# Croup — Management

Mainly supportive treatment.

### Mild croup

- Oral dexamethasone **0.5–0.6 mg/kg** as a single dose.

### Moderate to severe croup

If hypoxia is present:

- O₂ inhalation.
- Nebulization with racemic epinephrine:
  - Racemic epinephrine contains D and L isomers.
- Or L-epinephrine alone, whichever is available.

# Acute Epiglottitis

It is a **medical emergency**.

- Age group: 1–3 years (toddler age group).
- Etiology: **H. influenzae type b** (most common).

### Presentation

- Sudden-onset high-grade fever.
- Sick-looking child.
- Stridor.
- Respiratory distress.
- Characteristic posture:
  - Tripod posture
  - Sniffing-dog posture

### Investigations

- Lateral X-ray of neck:
  - **Thumb sign**
  - Swollen epiglottis causes narrowing of airway, giving impression of a thumb.
- Laryngoscopy:
  - Cherry-red epiglottis due to severe inflammation.

Laryngoscopy is not done in acute epiglottitis because of risk of acute laryngeal spasm while maneuvering.

### Management

1. Priority: Secure airway:
   - Endotracheal intubation or tracheotomy.

---

### Acute Epiglottitis

2. Antibiotics:
   - 3rd-generation cephalosporin:
     - Ceftriaxone
     - Cefotaxime
3. No role for sedatives.

## Differentials for Stridor

| Feature | Fever present | No fever |
|---|---|---|
| General appearance | Sick-looking | Not sick, otherwise well child |
| Other features | Drooling, tripod posture, barking cough | Since birth |
| Condition | Acute epiglottitis | Croup / Laryngomalacia |

**Stridor → inspiratory sound.**

---

### Page 242

# Foreign Body Aspiration, Congenital Lung Anomalies & Asthma

## Foreign Body Aspiration

Commonly seen in age <2 years.

### Most common foreign bodies

- Peanuts
- Coins

### Lower airway foreign body aspiration

**Location:** Bronchus; right and left bronchus equally affected.

### Presentation

- Sudden-onset cough/choke.
- Breathing difficulty.
- Presents without fever.
- Most common presentation: sudden onset of cough.
- On auscultation, unilateral wheeze is heard.

### Further course

#### Complete airway obstruction

- Air cannot pass through completely blocked bronchus.
- No ventilation of respective lung segment.
- Segmental atelectasis.

#### Partial airway obstruction

**Ball-valve effect:**

- During inspiration, air goes in.
- During expiration, no air goes out.
- Air trapping.

May lead to:

- Chronic cough/wheeze localized to one side.
- Recurrent infection.
- Bronchiectasis.

---

### Foreign Body Aspiration

Presence of foreign body is suspected when a child has:

- Recurrent pneumonia involving the same side of lungs and the same segment.
- Only 10–20% of foreign bodies are radio-opaque.
- All foreign bodies may not be seen on X-ray.

### Suspicion of foreign body aspiration

1. History suggestive of sudden-onset symptoms.
2. Focal abnormal lung findings.
3. Abnormal X-ray.

If any **2 out of 3** are present, suspicion is strong and removal of the foreign body is initiated.

### Treatment

- Rigid bronchoscopy-guided removal under GA.

## Upper airway foreign body aspiration

### Presentation

- Stridor.

### Treatment

Immediate removal.

Depends on age of child:

#### Infant

- Prone: back blows between shoulder blades (5 times).
- Supine: chest compression using finger technique over lower sternum (5 times).
- Repeat alternately until the foreign body is expelled.

#### Age >1 year

- Heimlich's manoeuvre:
  - Upward and backward epigastric thrusts from behind.

---

# Congenital Lung Anomalies

## Congenital Lobar Emphysema

Also called **congenital lobar hyperinflation**.

### Cause

Cartilaginous bronchial dysplasia:

- Developmental defect leading to partial ball-valve effect.
- Results in air trapping and hyperinflation.

### Site

- Left upper lobe
- Right middle lobe

### Characteristic features

- Mediastinal shift.
- Respiratory distress in newborn.

### Diagnosis

- X-ray / CT chest.

### Treatment

- Lobectomy.

## Pulmonary Sequestration

Non-functional pulmonary tissue that does not communicate with the tracheobronchial tree.

- Blood supply from systemic arteries.
- Classified as:
  - Intralobar
  - Extralobar
- Extralobar has separate pleural covering.
- Intralobar is covered by common pleura.
- Site: Lower lobes of left lung.
- Complication: Recurrent respiratory infection.
- Investigation: CT chest.
- Treatment: Surgical resection.

## Congenital Pulmonary Adenomatoid Malformation (CPAM)

Previously called **CCAM (Congenital Cystic Adenomatoid Malformation)**.

- Most common cause of cystic lung disease in newborns.
- Unilateral hamartomatous lesions.
- Site: Left lung.
- Presents either in newborn or older children as recurrent infection.
- Sometimes asymptomatic.
- Risk of malignancy (sarcoma/carcinoma) of lung.
- Treatment: Surgical resection within one year of age.

---

# Asthma

Characterized by **bronchoconstriction**.

Present with wheeze (airway narrowing).

### Prerequisites to diagnose asthma

- Triggered by allergens or infections.
- Reversible, either spontaneously or using short-acting beta agonists.
- Recurrent episodes (at least 3 or more).
- Presence of risk factors such as:
  1. History of asthma/other allergies in parents.
  2. Other allergic disorders:
     - Atopic dermatitis
     - Sinusitis
     - Allergic rhinitis
  3. Male gender.
  4. Low socioeconomic status.
  5. Repeated exposure to tobacco smoke.

### Triggers of asthma

- Dust
- Pollen
- Exercise
- Stress
- Respiratory infections

### Types

| Atopic (most common) | Non-atopic |
|---|---|
| Extrinsic | Intrinsic |
| Trigger: allergen | Trigger: drug-induced (aspirin), viral infection, exercise |
| IgE-mediated | — |

## Cough-variant asthma

- Presents with recurrent cough.
- Seen in young children.

### Investigations

## Spirometry

1. Evidence of airflow limitation:
   - Low FEV₁
   - FEV₁/FVC <0.8
2. Bronchodilator response:
   - Increased FEV₁ ≥12% after inhalation of bronchodilator like salbutamol.

---

### Investigations for Asthma

3. **Exercise challenge:** Worsening FEV₁ ≥15% after exercise.
4. Daily PEF or FEV₁ monitoring:
   - AM–PM variation and/or day-to-day variation.
   - PEF = Peak Expiratory Flow.
   - Variation ≥20% is suggestive of asthma.
5. Exhaled nitric oxide:
   - ≥20 ppb.

# Management of Asthma

## Long-term management: Step-wise management

### 1. Intermittent asthma

Characterized by:

- Infrequent symptoms.
- Child is normal in between attacks.

**Treatment:** Short-acting beta agonists as and when needed.

### 2. Persistent asthma

#### Mild persistent

- Day episode ≥2/month.
- Night-time awakening ≥1/month.

**Treatment:** Daily low-dose inhaled corticosteroids (ICS):

- Budesonide
- Fluticasone
- Beclomethasone

#### Moderate persistent

- Daily attacks or need to use SABA daily.

**Treatment:**

- Medium-dose ICS, or
- Low-dose ICS daily + LABA (long-acting beta agonist) such as:
  - Salmeterol
  - Formoterol

#### Severe persistent

- Daily attacks but continuous throughout day and night.
- Significant limitation of physical activities.

**Treatment:** High-dose ICS daily + LABA.

### Alternative choices

Add-on drugs for better control:

- Montelukast.

---

### Asthma

- Sustained-release theophylline.
- Oral short-course steroids or alternate-day steroids.

Oral short-course steroids are used only when the child does not respond to every other drug, to avoid systemic complications.

# Asthma Medication Devices

### Metered-dose inhaler (MDI)

Given along with a spacer.

- **<4 years:** Face mask + spacer + MDI.
- **4–12 years:** Spacer + MDI.
- **>12 years:** Only MDI.

### Rotahaler

Also called dry-powder inhaler (DPI):

- >5 years.

Components:

- Mouthpiece
- Hole for capsule
- Rota chamber

Rotacap is a capsule containing the drug.

### Nebulizer

Can be used to administer medicines in a hospital setup.

---

### Pharmacology notes

- **M₃ receptors in bronchi:** Ipratropium → short-acting antagonist.
- **Tiotropium:** Long-acting antagonist → COPD (not in children).
- **β₂-agonists:**
  - Terbutaline
  - Salbutamol → short-acting (SABA)

# Management of Acute Attacks of Asthma

### Initial treatment

- SABA (MDI/nebulizer):
  - One puff every minute (10 puffs) / single nebulisation.
- Give O₂ if SpO₂ <95%.
- Start oral prednisolone (1–2 mg/kg).
- Assess after 1 hour.

### If improvement

- Continue SABA every 6–8 hours for next 24 hours.

### If no response

- Nebulisation of salbutamol + ipratropium.
- Injection hydrocortisone (10 mg/kg).
- Assess after 2 hours.

### If still no response

- Injection theophylline.
- IV magnesium sulphate infusion.
- Mechanical ventilation if no response.

### Discharge treatment after improvement

- SABA
- Oral prednisolone for 5 days.

Tapering of oral prednisolone is not required.

Oral steroids followed by IV steroids is the norm.

# Life-Threatening Asthma

Any child presenting with:

- Silent chest.
- Cyanosis.
- Altered sensorium.
- Poor respiratory efforts/laboured breathing.
- SpO₂ <90%.
- PEFR <30% of expected.

### Treatment

- Injection adrenaline/terbutaline subcutaneously.
- Injection hydrocortisone.
- Nebulized salbutamol + ipratropium.

---

### Page 249

# Lower Respiratory Tract Infections

## Acute Bronchiolitis

It is a common seasonal infection which usually occurs in winter and spring seasons.

### Most common cause

**RSV — Respiratory Syncytial Virus**

### Clinical presentation

Initially starts as an upper respiratory tract infection.

After 1 week, infection spreads to the lower respiratory tract (bronchioles) leading to:

- Fever
- Breathing difficulty
- Bilateral wheeze due to passage of air through narrowed and inflamed bronchioles.

### X-ray findings

- Hyperinflation of lung fields.
- Bronchioles are inflamed and narrowed.

During inspiration, some amount of air is able to pass through bronchioles as they are not fully obstructed.

During expiration, air columns tend to become smaller and there is almost complete obstruction.

This results in trapping of air inside alveoli of affected bronchioles.

- Perihilar fullness/perihilar cuffing due to inflammation of bronchioles in hilar regions → white markings.

### Clinical findings due to hyperinflation

- Increased AP diameter of chest.
- Hyperresonance on percussion.

Clinical findings + X-ray are enough to make diagnosis.

### Other investigation

- PCR testing of nasopharyngeal swab/aspirate.
  - Not done routinely.

---

## Acute Bronchiolitis — Treatment

Usually a self-limiting condition.

### Supportive management

- Moist O₂ inhalation to maintain SpO₂ ≥92% in room air.
- Fluids:
  - Oral or IV fluids.

### Antivirals

Prescribed for high-risk children:

- Chronic lung diseases
- Congenital heart defects
- Immunodeficient children

**Drug of choice:** Ribavirin, inhalational form.

- Decreases duration and severity of illness.

Before the start of the season, prophylactic **Palivizumab** is given:

- Monoclonal antibody against F protein of RSV.

Bronchiolitis usually subsides within **3–7 days**.

Children with bronchiolitis may develop asthma in future.

# Pneumonia

An important cause of under-5 mortality.

### Definition

Pathological consolidation of alveoli or inflammation of interstitial tissue around alveoli or both.

### Etiology

- Bacterial
- Viral

### Common causes

- Overall most common bacteria: **Streptococcus pneumoniae**.
- Most common cause in neonates:
  - Gram-negative organisms:
    - Klebsiella
    - E. coli
  - Staphylococcus aureus
- Most common virus: RSV.
- In children with HIV: **Pneumocystis jirovecii (carinii)**.
- In adolescents/older children (>5 years):
  - **Mycoplasma pneumoniae** (atypical pneumonia).

Pneumonia is an important public health problem because of under-5 mortality.

---

# Community Management Guidelines

| Category | Features | Management |
|---|---|---|
| Home management | Color code: Green | Oral paracetamol. If wheeze present → salbutamol for 5 days |
| Pneumonia | Fever, cough, cold + fast breathing and/or chest indrawing. SpO₂ normal | Home management. Color code: Yellow. Oral antibiotic — amoxicillin for 5 days |
| Severe pneumonia / very severe disease | Any danger sign: hypoxia (SpO₂ <90% in room air), stridor in a calm child, or any danger signs | Color code: Pink. Give first dose of antibiotic (ampicillin + gentamicin) and immediately refer child to hospital |

### Danger signs

1. Not feeding/drinking
2. Lethargic
3. Cyanosis all over
4. Convulsions

### Criteria for fast breathing

| Age | Respiratory rate |
|---|---:|
| 0–2 months | >60/minute |
| 2–12 months | >50/minute |
| 1–5 years | >40/minute |

# X-ray Patterns of Pneumonia

## Streptococcus pneumoniae

**X-ray finding:** Lobar consolidation.

Associated with typical rusty sputum, not seen as children swallow sputum.

- **Lobar pneumonia**

---

## Staphylococcus aureus

### Staphylococcal pneumonia

#### X-ray pattern

- Patchy consolidation.
- **Pneumatoceles**:
  - Air-filled spaces.
  - Can rupture and lead to pneumothorax.

### Risk factors

- Severe malnutrition
- Diabetes
- As a complication of:
  - Influenza
  - Measles infection

### Complications

- Pneumothorax
- Empyema (pus in pleural cavity)

It is a severe pneumonia in children.

### Treatment

- Penicillin-G / co-amoxiclav.
- If no response, add 3rd-generation cephalosporins.
- If MRSA is suspected:
  - Add vancomycin / linezolid.

### Pneumatoceles are seen in

- Staphylococcus aureus-associated pneumonia.
- Klebsiella/E. coli-associated pneumonia.
- Hydrocarbon poisoning.

---

## Mycoplasma Pneumonia

### Atypical pneumonia

- X-ray pattern:
  - Bilateral interstitial pneumonia.
  - May appear as bronchopneumonia.

### M. pneumoniae features

- Usually affects older children (>5 years).
- Child appears clinically well (**walking pneumonia**).
- Extensive interstitial pattern but clinically well child.

### Treatment

- Macrolides:
  - Erythromycin.

# Recurrent Pneumonia and Persistent Pneumonia

### Recurrent pneumonia

- ≥2 episodes in 1 year, or
- ≥3 episodes in a lifetime.

### Persistent pneumonia

- Non-resolving pneumonia.
- Characterized by persistent radiological findings for >1 month.

### Risk factors for recurrent/persistent pneumonia

- Severe malnutrition.
- Immunodeficiency.
- Recurrent aspiration:
  1. GERD.
  2. Upper GI anomalies such as esophageal atresia:
     - Aspiration of vomitus.
  3. Congenital heart defects:
     - Due to pulmonary congestion.
- Genetic disorders:
  1. Cystic fibrosis.
  2. Kartagener's syndrome.

If risk factors are treated, recurrence could be avoided.

---

### Page 254

# Cystic Fibrosis

# CYSTIC FIBROSIS

## Introduction to Cystic Fibrosis (CF)

- Cystic fibrosis is a multisystem AR disorder:
  - GIT
  - Respiratory system
  - Genitourinary tract
  - Sweat glands
- One of the most common life-limiting genetic disorders.
- Seen mostly in Western population.
- Cases continue to increase in India as well.

### CFTR gene

- CFTR = cystic fibrosis transmembrane conductance regulator.
- Located on chromosome **7q**.
- Codes for CFTR protein (Cl⁻ channel).
- Mutation of CFTR gene causes defective CFTR protein.
- Most common mutation: **ΔF508** — deletion of phenylalanine at 508th position.
- Approximately 1500 mutations have been described in the gene.

## Mucoviscidosis

Thick mucus secretions in body.

### In normal people

- CFTR channel transports Cl⁻ ions from intracellular portion into lumen.
- Cl⁻ makes mucus thin.
- Thin mucus is easily expelled by mucociliary clearance.

### Cystic fibrosis

- Inactive CFTR channel.
- Cl⁻ ions cannot be transported into lumen.
- Thick mucus develops.
- Mucus cannot be expelled.
- **Mucoviscidosis**.
- Recurrent infections of respiratory tract / recurrent pneumonia.

---

# Clinical Features of Cystic Fibrosis

## Gastrointestinal tract

Earliest features.

### 1. Meconium ileus

Normally, meconium is the first stool passed by a newborn from small intestinal secretions.

- Early manifestation.
- In cystic fibrosis:
  - Meconium becomes thick.
  - Not easily expelled.
  - Gets stuck in intestinal lumen.
  - Inspissation of thick meconium in ileum → intestinal obstruction.
- Patient presents with:
  - Delayed passage of meconium
  - Abdominal distension

Seen in approximately 10–15% of patients with CF.

### Contrast enema

- Microcolon (large intestine appears small).
- Filling defects in colon.

### Obstruction of terminal ileum

- Distended small intestine proximal to terminal ileum.
- Air-fluid levels.
- Ground-glass appearance in lower abdomen.

### 2. Constipation

Occurs in older children.

- Impaction of fecal matter in distal part of ileum + **DIOS (Distal Intestinal Obstruction Syndrome)**.

---

### 3. Exocrine pancreatic insufficiency

Pancreatic secretions become thick → not expelled into duodenum.

- Fat and fat-soluble vitamins (A, D, E, K) are not absorbed.
- Steatorrhea + deficiency of vitamins A, D, E & K.
- 85% of children have this condition.
- One of the most common manifestations of cystic fibrosis.

### 4. Endocrine pancreatic insufficiency

Endocrine pancreatic insufficiency → decreased insulin secretion → may cause **diabetes mellitus** in future.

### 5. Rectal prolapse

- Uncommon due to early supplementation with pancreatic enzyme.
- Presents, if at all, in older children.

## Respiratory tract manifestations

### Recurrent infections

Different organisms cause infection or pneumonia in a child with cystic fibrosis:

- In early childhood:
  - **Staphylococcus aureus** — overall most common.
  - **H. influenzae type B**
- In late childhood/adults:
  - **Pseudomonas**

Pseudomonas colonizes respiratory tract:

- Produces mucoid secretions.
- Forms biofilm around organism.
- Provides antibiotic resistance to Pseudomonas.

**Burkholderia cepacia:** Increased risk of death in cystic fibrosis.

### Nasal polyps

In a child with bilateral nasal polyps → cystic fibrosis should be evaluated.

## Other Features of Cystic Fibrosis

### Biliary tract

- Biliary cirrhosis/neonatal cholestasis.
- Thickening of biliary secretions → bile outflow obstruction.

---

# Cystic Fibrosis

### Pancreatic insufficiency

Initially exocrine → endocrine.

### Genitourinary tract manifestations

- All affected males are infertile.
- Associated with azoospermia due to failure of development of Wolffian duct.
- Females:
  - Decreased fertility rate.

### Sweat glands

Normally, CFTR protein helps in reabsorption of Na⁺/Cl⁻ from sweat glands.

In cystic fibrosis, CFTR protein is non-functional:

- Excessive loss of Na⁺/Cl⁻ in sweat.
- Salty skin (may feel salty while kissing).
- Frosting of skin.
- Alkalosis.
- Increased sweat chloride helps in diagnosis.

# Diagnostic Criteria for Cystic Fibrosis

## When to suspect?

- Presence of typical clinical features:
  - Respiratory
  - Gastrointestinal
  - Genitourinary
- History of CF in a sibling because it is autosomal recessive.
- Positive newborn screening test:
  - Immunoreactive trypsin test.
  - Commonly done in Western countries.

Presence of any one should prompt confirmation with lab tests.

### Confirmation

- Elevated sweat chloride concentration:
  - ≥60 mEq/L obtained on separate days.
- Identification of two CF mutations.
- Epithelial nasal potential difference measurement.

---

## Sweat Chloride Test

### Pilocarpine iontophoresis

- Pilocarpine is administered into skin of baby with the help of electrodes.
- Baby's sweat is collected in a collection device.
- Collection device automatically processes the chloride levels.

### Confirmation

- Identification of two CF mutations, or
- Epithelial nasal potential difference measurement.

Any one positive laboratory test is sufficient.

# Management of Cystic Fibrosis

## Respiratory care

### Airway clearance treatment

- Physiotherapy to remove thick mucus.

### Mucolytics

- Inhaled hypertonic saline.
- Human recombinant DNAse (**human dornase alfa**).

### Antibiotics

- **Co-amoxiclav** (amoxicillin + clavulanic acid):
  - Preferred as first antibiotic.
- Fluoroquinolone.
- Azithromycin.

### Prophylaxis for Pseudomonas infection

- Inhaled tobramycin or inhaled aztreonam.
- Oral azithromycin 3 times a week:
  - Decreases colonization.

## Dietary measures

- Diet:
  - High fat
  - High protein
  - High calories
- Salt supplementation, especially during dehydration.
- Pancreatic enzyme supplements:
  - Enterically coated microspheres.
  - Cornerstone of gastrointestinal management in cystic fibrosis.

---

### Fat-soluble vitamin supplementation

- Vitamins **A, D, E & K**.
- Vitamins A and D are the most important supplements.

# CFTR Modulators

Increase the activity of the CFTR channel by modulating it.

1. **Ivacaftor:** >2 years of age.
2. **Ivacaftor + Lumacaftor:** >6 years of age.
   - Useful in **ΔF508** mutation cases.
3. **Ivacaftor + Tezacaftor:** >12 years of age.
4. **Triple combination therapy:**
   - Ivacaftor + Tezacaftor + Elexacaftor.
   - Covers most of the mutations.
   - Most promising evolving treatment.

---

## Fetal Circulation & Intro To Congenital Heart Diseases


### Fetal Circulation & Intro To
### Congenital Heart Diseases
Fetal circulation
- Grcais, previcts, gaugen feylumblical ver? and
nutrients to the baby as the lungs aren't yet Functional.
- High pulmonary vascular resistance. => gine Lays one wot fete
- Quctus venosus.
- Foramen ovole.
- Ductus arteriosus.
The umbilical artery carries deoxygenated blood,
The umbilical vein carries oxygenated blood. The ductus
venosus connects the umbilical vein to \VC and bypasses the

---

### Fetal blood flow

- Deoxygenated blood from the SVC enters the **right atrium**.
- Oxygenated blood from the umbilical vein also enters the right atrium.
- **1/3 blood (oxygenated)** passes through the **foramen ovale** → LA → LV → aorta → descending aorta.
- **2/3 blood (mixed)** passes through the **tricuspid valve** → RV → pulmonary artery → lungs.
- **Ductus arteriosus** connects the pulmonary artery to the aorta.

| Site | Oxygen saturation |
|---|---:|
| Umbilical vein | 80% |
| Ductus venosus | 75% |
| IVC | 70% |
| LV | 70% |
| RV | 55% |
| Descending aorta | 55% (50–60%) |
| Umbilical artery | 55% (50–60%) |

---

### Circulatory changes at birth

**At birth:**

- Placenta is cut off → closure of umbilical vessels → closure of ductus venosus.
- Baby starts breathing → oxygen enters the lungs → decreased pulmonary vascular resistance → decreased RA/RV pressure.
- Clamping of the umbilical cord → removal of low-pressure placental circulation → increased systemic vascular resistance (increased pressure in the aorta) → increased LA/LV pressure.
- Decreased RA pressure + increased LA pressure → closure of foramen ovale.
- Decreased pulmonary vascular resistance + increased systemic vascular resistance → closure of ductus arteriosus.

| Structure | Functional closure | Structural closure after birth (anatomical) | Remnant |
|---|---|---|---|
| Umbilical vessels | At/soon after birth | 5–10 days | Umbilical vein: **ligamentum teres**; umbilical artery: **medial umbilical ligament** |
| Ductus venosus | At/soon after birth | 3–7 days | **Ligamentum venosum** |
| Foramen ovale | At/soon after birth | 3 months up to few years (structurally patent foramen ovale may persist) | **Fossa ovalis** |
| Ductus arteriosus | 10–14 hours after birth | 10–21 days | **Ligamentum arteriosum** |

---


## Congenital Heart Diseases

### High-yield overview

- **Prevalence:** commonly reported around 6–8 per 1,000 live births, with variation by population and ascertainment.
- Most CHD has a **multifactorial/polygenic** basis; chromosomal and single-gene disorders account for a smaller proportion.
- **Most common CHD:** ventricular septal defect (VSD).
- **Most common cyanotic CHD:** tetralogy of Fallot (TOF).

### Important syndromic and exposure associations

| Association | Important cardiac lesion(s) |
|---|---|
| **Down syndrome (trisomy 21)** | Atrioventricular septal defect / endocardial cushion defect; VSD |
| **Turner syndrome** | Coarctation of aorta; bicuspid aortic valve |
| **Noonan syndrome** | Pulmonary valve stenosis; hypertrophic cardiomyopathy |
| **Edwards syndrome (trisomy 18)** | VSD and complex CHD |
| **Patau syndrome (trisomy 13)** | VSD and complex CHD |
| **22q11.2 deletion / DiGeorge syndrome** | Conotruncal defects |
| **Williams syndrome** | Supravalvular aortic stenosis |
| **Holt–Oram syndrome** | ASD/VSD with radial-ray abnormalities |
| **Alagille syndrome** | Peripheral pulmonary artery stenosis |
| **Maternal diabetes** | Increased risk of CHD, including VSD and TGA |
| **Congenital rubella** | PDA; pulmonary artery stenosis |
| **Maternal SLE (anti-Ro/SSA, anti-La/SSB)** | Congenital heart block / conduction disease |
| **Lithium exposure** | Classically associated with Ebstein anomaly |

### Clinical screening clues for CHD

The source uses a Nadas-style murmur/cyanosis framework.

**Major warning signs**
- Systolic murmur **grade ≥3/6**.
- **Any diastolic murmur**.
- **Cyanosis**.
- **Congestive cardiac failure**.

**Minor warning signs**
- Systolic murmur below grade 3 when clinically concerning.
- Abnormal oxygen saturation.
- Abnormal chest radiograph.
- Abnormal blood pressure.
- Abnormal ECG.

### Murmur terminology

- **Pansystolic / holosystolic murmur:** heard throughout systole; classically associated with VSD, mitral regurgitation and tricuspid regurgitation.
- **Ejection systolic murmur:** associated with outflow obstruction such as aortic stenosis or pulmonary stenosis.
