- Soft systolic murmurs can occur in high-flow states such as fever or anemia; clinical context is essential.

### Central vs peripheral cyanosis

**Cyanosis** results from severe hypoxia (**SpO₂ < 80%; hypoxia < 94%**).

| | Central cyanosis | Peripheral cyanosis |
|---|---|---|
| **Reason** | Associated with CHD. Cyanosis due to: 1. Mixing (right → left); 2. Obstruction to pulmonary blood flow. | Due to conditions leading to vasoconstriction of limbs → decreased oxygen delivery to tissues. |
| **Site** | Mucous membranes, extremities. | Extremities (hands/feet). |

---


### Page 266

## Acyanotic Congenital Heart Defects


### Overview

Acyanotic congenital heart disease is dominated by:

1. **Left-to-right shunt lesions** → increased pulmonary blood flow.
   - Atrial septal defect (ASD)
   - Ventricular septal defect (VSD)
   - Patent ductus arteriosus (PDA)
2. **Obstructive lesions** → obstruction to systemic outflow.
   - Coarctation of the aorta

### Ventricular septal defect (VSD)

- **Most common congenital cardiac defect.**
- Major anatomical types:
  - **Perimembranous VSD** — most common type.
  - Muscular VSD — relatively high chance of spontaneous closure.
  - Outlet/subaortic (supracristal) VSD — associated with aortic regurgitation; spontaneous closure is uncommon.
  - Inlet VSD — associated with atrioventricular septal-defect spectrum lesions.
- Left ventricular pressure exceeds right ventricular pressure → **left-to-right shunt**.
- A significant VSD produces a **pansystolic murmur** at the lower left sternal border.
- A large, non-restrictive VSD may have a softer murmur despite a large shunt because the pressure difference is smaller.
- Increased pulmonary blood flow may produce a pulmonary flow murmur and a mid-diastolic flow murmur across the mitral valve.

### Hemodynamic consequences of VSD

- Large left-to-right shunt → pulmonary overcirculation → left atrial and left ventricular volume overload.
- Cardiomegaly may develop, with an enlarged cardiac silhouette and prominent apex from left ventricular enlargement.
- **Congestive cardiac failure** commonly appears during early infancy as pulmonary vascular resistance falls.
- Long-standing large shunts can lead to pulmonary vascular disease and eventually **Eisenmenger syndrome** with shunt reversal and cyanosis.

### VSD complications

- Pulmonary overcirculation and recurrent respiratory infections.
- Congestive heart failure.
- Infective endocarditis risk.
- Aortic regurgitation in selected outlet VSDs.
- Eisenmenger syndrome with an unrepaired large shunt.

### VSD treatment

- Small asymptomatic VSDs may be observed because spontaneous closure can occur.
- Medical therapy for symptomatic heart failure may include diuretics and other heart-failure-directed therapy.
- Closure is considered for significant hemodynamic shunts, persistent symptoms, important ventricular enlargement or selected associated lesions.
- **Surgical patch closure** or **device closure** may be used depending on anatomy.
- **Do not close an established Eisenmenger VSD** because pulmonary vascular disease is generally irreversible and closure can be harmful.

---

### Atrial septal defect (ASD)

### Types

1. **Ostium secundum ASD** — most common type.
2. Ostium primum ASD — part of the atrioventricular septal-defect spectrum; may be associated with mitral/left AV-valve abnormalities and conduction defects.
3. Sinus venosus ASD.
4. Coronary sinus ASD — rare.

### Pathophysiology

- Left atrial pressure slightly exceeds right atrial pressure → **left-to-right atrial shunt**.
- Because the atrial pressure difference is small, the ASD itself usually does **not** produce a loud shunt murmur.
- Increased flow through the right ventricle and pulmonary valve produces an **ejection systolic flow murmur**.
- Characteristic finding: **wide, fixed splitting of S2** due to persistently increased right-sided flow and delayed pulmonary valve closure.

### ASD investigations

- Ostium secundum ASD often shows **right-axis deviation/right ventricular volume overload**.
- Ostium primum ASD may show **left-axis deviation**, often related to associated conduction-system involvement.
- Echocardiography defines the anatomy and shunt.

### ASD complications and treatment

- Many small ASDs close spontaneously.
- Significant persistent ASDs may cause right-sided volume overload and later complications.
- Closure may be surgical or catheter-based depending on size and anatomy.
- Device closure is commonly used for suitable secundum ASDs.

---

### Patent ductus arteriosus (PDA)

- Persistent communication between the **descending aorta and pulmonary artery**.
- Left-to-right shunt after birth → increased pulmonary blood flow.
- Characteristic **continuous "machinery" murmur**.
- A large PDA can cause heart failure and pulmonary vascular disease.
- In Eisenmenger physiology, shunt reversal may cause **differential cyanosis**: lower limbs become cyanotic while the right upper limb remains relatively better oxygenated.

### PDA closure in preterm infants

- Pharmacologic closure may be attempted in selected symptomatic preterm infants.
- **Ibuprofen or indomethacin** are cyclooxygenase inhibitors used for closure.
- Ibuprofen is often preferred because of a more favorable renal/gastrointestinal adverse-effect profile in many protocols.
- Contraindications and treatment selection depend on renal function, platelet count, bleeding, necrotizing enterocolitis and overall clinical status.
- Persistent symptomatic PDA may require catheter-based device closure or surgery.

---

### Coarctation of the aorta

- Obstructive narrowing of the aorta, classically near the **ductal insertion** at the junction of the arch and descending thoracic aorta.

### Clinical features

**Neonates with critical coarctation**
- Poor systemic perfusion when the ductus closes.
- Shock, metabolic acidosis and weak/absent femoral pulses.

**Older children**
- Upper-limb hypertension.
- **Radio-femoral delay.**
- Weak femoral pulses.
- Exercise-related lower-limb symptoms or claudication in significant obstruction.

### Radiology

- **Inferior rib notching** from enlarged collateral intercostal arteries in older children.
- **"3 sign"** on chest radiograph from pre- and post-stenotic aortic contour changes.
- Barium swallow can produce a **reverse-3/E sign** from esophageal indentation.

### Management

- In a critically ill neonate, **prostaglandin E1** can maintain ductal patency while definitive intervention is arranged.
- Definitive treatment depends on age and anatomy and may involve surgical repair or catheter-based balloon/stent intervention.

---

### Page 277

## Cyanotic Congenital Heart Defects

### Major mechanisms

1. **Reduced pulmonary blood flow** because of right-sided outflow obstruction.
2. **Mixing lesions** in which systemic venous and pulmonary venous blood mix before systemic ejection.

### Tetralogy of Fallot (TOF)

Four components:

1. **Ventricular septal defect (VSD).**
2. **Right ventricular outflow tract obstruction / pulmonary stenosis.**
3. **Overriding aorta.**
4. **Right ventricular hypertrophy.**

- Severity of cyanosis is largely determined by the degree of RV outflow obstruction.
- Right-to-left shunting across the VSD produces systemic desaturation when obstruction is significant.
- The murmur is usually generated by the **RV outflow obstruction**, not by the large VSD itself.
- A **single loud S2** may occur because the pulmonary component is soft or absent.

### TOF complications

- Chronic cyanosis → secondary erythrocytosis/polycythemia and hyperviscosity.
- Thromboembolic complications.
- Brain abscess from paradoxical septic embolization.
- Infective endocarditis.
- **Hypercyanotic (Tet) spells.**

### Hypercyanotic spell

Often precipitated by crying, feeding, defecation or exertion.

Management principles:

- **Knee-chest/squatting position** → increases systemic vascular resistance and reduces right-to-left shunting.
- Oxygen.
- Calm the child; **morphine** can reduce sympathetic drive and hyperventilation.
- **Beta-blockade** (e.g. propranolol/esmolol) may reduce infundibular spasm.
- **Phenylephrine** can increase systemic vascular resistance in severe spells.
- Correct severe metabolic acidosis when indicated.

### TOF treatment

- Definitive repair is surgical and timing depends on anatomy and clinical severity.
- Palliative systemic-to-pulmonary shunts may be used when definitive repair is not immediately appropriate.
- Classical and modified **Blalock–Taussig shunts** are important historical/high-yield examples.

---

### Tricuspid atresia

- Absence/atresia of the tricuspid valve → no direct flow from right atrium to right ventricle.
- **Hypoplastic right ventricle** is typical.
- An ASD/PFO is essential for systemic venous blood to reach the left atrium.
- A VSD and/or PDA may be required for pulmonary blood flow.
- Cyanosis is common and depends on pulmonary blood flow.
- **Left-axis deviation** on ECG is a classic exam clue.

### Management

- If pulmonary blood flow is duct-dependent, give **prostaglandin E1**.
- Definitive management is staged palliation in suitable anatomy, generally progressing through a Glenn-type circulation and ultimately Fontan-type physiology.

---

### Ebstein anomaly

- Downward displacement of the tricuspid valve → **atrialization of part of the right ventricle**.
- Tricuspid regurgitation is common.
- Right-to-left atrial shunting can cause cyanosis.
- **Maternal lithium exposure** is a classic association.
- Arrhythmias, including **WPW/pre-excitation**, are well recognized.

### Management

- Stabilization and prostaglandin E1 may be required in duct-dependent neonatal presentations.
- Surgical strategy depends on anatomy and severity; **cone repair** is an important valve-repair approach in suitable patients.

---

### Truncus arteriosus

- A **single arterial trunk** arises from the heart and receives blood from both ventricles, usually with an associated large VSD.
- Complete mixing produces variable cyanosis.
- Pulmonary blood flow is often increased → early heart failure.
- It is a **conotruncal defect**.
- Definitive treatment is early surgical repair, classically involving VSD closure and separation of pulmonary from systemic outflow; a Rastelli-type conduit is used in relevant surgical strategies.

---

### Hypoplastic left heart syndrome (HLHS)

- Severe underdevelopment of left-sided cardiac structures, commonly involving **mitral and/or aortic atresia/stenosis with hypoplastic left ventricle and ascending aorta**.
- Systemic circulation becomes **duct-dependent**.
- As the ductus closes, systemic perfusion deteriorates → shock, acidosis and end-organ hypoperfusion.
- An atrial-level communication is important for pulmonary venous blood to reach the systemic circulation.

### Management

- **Immediate prostaglandin E1** to maintain ductal patency.
- Urgent specialist cardiac management.
- Staged palliation, beginning with the **Norwood procedure** in appropriate candidates.

---

### Transposition of the great arteries (TGA)

- Aorta arises from the **right ventricle** and pulmonary artery from the **left ventricle**.
- This produces **parallel circulation** rather than normal series circulation.
- Survival depends on adequate mixing through an ASD/PFO, VSD and/or PDA.

### Clinical forms

- **TGA with intact ventricular septum:** severe cyanosis soon after birth.
- **TGA with VSD:** more mixing and often less severe cyanosis, but increased pulmonary blood flow can cause heart failure.

### Management

- **Prostaglandin E1** to maintain ductal patency and improve mixing when necessary.
- **Balloon atrial septostomy (Rashkind procedure)** when interatrial mixing is inadequate.
- **Arterial switch (Jatene) procedure** is the definitive repair for typical dextro-TGA and is performed early in life when appropriate.

---

### Total anomalous pulmonary venous connection (TAPVC/TAPVR)

- Pulmonary veins drain abnormally to the systemic venous/right-atrial circulation rather than directly to the left atrium.
- An **ASD/PFO is obligatory** for systemic output.

### Types

1. **Supracardiac** — pulmonary veins → vertical vein → innominate vein → SVC.
2. **Cardiac** — drainage via the coronary sinus/right atrium.
3. **Infracardiac** — drainage via the IVC/hepatic/portal venous system; obstruction is common.
4. **Mixed** — more than one pathway.

### Clinical features

- Increased pulmonary blood flow and variable cyanosis.
- **Obstructed infracardiac TAPVC** can present with severe respiratory distress and marked pulmonary edema soon after birth.
- Unobstructed supracardiac TAPVC may produce a classic **snowman/figure-of-8** appearance later in infancy when venous structures enlarge.

### Management

- **Obstructed TAPVC is a surgical emergency.**
- Prostaglandin E1 is not a definitive treatment for severe obstructed TAPVC.
- Definitive surgical repair is required; timing depends on obstruction and clinical condition.

---

### Page 289

## Acute Rheumatic Fever


### Acute rheumatic fever (ARF)

- ARF is a **post–Group A streptococcal** inflammatory disease caused by an abnormal immune response to recent infection.
- **Molecular mimicry** is an important mechanism: immune responses directed against streptococcal antigens can cross-react with host tissues.
- It most commonly affects **school-aged children and adolescents**.
- Typical latency after pharyngitis is **about 2–4 weeks**; chorea may have a longer latent period.

### Revised Jones criteria — high-yield framework

Diagnosis of a first episode requires:

**Evidence of preceding Group A streptococcal infection** **plus** either:

- **2 major manifestations**, or
- **1 major + 2 minor manifestations**.

The 2015 AHA revision distinguishes low-risk from moderate/high-risk populations. India is generally treated as a moderate/high-risk setting for exam purposes.

### Major manifestations

| Low-risk population | Moderate/high-risk population |
|---|---|
| Carditis (clinical or subclinical) | Carditis (clinical or subclinical) |
| Polyarthritis | **Monoarthritis or polyarthritis**, and polyarthralgia may also qualify in high-risk settings |
| Sydenham chorea | Sydenham chorea |
| Erythema marginatum | Erythema marginatum |
| Subcutaneous nodules | Subcutaneous nodules |

### Minor manifestations

- **Fever:** ≥38.5°C in low-risk populations; ≥38.0°C in moderate/high-risk populations.
- **Polyarthralgia** in low-risk populations; **monoarthralgia** in moderate/high-risk populations.
- Elevated inflammatory markers (ESR and/or CRP), with population-specific thresholds.
- Prolonged PR interval on ECG, provided it is not already counted as part of carditis.

> **Exam point:** A Doppler echocardiogram should be obtained in suspected/confirmed ARF because **subclinical carditis** can satisfy a major criterion in the revised Jones criteria.

### Evidence of preceding streptococcal infection

- Rising/elevated **ASO titer** or anti-DNase B antibody.
- Positive throat culture or rapid antigen testing where appropriate.
- A history compatible with recent streptococcal pharyngitis can support the diagnosis, but laboratory evidence is preferred when available.

### Carditis

- Can involve the **endocardium/valves, myocardium and pericardium**.
- **Mitral regurgitation** is the classic valvular lesion.
- Aortic valve involvement can occur, often with mitral disease.
- Clinical findings may include:
  - Tachycardia out of proportion to fever.
  - New murmur, especially of mitral regurgitation.
  - Cardiomegaly and heart failure in severe disease.
  - Pericarditic chest pain/rub when pericarditis is present.
- **Echocardiography/Doppler** is important for detecting both clinical and subclinical valvulitis.

### Arthritis

- Typically **migratory large-joint arthritis** involving knees, ankles, elbows or wrists.
- Often responds dramatically to salicylates/NSAIDs.
- Does not usually produce permanent joint damage.

### Erythema marginatum

- Uncommon.
- Nonscarring, nonpruritic, erythematous lesions with central clearing.
- Usually affects the trunk and proximal limbs and typically spares the face.

### Subcutaneous nodules

- Rare and usually associated with significant carditis.
- Small, painless, firm nodules over bony prominences or extensor surfaces.

### Sydenham chorea

- May appear **months after the precipitating streptococcal infection**.
- Characterized by involuntary, purposeless/jerky movements and impaired coordination.
- Associated features:
  - Emotional lability.
  - Hypotonia.
  - Dysarthria/jerky speech.
  - **Milkmaid grip**.
  - **Darting tongue**.
- Often self-limited but may require treatment when movements are functionally significant.

### Treatment of the acute episode

#### 1. Eradicate streptococci

- **Benzathine penicillin G** or an appropriate oral penicillin regimen is standard.
- Use an alternative such as a macrolide when there is a significant penicillin allergy, following local guidance.

#### 2. Control inflammation

- **Aspirin/NSAIDs** are commonly used for arthritis.
- Corticosteroids may be considered in significant carditis, particularly when heart failure/inflammation is substantial.

#### 3. Heart failure

- Managed according to severity with appropriate diuretics, afterload reduction and supportive therapy.

#### 4. Chorea

- Mild cases may need reassurance and observation.
- More severe cases may be treated with agents such as **valproate or other specialist-directed anti-choreic therapy**.

### Secondary prophylaxis

Goal: prevent recurrent attacks of ARF and progression of rheumatic heart disease.

- **Benzathine penicillin G IM** at regular intervals is the preferred regimen in many guidelines.
- Oral penicillin V is an alternative when appropriate; macrolides can be used in true penicillin allergy according to local guidance.
- Duration depends on whether carditis and/or residual valvular disease occurred during the previous episode.


---

### Page 294

## Congenital Anomalies of Kidney & Urinary Tract (CAKUT)


### Major groups

1. **Renal parenchymal anomalies** — agenesis, dysplasia, hypoplasia, cystic disorders.
2. **Anomalies of renal fusion/position** — horseshoe kidney and related ectopia/fusion anomalies.
3. **Upper urinary-tract anomalies** — ureteric obstruction, duplication and reflux.
4. **Distal urinary-tract anomalies** — bladder and urethral abnormalities.

### Multicystic dysplastic kidney

- Usually unilateral.
- Nonfunctioning kidney replaced by multiple noncommunicating cysts and dysplastic tissue.
- The opposite kidney may show compensatory hypertrophy.
- Many cases show spontaneous involution over childhood.
- Antenatal/postnatal ultrasonography is the main diagnostic tool.

### Renal agenesis

- **Unilateral renal agenesis:** one kidney is absent; the contralateral kidney often hypertrophies and renal function may remain normal.
- **Bilateral renal agenesis:** severe oligohydramnios → **Potter sequence**.

### Potter sequence

- Severe reduction/absence of fetal urine → oligohydramnios.
- Reduced amniotic fluid causes fetal compression and characteristic facies.
- **Pulmonary hypoplasia** is the major life-threatening consequence.
- Typical facies may include low-set ears, flattened/broad nose and receding chin.
- Limb positioning abnormalities such as clubfoot may occur.

---

### Autosomal recessive polycystic kidney disease (ARPKD)

- **Autosomal recessive** cystic kidney disease.
- Caused by pathogenic variants in **PKHD1**, encoding fibrocystin/polyductin.
- Both kidneys are affected by collecting-duct dilatation and fibrosis.
- Associated **congenital hepatic fibrosis** is characteristic.

### Clinical features

- Bilateral enlarged, echogenic kidneys.
- Hypertension from renal ischemia/renin activation.
- Variable renal impairment; severe neonatal disease can cause pulmonary hypoplasia.
- Congenital hepatic fibrosis may lead to portal hypertension and splenomegaly later in childhood.

### Ultrasound

- Enlarged **hyperechoic kidneys**.
- Poor corticomedullary differentiation.

### Management

- Control hypertension and monitor renal function.
- Manage complications of chronic kidney disease and portal hypertension.
- Advanced kidney failure may require **kidney transplantation**; combined liver-kidney transplantation is reserved for selected severe disease with advanced renal and hepatic complications.

---

### Horseshoe kidney

- Fusion of the lower poles of the kidneys, usually across the midline through an isthmus.
- Often asymptomatic and discovered incidentally.
- Associated with **Turner syndrome** and other chromosomal/congenital disorders.

### Complications

- Ureteropelvic junction obstruction.
- Hydronephrosis.
- Recurrent urinary tract infection.
- Renal calculi.
- Increased risk of some renal tumors, including Wilms tumor, compared with the general population.

---

## Distal urinary tract anomalies

### Obstructive uropathy

Important causes include:

- **Ureteropelvic junction (UPJ) obstruction**.
- **Posterior urethral valves (PUV)**.
- Severe bladder outlet anomalies.
- Prune belly/Eagle–Barrett syndrome with associated urinary-tract abnormalities.

### Hydronephrosis

- May present antenatally on ultrasound or postnatally as an abdominal/flank mass.
- Older children may have abdominal/flank pain, vomiting or recurrent UTIs.
- Evaluation is guided by ultrasound and, where indicated, functional renal imaging or VCUG.

### Ureteropelvic junction obstruction

- Common cause of antenatal hydronephrosis.
- Usually due to abnormal peristalsis/structure at the UPJ.
- Often unilateral.
- Severe neonatal cases present with hydronephrosis; older children may have episodic flank/abdominal pain and vomiting (**Dietl crisis**).
- Selected mild cases improve spontaneously.
- Persistent significant obstruction may require **Anderson–Hynes dismembered pyeloplasty**.

### Posterior urethral valves (PUV)

- Occur **only in males**.
- Most common severe congenital cause of bladder outlet obstruction in boys.
- Abnormal posterior urethral folds obstruct urinary outflow.

### Consequences

- Thick-walled/trabeculated bladder.
- Hydroureteronephrosis.
- Recurrent UTI.
- Progressive renal dysfunction and chronic kidney disease in severe disease.

### Diagnosis

- **Voiding/micturating cystourethrogram (VCUG/MCUG)** is diagnostic.
- Antenatal ultrasound may show bilateral hydronephrosis, distended bladder and oligohydramnios in severe disease.

### Management

- Immediate bladder drainage, usually with an appropriately sized urethral catheter.
- **Endoscopic valve ablation** is definitive treatment when feasible.
- Temporary urinary diversion may be considered in selected severe cases when definitive ablation is not immediately possible.

---

### Prune belly (Eagle–Barrett) syndrome

Classic triad:

1. Deficient abdominal wall musculature → wrinkled abdominal skin.
2. Urinary-tract dilatation/malformations.
3. **Cryptorchidism** in males.

Associated findings may include megaureter, megacystis, vesicoureteric reflux and pulmonary abnormalities.

### Bladder exstrophy

- Bladder mucosa is exposed through an infraumbilical abdominal-wall defect.
- Often associated with **epispadias** and pelvic-ring abnormalities.
- **Cloacal exstrophy** is a more severe related anomaly involving bowel and urinary tract abnormalities.

---

## Vesicoureteric reflux (VUR) and reflux nephropathy

- Abnormal retrograde flow of urine from bladder to ureter/kidney.
- Usually related to an abnormally short/inadequate intramural ureteric tunnel; may be primary/familial or secondary to bladder dysfunction/obstruction.
- Associated with recurrent febrile UTIs and renal scarring.

### International reflux grading

| Grade | Description |
|---|---|
| I | Reflux into ureter only |
| II | Reflux reaches renal pelvis without dilatation |
| III | Mild/moderate ureteric and renal pelvic dilatation |
| IV | Moderate ureteric tortuosity/dilatation and calyceal changes |
| V | Gross dilatation and tortuosity with marked calyceal distortion |

### Complications

- Recurrent pyelonephritis.
- **Reflux nephropathy** with renal scarring.
- Hypertension.
- Chronic kidney disease in severe bilateral disease.

### Management principles

- Many low-grade cases resolve spontaneously with growth.
- Prevent and promptly treat febrile UTIs.
- Antibiotic prophylaxis is selectively used, especially in young children with recurrent infections or higher-grade reflux, according to current nephrology/urology guidance.
- Persistent breakthrough infections, high-grade VUR or significant renal damage may lead to consideration of ureteric reimplantation or endoscopic intervention.

---

### Page 301

## Glomerulonephritis

### Acute post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis (APSGN)

**Hallmark:** acute nephritic syndrome with hematuria, edema and hypertension, often with oliguria.

- Urine may appear **cola/tea coloured**.
- Microscopy commonly shows dysmorphic RBCs and **RBC casts**.
- Proteinuria is usually subnephrotic but can occasionally be heavier.
- Common trigger: nephritogenic **Group A Streptococcus**.
  - Following pharyngitis: typically a latency of about 1–3 weeks.
  - Following skin infection/pyoderma: typically a longer latency of about 3–6 weeks.

### Pathophysiology

Streptococcal antigens and antibodies form immune complexes that activate complement and cause glomerular inflammation.

### Investigations

- **Serum C3:** typically reduced during the acute phase.
- Streptococcal serology: ASO and/or anti-DNase B depending on the preceding infection.
- Renal function tests when indicated.
- Renal biopsy is usually unnecessary when the clinical picture is classic and recovery occurs as expected.

### Renal biopsy findings, when performed

- Light microscopy: diffuse proliferative/exudative glomerulonephritis.
- Immunofluorescence: granular deposits of **IgG/IgM/C3** along capillary loops and mesangium.
- Electron microscopy: characteristic **subepithelial "humps"**.

### Management

- Mainly **supportive**.
- Control edema, hypertension and fluid overload.
- Eradicate any residual streptococcal infection when appropriate; antibiotics do not reverse established glomerulonephritis but are used to eliminate the organism and prevent spread/transmission.

### Expected course

- C3 usually returns toward normal within approximately **6–8 weeks**.
- Microscopic hematuria may persist for months.
- Persistent low C3, nephrotic-range proteinuria, progressive renal dysfunction or atypical features should prompt reconsideration of the diagnosis.

---

### IgA nephropathy

- Also called **Berger disease**.
- Recurrent episodes of visible hematuria often occur **within days of an upper respiratory infection** ("synpharyngitic" hematuria), unlike APSGN which has a longer latent period.
- Serum complement is usually normal.
- Renal biopsy demonstrates **mesangial IgA deposition** with mesangial proliferation of variable severity.
- A proportion of children progress to chronic kidney disease over time, particularly those with persistent proteinuria, hypertension or reduced kidney function.

### Management principles

- Blood-pressure and proteinuria control, often with **ACE inhibitor/ARB therapy** when indicated.
- Additional immunomodulatory therapy is reserved for selected patients under specialist nephrology care.

---

### Hereditary nephritis (Alport syndrome)

- Most commonly **X-linked** due to pathogenic variants affecting type IV collagen, especially **COL4A5**.
- Other inheritance patterns include autosomal recessive and autosomal dominant forms.

### Classic features

1. **Hematuria** — usually the earliest and most consistent renal manifestation.
2. **Sensorineural hearing loss**.
3. **Ocular abnormalities**, especially anterior lenticonus.

Other findings can include macular flecks and progressive proteinuria.

### Diagnosis

- Genetic testing is increasingly preferred when available.
- Kidney biopsy may show characteristic basement-membrane abnormalities with thinning, splitting and a **basket-weave** pattern on electron microscopy.

### Management

- Renin–angiotensin system blockade can reduce proteinuria and delay progression of kidney disease.
- Hearing and ocular surveillance is important.
- Kidney transplantation is definitive renal replacement therapy for end-stage kidney disease.

---

### Page 305

## Nephrotic Syndrome

### Definition

**Nephrotic syndrome** is characterized by heavy proteinuria with hypoalbuminemia and/or edema.

Common pediatric thresholds include:

- Urinary protein excretion **>40 mg/m²/hour**.
- Spot urine protein/creatinine ratio **≥2 mg/mg**.
- Dipstick proteinuria commonly **3+ or 4+**.
- Hypoalbuminemia is usually present.
- Hyperlipidemia commonly accompanies the syndrome but is not required as the defining criterion.

### Minimal change disease

- **Most common cause of childhood nephrotic syndrome.**
- Light microscopy: essentially normal glomeruli.
- Electron microscopy: **diffuse foot-process effacement**.
- Usually highly steroid responsive.

### Other important causes

- Focal segmental glomerulosclerosis (FSGS).
- Membranous nephropathy.
- Membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis/C3 glomerulopathy spectrum in selected patients.
- Genetic/syndromic nephrotic syndromes, especially in congenital/infantile disease.

### Treatment terminology

- **Complete remission:** urine protein becomes negative/trace or the UPCR falls to the accepted remission threshold for consecutive days.
- **Relapse:** recurrence of significant proteinuria after remission.
- **Frequently relapsing nephrotic syndrome (FRNS)** and **steroid-dependent nephrotic syndrome (SDNS)** are defined using standardized relapse patterns rather than a single isolated relapse.


---

### Page 310

## Inherited Tubular Disorders


### Common features

- Tubular disorders impair renal handling of electrolytes and water.
- Common manifestations include **polyuria, polydipsia, failure to thrive, electrolyte abnormalities and acid-base disturbances**.
- Many inherited salt-losing tubulopathies produce **hypokalemic, hypochloremic metabolic alkalosis**.

### Comparison

| Disorder | Main site / defect | Typical clues | Blood pressure |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Bartter syndrome** | Thick ascending limb; defects involving the Na⁺-K⁺-2Cl⁻ transporter (NKCC2), ROMK or related chloride transport pathways | Salt wasting, polyuria; **hypercalciuria** is typical of classic loop-type forms | Normal/low |
| **Gitelman syndrome** | Distal convoluted tubule; **Na⁺-Cl⁻ cotransporter (NCC)** defect | Later presentation; **hypomagnesemia + hypocalciuria** | Normal/low |
| **Liddle syndrome** | Collecting duct; **gain-of-function ENaC** mutation | Sodium retention, **hypokalemic metabolic alkalosis**, low renin and low aldosterone | **High** |

### Bartter syndrome

- Usually **autosomal recessive**.
- Presents most often in infancy or childhood; severe forms may present antenatally/neonatally.
- Defect in salt reabsorption in the **thick ascending limb of the loop of Henle**.
- Typical consequences:
  - NaCl and water loss → volume depletion.
  - Activation of renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system.
  - Increased distal K⁺ and H⁺ secretion → **hypokalemic, hypochloremic metabolic alkalosis**.
  - **Hypercalciuria** and nephrocalcinosis in classic loop forms.
  - Polyuria, polydipsia and failure to thrive.
- Treatment is individualized and includes electrolyte replacement; NSAID/prostaglandin inhibition may be used in selected patients.

### Gitelman syndrome

- Usually **autosomal recessive**.
- Defect in the distal convoluted tubule involving the **NCC transporter**.
- Typical findings:
  - Hypokalemic, hypochloremic metabolic alkalosis.
  - **Hypomagnesemia**.
  - **Hypocalciuria**.
  - Usually a later and milder presentation than severe Bartter syndrome.
- Treatment: magnesium and potassium replacement as required; other therapy is individualized.

### Liddle syndrome

- **Autosomal dominant**.
- Gain-of-function mutation affecting epithelial sodium channel (**ENaC**) activity in the collecting duct.
- Increased Na⁺ reabsorption → volume expansion and **hypertension**.
- Because of suppressed renin and aldosterone:
  - Hypokalemia.
  - Metabolic alkalosis.
  - **Low renin + low aldosterone**.
- **Treatment:** amiloride (or another ENaC blocker).

### High-yield differentiation

- **Bartter:** loop → hypercalciuria.
- **Gitelman:** DCT → hypomagnesemia + hypocalciuria.
- **Liddle:** collecting duct → hypertension + low renin/low aldosterone.

---

### Page 315

## Renal Insufficiency


### Hemolytic uremic syndrome (HUS)

**HUS is characterized by the triad of:**

1. **Microangiopathic hemolytic anemia**.
2. **Thrombocytopenia**.
3. **Acute kidney injury**.

### Typical / diarrhea-associated HUS (STEC-HUS)

- Usually follows a diarrheal illness caused by a **Shiga-toxin-producing organism**, classically Shiga-toxin-producing *E. coli*; *Shigella dysenteriae* type 1 is another recognized cause.
- Typical sequence:
  - Diarrhea, often initially bloody.
  - After several days, pallor, weakness, oliguria and other features of HUS may appear.
- Investigations:
  - CBC: anemia + thrombocytopenia.
  - Peripheral smear: **schistocytes**.
  - Evidence of hemolysis: raised LDH, indirect bilirubin and reticulocytosis as appropriate.
  - Renal dysfunction: raised serum creatinine/urea.
  - Coombs test is usually negative.
  - Stool testing should seek the pathogen and/or **Shiga toxin by culture, PCR or toxin assay**, depending on availability.

### Treatment of STEC-HUS

- Mainly **supportive care**:
  - Careful fluid and electrolyte management.
  - Renal replacement therapy/dialysis when indicated.
  - Management of severe anemia and other complications.
- Routine antibiotics and antimotility agents are **not used to treat STEC infection with the aim of preventing HUS** because of potential harm and lack of demonstrated benefit in established disease.

### Atypical HUS (complement-mediated HUS)

- May be triggered by infection, surgery or other stressors in genetically susceptible children.
- Results from dysregulated **alternative complement pathway** activity.
- Can present with recurrent HUS or severe AKI without a preceding diarrheal illness.
- **Complement inhibition with eculizumab** is an important specific therapy when complement-mediated aHUS is diagnosed or strongly suspected.

### Acute kidney injury (AKI)

**KDIGO definition in children/adults:** AKI is present when there is an increase in serum creatinine of **≥0.3 mg/dL within 48 hours**, or an increase to **≥1.5 times baseline within the prior 7 days**, or reduced urine output meeting stage criteria.

### KDIGO AKI staging

| Stage | Serum creatinine | Urine output |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | 1.5–1.9 × baseline or ≥0.3 mg/dL rise | <0.5 mL/kg/h for 6–12 h |
| **2** | 2.0–2.9 × baseline | <0.5 mL/kg/h for ≥12 h |
| **3** | ≥3 × baseline, or severe creatinine criteria / dialysis | <0.3 mL/kg/h for ≥24 h or anuria ≥12 h |

### Causes of AKI

- **Pre-renal:** dehydration, blood loss and other causes of reduced renal perfusion.
- **Intrinsic renal:** HUS, acute tubular injury, acute interstitial nephritis, glomerular disease and vascular causes.
- **Post-renal:** urinary tract obstruction.

### Management of AKI — principles

- Identify and correct the cause.
- Assess volume status carefully; give isotonic fluid when clinically indicated for hypovolemia.
- Avoid nephrotoxic drugs where possible and adjust drug doses for kidney function.
- Correct major electrolyte and acid-base disturbances.
- Treat hyperkalemia urgently when severe or associated with ECG changes.
- Dialysis/renal replacement therapy is indicated for refractory complications such as severe hyperkalemia, severe acidosis, significant fluid overload, uremic complications or certain toxic ingestions.

### Estimated GFR in children

A commonly used bedside Schwartz approach is:

**eGFR ≈ 0.413 × height (cm) / serum creatinine (mg/dL)**

> The exact equation and calibration should be specified when eGFR is used clinically; older teaching materials may show historic Schwartz constants.

### Chronic kidney disease (CKD)

- CKD is defined by abnormalities of kidney structure or function **present for ≥3 months**.
- Examples include persistently reduced GFR, proteinuria/albuminuria, structural abnormalities or persistent urinary abnormalities attributable to kidney disease.
- In children, **congenital anomalies of the kidney and urinary tract (CAKUT)** are major causes of CKD.

### Important complications of CKD

- Growth failure / short stature.
- Normocytic anemia, largely related to reduced erythropoietin production.
- Mineral and bone disorder:
  - Reduced renal activation of vitamin D.
  - Hyperphosphatemia.
  - Hypocalcemia.
  - Secondary hyperparathyroidism.
- Hypertension and cardiovascular risk.
- Nutrition and electrolyte disturbances.

### Management of CKD

- Adequate nutrition and management of fluid/electrolyte balance.
- Treat anemia appropriately, including erythropoiesis-stimulating therapy when indicated.
- Manage CKD-mineral and bone disorder with dietary measures, phosphate binders, vitamin D analogues and calcium when indicated.
- Control hypertension; **ACE inhibitors/ARBs are particularly useful when significant proteinuria is present**, provided renal function and potassium are monitored.
- Recombinant GH may be considered for persistent growth failure in selected children with CKD.
- **Kidney transplantation** is the preferred definitive renal replacement strategy for suitable children with kidney failure.

---

### Page 323

## Growth Hormone Deficiency and Hypothyroidism


### Growth hormone deficiency (GHD)

#### Types

- **Isolated GHD:** GH deficiency without other pituitary hormone deficiencies.
- **Combined pituitary hormone deficiency / hypopituitarism:** GH deficiency occurs with one or more additional pituitary deficiencies.

#### Causes

- Idiopathic GHD.
- Congenital pituitary/hypothalamic abnormalities, including septo-optic dysplasia or ectopic posterior pituitary.
- Genetic defects affecting GH/GHRH pathways.
- Tumors, especially **craniopharyngioma**.
- Infiltrative disorders such as histiocytosis or sarcoidosis.
- CNS irradiation or trauma.

#### Clinical features

- Usually normal size at birth because GH has a limited role in fetal growth.
- **Reduced height velocity** → progressive short stature.
- Proportionate short stature.
- Delayed bone age and often delayed dentition.
- Increased truncal adiposity / a characteristic youthful or “doll-like” facial appearance may occur.
- In combined pituitary hormone deficiency, associated findings may include:
  - Micropenis/cryptorchidism in boys due to gonadotropin deficiency.
  - Hypoglycemia due to ACTH/GH deficiency.
  - Prolonged neonatal jaundice in severe congenital hypopituitarism.

#### Evaluation

- Serial **height and growth velocity** are essential.
- **IGF-1** and, when useful, **IGFBP-3** support evaluation but must be interpreted with age, puberty and nutritional status.
- A random serum GH concentration is **not diagnostic** because GH secretion is pulsatile.
- **GH stimulation testing** is used when clinical/auxological suspicion is strong; interpretation depends on the test and laboratory context.
- MRI of the hypothalamic-pituitary region is important when structural disease is suspected or treatment is being considered.

#### Treatment

- **Recombinant human GH** by subcutaneous injection for established indications.
- Selected indications may include confirmed GHD, Turner syndrome, selected children with CKD-related growth failure, SHOX deficiency, Noonan syndrome and some other approved conditions.

### Laron syndrome (GH resistance)

- Defect in the **GH receptor/signaling pathway**.
- **GH is high/inappropriately normal but IGF-1 is low**.
- Treatment differs from GHD; **mecasermin (recombinant IGF-1)** may be used in selected children.

### Congenital hypothyroidism

#### Important causes

1. **Thyroid dysgenesis** — agenesis, hypoplasia or ectopy; the commonest category.
2. **Dyshormonogenesis** — inherited defects in thyroid hormone synthesis; often autosomal recessive and may present with goiter.
3. Iodine deficiency/excess in susceptible settings.
4. Transplacental maternal TSH-receptor blocking antibodies.


#### Clinical features

Most newborns are initially asymptomatic.

Later findings may include:

- Lethargy/decreased activity.
- Hypothermia.
- Constipation and delayed meconium passage.
- Prolonged jaundice.
- Coarse facies.
- Hoarse cry.
- Macroglossia.
- Umbilical hernia.
- Poor growth and developmental impairment if untreated.

#### Diagnosis / screening

- Newborn screening generally uses **TSH with or without T4 depending on the program**.
- An abnormal screen must be confirmed using venous **TSH and free T4/total T4** as appropriate.
- In India, ISPAE guidance recommends screening ideally around **48–72 hours** of life, with special considerations for preterm/LBW and sick neonates.
- Thyroid ultrasound and/or radionuclide scan can help determine anatomy/etiology **after biochemical confirmation**, but treatment should not be delayed while waiting for imaging.

#### Treatment

- **Levothyroxine 10–15 μg/kg/day** is the standard neonatal starting range recommended by Indian guidance.
- Treatment should begin promptly once congenital hypothyroidism is confirmed/sufficiently established because early treatment is critical for neurodevelopment.

### Acquired autoimmune hypothyroidism

- Common in older children/adolescents, especially girls.
- **Hashimoto thyroiditis** is a common cause.
- Features:
  - Reduced growth velocity / short stature.
  - Constipation.
  - Dry skin.
  - Weight gain.
  - Goiter may be present and often feels firm/rubbery.
  - Delayed puberty may occur.
- Anti-TPO antibodies support autoimmune thyroiditis.
- Treatment: levothyroxine, with dose individualized to age, weight and biochemical response.

---

### Page 329

## Adrenal Disorders in Children

### Cushing syndrome

#### Causes

- **Exogenous glucocorticoids** are an important cause of Cushing syndrome.
- Endogenous causes are divided into:
  - **ACTH-dependent:** pituitary corticotroph adenoma or ectopic ACTH production.
  - **ACTH-independent:** adrenal adenoma/carcinoma or other autonomous cortisol production.

#### Clinical features in children

- **Growth retardation / reduced height velocity** is a key clue.
- Weight gain with truncal obesity.
- Moon facies.
- Dorsocervical fat pad.
- Wide/purplish abdominal striae.
- Hypertension.
- Proximal muscle weakness.
- Acne/hirsutism may occur, particularly with androgen excess.

#### Evaluation

Screening tests may include:

- 24-hour urinary free cortisol.
- Late-night salivary or serum cortisol where validated.
- Low-dose dexamethasone suppression testing.

Once endogenous Cushing syndrome is established:

- **Plasma ACTH** helps distinguish ACTH-dependent from ACTH-independent disease.
- Additional testing/imaging is then directed at the suspected source.

> The older source material described high-dose dexamethasone suppression as a pituitary-vs-ectopic discriminator. This is a traditional exam concept, but modern evaluation is more nuanced and often relies on specialized endocrine testing and imaging.

#### Treatment

- Treat the underlying cause, usually by definitive treatment of the responsible tumor/source.
- Medical steroidogenesis inhibition may be used when surgery is not immediately possible or as bridge therapy.

### Adrenal insufficiency

#### Primary vs secondary

- **Primary adrenal insufficiency:** adrenal gland is unable to produce adequate cortisol; aldosterone deficiency may also occur.
- **Secondary adrenal insufficiency:** reduced ACTH drive from pituitary/hypothalamic disease; aldosterone is usually preserved.

#### Causes of primary adrenal insufficiency in children

- **Congenital adrenal hyperplasia (CAH)** is an important pediatric cause.
- Autoimmune adrenalitis is more common later in childhood/adolescence and may occur in autoimmune polyglandular syndromes.
- Tuberculosis and other infections.
- Adrenal hemorrhage, including meningococcal sepsis (Waterhouse-Friderichsen syndrome).
- Adrenoleukodystrophy in appropriate clinical settings.

#### Acute adrenal crisis

- Shock/hypotension.
- Hypoglycemia may occur.
- In primary adrenal insufficiency: **hyponatremia and hyperkalemia**.
- **Treatment:** immediate IV/IM hydrocortisone plus appropriate isotonic fluid resuscitation and correction of hypoglycemia/electrolyte abnormalities.

#### Chronic adrenal insufficiency

- Fatigue/lethargy.
- Weight loss.
- Postural hypotension.
- Salt craving.
- Hyperpigmentation in primary adrenal insufficiency.

#### Diagnosis

- Morning cortisol and ACTH can be useful initial tests.
- **Short corticotropin (ACTH) stimulation test** is a key confirmatory test when feasible.
- A low cortisol with high ACTH supports primary adrenal insufficiency.

#### Maintenance treatment

- Glucocorticoid replacement, usually hydrocortisone in children.
- **Fludrocortisone** when mineralocorticoid replacement is required.
- Educate families regarding **stress dosing** and emergency steroid administration.

### Congenital adrenal hyperplasia (CAH)

- A group of **autosomal recessive** disorders caused by defects in adrenal steroid biosynthesis.
- **21-hydroxylase deficiency** is by far the most common form.

#### 21-hydroxylase deficiency

- ↓ Cortisol.
- In salt-wasting disease: ↓ aldosterone → **hyponatremia, hyperkalemia, dehydration and hypotension**.
- ↑ ACTH → adrenal hyperplasia and androgen excess.
- In affected 46,XX infants: **virilization/ambiguous genitalia** may occur.
- In affected 46,XY infants: genital appearance may be normal at birth, with later virilization/precocious puberty if untreated.
- **17-hydroxyprogesterone (17-OHP)** is elevated and is used for newborn screening/diagnosis.
- Treatment generally includes glucocorticoid replacement; mineralocorticoid and salt supplementation are required in salt-wasting disease.

#### 11β-hydroxylase deficiency

- ↓ Cortisol.
- ↑ 11-deoxycorticosterone (DOC) → **hypertension and hypokalemia**.
- Androgen excess → virilization/precocious puberty.
- Hyperpigmentation may occur due to elevated ACTH.

#### 17α-hydroxylase deficiency

- ↓ Cortisol.
- ↓ Androgens.
- Accumulation of mineralocorticoid precursors → **hypertension and hypokalemia**.
- 46,XY individuals may have female-appearing or undervirilized external genitalia.
- Pubertal development is impaired in both sexes.

#### 3β-hydroxysteroid dehydrogenase deficiency

- Rare CAH form causing deficient synthesis of cortisol, aldosterone and sex steroids.
- Salt wasting may occur.
- 46,XY infants may be undervirilized.
- 46,XX infants may have variable genital findings depending on residual enzyme activity and precursor androgen production.

### High-yield CAH approach

- **Salt wasting / hypotension + androgen excess → think 21-hydroxylase deficiency.**
- **Hypertension + androgen excess → think 11β-hydroxylase deficiency.**
- **Hypertension + low/absent androgens → think 17α-hydroxylase deficiency.**
- **High 17-OHP → strongly supports 21-hydroxylase deficiency in the appropriate clinical setting.**

---

### Page 337

## Pubertal Disorders

### Normal pubertal timing

- Puberty normally begins within a broad physiologic range.
- For exam purposes, **precocious puberty** is classically defined as onset of secondary sexual characteristics **before age 8 years in girls or before age 9 years in boys**.

### Delayed puberty

Common clinical thresholds include:

- **Girls:** no breast development by about 13 years, or no menarche by 15 years / prolonged interval after thelarche.
- **Boys:** no testicular enlargement by about 14 years.

The commonest cause of delayed puberty in an otherwise healthy adolescent is **constitutional delay of growth and puberty (CDGP)**.

### Classification of delayed puberty

| Pattern | Gonadotropins | Typical examples |
|---|---|---|
| **Hypogonadotropic hypogonadism** | Low/normal LH and FSH | Constitutional delay, functional hypothalamic suppression, hypothalamic/pituitary disease, Kallmann syndrome, some syndromic disorders |
| **Hypergonadotropic hypogonadism** | High LH and FSH | Turner syndrome, Klinefelter syndrome, primary ovarian/testicular failure |

### Important causes

#### Hypogonadotropic

- Constitutional delay.
- Kallmann syndrome → hypogonadotropic hypogonadism + **anosmia**.
- Functional suppression due to chronic systemic illness, undernutrition or excessive exercise.
- Hypothalamic/pituitary tumors or infiltrative disorders.
- Syndromic disorders such as Prader-Willi syndrome.

#### Hypergonadotropic

- Turner syndrome.
- Klinefelter syndrome.
- Primary ovarian/testicular failure.
- Prior gonadotoxic treatment or other acquired gonadal injury.

### Investigation of delayed puberty

- History and physical examination with **Tanner staging**.
- Growth pattern and bone age.
- LH, FSH and sex steroid levels.
- TSH/free T4 and other tests when systemic/endocrine disease is suspected.
- Consider karyotype in appropriate cases (e.g. suspected Turner/Klinefelter syndrome).
- Further hypothalamic-pituitary imaging when central pathology is suspected.

### Precocious puberty

- **Central precocious puberty (GnRH-dependent):** premature activation of the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis.
- **Peripheral precocious puberty (GnRH-independent):** sex-steroid production independent of normal gonadotropin activation.

#### Central causes

- Idiopathic, especially in girls.
- Hypothalamic hamartoma and other CNS lesions.
- Prior CNS injury/infection or irradiation.

#### Peripheral causes

- Congenital adrenal hyperplasia.
- McCune-Albright syndrome.
- Ovarian/testicular or adrenal tumors.
- Exogenous sex steroids.

### McCune-Albright syndrome

- Usually due to **postzygotic activating GNAS mutations**, causing mosaic endocrine hyperfunction.
- Classic triad:
  - Peripheral precocious puberty.
  - Café-au-lait macules with characteristic irregular “coast of Maine” borders.
  - Polyostotic fibrous dysplasia.
- Other endocrinopathies, such as hyperthyroidism or GH excess, may occur.

### Evaluation of suspected precocious puberty

- History, growth velocity and Tanner staging.
- **Bone age** is often advanced.
- Basal ultrasensitive LH is useful for assessing central activation; GnRH/GnRH-agonist stimulation may be used when basal results are equivocal.
- Pelvic/testicular/adrenal evaluation is guided by the clinical phenotype.
- Brain MRI is particularly important in boys, very young girls and children with neurologic features; current guidance is more selective in older girls without CNS symptoms.

### Treatment

- **Central precocious puberty:** long-acting GnRH agonist therapy (e.g. leuprolide or another approved formulation) when treatment is indicated.
- **Peripheral precocious puberty:** treat the underlying cause.
- Aromatase inhibitors or other steroid-directed therapies may have selected specialist indications but are not the default treatment for all precocious puberty.

### Normal variants

- **Premature thelarche:** isolated breast development without the full progression of puberty.
- **Premature adrenarche:** early adrenal androgen signs without true central puberty.

---


# Rheumatic Diseases of Childhood

## Juvenile dermatomyositis

- It is most common inflammatory myositis in childhood.
- Muscle + skin manifestations.

### Skin manifestations

- **Heliotrope rash:** blue-violet erythematous rash around eyelids + periorbital edema.
- **Gottron's papules:** erythematous, scaly red lesions over dorsal surface of joints (MCP, interphalangeal joints).
- **Shawl sign (associated feature):** erythematous rash over neck & upper anterior chest region.
- **Mechanic hands:** thick erythematous scaly lesions over the fingers and toes.

*Heliotrope rash (1), Gottron's papules (2), Shawl sign (3).*

---

## Diagnostic criteria

Skin features (heliotrope rash and Gottron papules) + any 3 of the following:

1. Symmetrical + proximal muscle weakness (shoulder/thigh).
   - Gower's sign positive (using hands for supporting thigh to get up).
2. Elevation of muscle enzymes (muscle fibers inflamed and destructed) → Creatine kinase, Aldolase.
3. EMG → Short motor unit potentials and fibrillations.
4. Muscle biopsy → Necrosis/inflammation of muscle fibers.

### Serology findings

1. **Myositis associated antibodies (MAA) (non-specific):** SSA, SSB, Anti-dsDNA.
2. **MSA (specific):** Anti-Jo1, Anti-Mi2.

### Treatment

1. Corticosteroids (IV > oral).
2. Methotrexate (maintenance treatment/long term) for 2 years.

## Juvenile idiopathic arthritis (JIA)

**Arthritis:** Intra-articular swelling, and any two of the following:

- Limitation in the joint movement.
- Tenderness/pain while moving joint.
- Warmth of joint (any 2 at least).

It is most common rheumatological arthritis in children.

- **Onset:** <16 years.
- **Duration:** ≥6 weeks.
- Common cause of increased childhood morbidity like school absenteeism, emotional disturbances.

### Subtypes of JIA

- Oligoarticular JIA: ≤4 joints (m/c subtype).
- Polyarticular JIA: ≥4 joints. Both rheumatoid factor (RF) positive and negative.
- Systemic JIA (SJIA): (F = M).
- Psoriatic JIA.
- Enthesitis related JIA (M > F).

---

- All other JIA: F > M.

## Oligoarticular JIA

- Age group is 3 to 5 years.
- ≤4 joints affected in the first 6 months of disease.

### Subdivided into

- **Persistent:** Always ≤4 joints at any time of disease.
- **Extended:** >4 joints affected after 6 months.

- M/c affected joints: knee > ankle > wrist.
- Involvement of hip joint, small joints of hands and feet: rare.
- Extra-articular manifestations: chronic uveitis which may lead to blindness (especially if ANA positive).
- HLA-DR 5/8 positivity is a predisposition for oligoarticular JIA.

## Polyarticular JIA

|  | RF negative | RF positive |
|---|---|---|
| **HLA** | HLA DR-B1 | HLA DR-4, DR-1 |
| **Clinical picture** | Knee, hip, wrist | Large joints and small joints of hand and feet, cervical spine, temporomandibular joint (TMJ) |
| **Age** | Any age | Late childhood and early adolescence (9 to 11 years) |
| **Symmetry** | Asymmetrical involvement | Symmetrical involvement |
| **Association** | ANA positive → Chronic uveitis | Rheumatoid nodules (noticed in extensor surfaces of joints like elbow and occiput, Achilles tendon) |

## Systemic JIA

### Clinical features

1. Fever.
2. Any one of the following:
   - Rash.
   - Hepatosplenomegaly.
   - Lymphadenopathy.
   - Serositis (pleuritis/pericarditis).

---

- Evenings → intermittent fever spikes (1 to 2 per day/quotidian fever) → rash in trunk only during fever (non-pruritic, maculopapular, salmon-pink colored transient rash).

## Psoriatic JIA

Arthritis before/together and after psoriasis.

### Clinical features

1. Psoriasis.
2. If psoriasis not evident, any one of the two:
   - Nail pitting.
   - Dactylitis.
   - First-degree relative with psoriasis.

## Enthesitis related arthritis

- Inflammation of tendons at the site of attachment to the bone.
- Males are more commonly affected than females.
- Onset: older children >8 years.
- Joint: lower limb joints (hip/knee/ankle).
- Asymmetrical involvement.
- HLA B-27 positive.
- Associated with sacroiliitis.

## Investigations of JIA

It is a clinical diagnosis usually.

---

1. **CBC:** Anemia of chronic disease + increased WBC + increased platelets.
2. **X-rays:** Normal in early stages.

   Later stages:
   - Soft tissue swelling around joint.
   - Peri-articular osteopenia.

- Most sensitive investigation for joint involvement: **MRI**.
3. **Serology (antibodies):** for prognostic importance.

## Treatment of JIA

1. **NSAIDs (pain control):** Naproxen, Ibuprofen.
2. **Intra-articular steroids (Triamcinolone)** if not controlled with NSAIDs.
3. **Disease modifying agents:** Methotrexate, Hydroxychloroquine.
4. **Biological agents:**
   - Interleukin 1/IL-1 receptor antagonists: Anakinra.
   - Anti-TNF alpha: Infliximab, Etanercept, Adalimumab.
   - IL-6 receptor antibodies: Tocilizumab.
   - Inhibitors of T-cell activation: Abatacept.

## Henoch-Schonlein purpura

- It is small vessel vasculitis of childhood.
- IgA deposits in vessels → IgA vasculitis.
- Also called as **Purpura rheumatica** and leukocytoclastic vasculitis (debris of neutrophils around blood vessels).
- Affects the age group: 4 to 8 years.

### Features

1. Purpura (non-thrombocytic, palpable, bleeding spots over lower limbs or buttocks) doesn't affect upper part of the body.
2. Arthralgia/arthritis.
3. Post-prandial abdominal pain.
4. Renal manifestations (nephritis) → developed later, initially normal.

- Skin biopsy → IgA deposition.
- **Diagnosis:** Purpura + any one of the 4 features given above.

---

## Investigations

- Platelet count: Normal.
- Initial urine analysis: Normal.
- Follow up needed for 6 months to look for later renal manifestations.
- Prognosis: excellent.

### Complications

- Acute: GI (intussusception).
- Chronic kidney disease.

## Kawasaki disease

- Medium vessel vasculitis.
- M/c vasculitis of childhood in India.
- Age group: <5 years.
- Etiology: Idiopathic/unknown.

### Three phases

|  | Acute phase | Subacute phase | Convalescent/recovery phase |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Duration** | 1 to 2 weeks | 3 to 4 weeks | 1 to 8 weeks |
| **Features** | Prolonged fever >5 days. Other manifestations: mnemonic CREAM: conjunctivitis: bilateral, non-purulent; rash: nonspecific maculopapular rash; extremities: erythema and edema, periungual desquamation (subacute); adenopathy (lymphadenopathy): usually single, cervical; mucosal involvement: cheilitis/glossitis (strawberry tongue). | Periungual desquamation (peeling of the nails). Thrombocytosis. | Recovery from illness. |

---

### Complications

|  | Acute | Subacute |
|---|---|---|
| **Complications** | Acute myocarditis (50–70% incidence) | Coronary artery aneurysms (20–30%), increased risk of mortality if size ≥8 mm |

*A: Conjunctivitis, B: Strawberry tongue, E: Arthritis, F: Periungual desquamation, G: Rash due to reactivation of BCG scar.*

## Diagnostic criteria of Kawasaki disease

- Fever + at least 4/5 features of CREAM.
- If <4 criteria → incomplete Kawasaki disease.
- Echocardiography → if coronary arteries involved, treat like Kawasaki disease.

### Other features

- Reactivation of BCG scar.
- Perianal desquamation.
- USG abdomen → Hydrops/distended gall bladder.
- Sterile pyuria.
- Aseptic meningitis.
- Arthritis.
- Hepatitis.

### Complications

- Acute: myocarditis (50–70%).
- Subacute: coronary aneurysm (20–30%). Increased risk of mortality due to thrombosis and rupture (size ≥8 mm).

## Treatment of Kawasaki disease

1. DOC: IVIG (intravenous immunoglobulin).
2. High-dose aspirin (80 to 100 mg/kg).

---

- Anti-inflammatory.
- Continued till patient afebrile for 48 hours, then we can decrease dosage.
3. Low-dose aspirin (3 to 5 mg/kg/day).
   - Continued till 6 to 8 weeks.
   - After 6 to 8 weeks, echocardiography has to be done.
   - If ECHO normal: Stop aspirin.
   - If coronary abnormalities: Continue aspirin till normalized.

---

### Page 349

# Approach to Anemia in Children and Nutritional Anemia

## Anemia in children

### WHO definition of anaemia

Based on hemoglobin (Hb) levels as per the age group.

| Age | Cut-off hemoglobin (g/dL) |
|---|---:|
| 6 months to 5 years | 11 |
| 5 years to 11 years | 11.5 |
| ≥12 years | 12 |

## Approach to anaemia

### History

- Diet (nutritional deficiency).
- History of chronic disease.
- History of hemolytic disorder: Jaundice.
- Family history of:
  - Anaemia/jaundice.
  - Gall bladder disease.
  - Splenectomy.

### Physical examination

| Findings | Associated conditions |
|---|---|
| Associated congenital anomalies | Fanconi's anaemia |
| Petechiae/purpura | Leukemia, aplastic anaemia, hemolytic uremic syndrome/HUS |
| Generalized lymphadenopathy | Leukemia, systemic onset Juvenile Idiopathic Arthritis (JIA) |
| Splenomegaly | Leukemia, hemolytic disorders |

---

## Lab approach to anaemia

**Mean corpuscular volume / MCV**

Normal range: **90 ± 8 fL**

### Low MCV / microcytic anaemia

1. Iron deficiency anaemia / IDA.
2. Anaemia of chronic disease / ACD.
3. Thalassemia.

↓

**History and physical examination**

↓

**Suggestive of IDA**

↓

**Trial of therapeutic iron**

↓

**Response**

- (+) → IDA.
- (−) → Do serum ferritin.

### Serum ferritin

- Decreased: IDA.
- Normal: Thalassemia.
- High: ACD.

---

## Normal / High MCV

↓

**Reticulocyte count**

### Low

↓

Peripheral smear: Look for evidence of hemolysis.

**Aplastic crisis**

- Platelet/neutrophil count:
  - **Low:**
    1. Bone marrow failure.
    2. Megaloblastic anaemia.
  - **Normal:** Pure red cell aplasia, e.g. Blackfan-Diamond syndrome.

### High

↓

Peripheral smear: Look for evidence of hemolysis.

- Hemolytic anemia.
- Blood loss.

The aplastic crisis is facilitated by infections especially **Parvovirus B19 infection**.

## Iron deficiency anemia

- Most common condition causing anaemia in children worldwide: iron deficiency.
- Iron deficiency: State of insufficient iron, such that it's unable to maintain normal physiological function. It is characterized by low levels of iron stores/ferritin.

### Risk factors of iron deficiency in children

- Dietary deficiency of iron.
- Increased demand: growth spurts (first 2 years & puberty).
- Preterm/low birth weight (LBW), VLBW <1.5 kg: low iron stores.
- Malabsorption: celiac disease (villous atrophy in small intestine).
- Hookworm infestation (blood loss and decreased absorption; treated by deworming).

- Age group: 9 months to 24 months.

---

In the first 6–9 months: No anemia as iron stores are derived from the mother during the last trimester of pregnancy.

### Clinical features

- Pallor, irritability, fatigue.
- Koilonychia: spooning of nails.
- Platynychia: flat nail.
- Glossitis, angular stomatitis.

### Associations in clinical situations

- PICA.
- Temper tantrum.
- Breath holding spells.
- Restless leg syndrome.

### Lab features

- Microcytosis, anisocytosis.
- Low MCV.
- Low serum ferritin.

### Treatment

Most economical and effective form of iron is **ferrous sulfate: 20% elemental iron**.

- Dose: 3 to 6 mg/kg/day.
- Duration: 4 to 6 months (replenish iron stores).

### Response following iron therapy

| Time after iron administration | Response |
|---|---|
| 12 to 24 hours | Subjective improvement. Replacement of intracellular iron enzymes. |
| 36 to 48 hours | Bone marrow response: erythroid hyperplasia (increase in erythroid cells). |
| 48 to 72 hours | Reticulocytosis: peaks at 5 to 7 days. |
| 4 to 30 days | Increase in hemoglobin. |
| 1 to 3 months | Replenish iron stores. |

---

## Megaloblastic anaemia

- Macrocytes: delay in nuclear maturation.
- Causes: Deficiency of vitamin B12 and folic acid.

### Vitamin B12 deficiency causes

1. Diet: Pure vegetarian/vegan.
2. Pernicious anaemia: autoimmune antibody-mediated damage causing deficiency of intrinsic factor needed for absorption of vitamin B12.
3. Malabsorption:
   - Crohn's disease.
   - Chronic pancreatitis.
   - *Diphyllobothrium latum* (fish tapeworm).
4. Metabolic:
   - Homocystinuria.
   - Orotic aciduria.
   - Methylmalonic aciduria / MMA.

### Folic acid deficiency causes

- Diet: excess goat milk.
- Malabsorption: Giardia.
- Decreased utilization of drugs:
  - 6-mercaptopurine.
  - Methotrexate.
  - Trimethoprim.

### Clinical features

- Pallor.
- Smooth beefy red tongue.
- Hepatosplenomegaly (30–40%).
- Hyperpigmentation (knuckles).
- Neurological (vit B12 deficiency), can precede anaemia:
  - Loss of position/vibration sense: earliest.
  - Unsteady gait, paresthesia.

### Lab features of peripheral smear

- Macro-ovalocytes (large size RBCs).
- Hypersegmented neutrophils.

### Treatment

**Folic acid:** 1 to 5 mg daily for 3 to 4 weeks.

---

### Vitamin B12

- 1000 mcg oral.
- Daily for 2 weeks.
- Weekly for the next 2 weeks.
- Monthly for a lifetime.

## Inherited pancytopenia

Decrease in RBCs, WBCs and platelets due to bone marrow failure.

### Fanconi's anaemia

- Inheritance: Autosomal recessive.
- Defect in DNA repair (chromosomal breaks seen in cells).

### Features

Pallor, increased risk of infections, petechiae/purpura.

**3 "S":**

1. Short stature.
2. Skeletal anomalies:
   - Like radial ray defects: absent or hypoplastic radius/thumb.
3. Skin pigmentation:
   - Hyperpigmentation.
   - Cafe-au-lait macules.
4. Microcephaly + tapering jaw.

- Radial ray defects.
- Bifid thumb.
- Chromosomal breaks.

### Treatment

- HSCT (Hematopoietic Stem Cell Transplantation).
- Palliative: Oral androgens.
- Long-term follow-up due to the increased risk of AML, and oral/liver cancers.

---

### Page 355

# Congenital Hemolytic Anemia

## Congenital hemolytic anaemias

- Defect of the red cell membrane. E.g., hereditary spherocytosis.
- Hemoglobinopathies. E.g., alpha/beta thalassemia, sickle cell anaemia.
- Disorders of red cell metabolism. E.g., glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase deficiency.

## Hereditary spherocytosis

- Others: Autosomal recessive.
- De novo mutation.
- Basic defect: Cytoskeleton defect (defect in RBC shapes).
- Defective proteins →
- Not able to squeeze through splenic microcirculation.
- Trapped/engulfed by splenic macrophages.

### Features

**Clinical:** Anemia, intermittent jaundice, splenomegaly, family history positive for jaundice/splenectomy.

---

## Hereditary spherocytosis — Lab

Peripheral smear shows:

- Densely stained spherocytes.

**Spherocytes**

- Increased MCHC / mean corpuscular haemoglobin concentration.
- Increased osmotic fragility (incubated osmotic fragility):
  - Noted especially when incubated for 24 hours at 37°C.

### Management

- Folic acid supplementation for a life.
- To decrease the possibility of red cell hypoplasia.
- Splenectomy:
  - Normalise RBC life span / improve anaemia.
  - Is deferred until 5 years, as it increases post-splenectomy infections as encapsulated organisms are destroyed in spleen:
    1. Pneumococci.
    2. *H. influenzae* type b.
    3. Meningococci.
  - All children need to be vaccinated for these infections at least 2 weeks before undergoing splenectomy.
  - Penicillin prophylaxis is given for 1 year post-splenectomy.

## Hemoglobinopathies

1. Sickle cell anaemia.
2. Thalassemia.

## Sickle cell anemia

- Mode of inheritance: Autosomal recessive.

---

## Sickle cell anemia

### Defect

Glutamic acid in 6th position of β-globin chain

↓

Replaced by

**Valine**

**Mnemonic:** Glutamate Goes, Valine walks.

**Reason:** Point/missense mutations.

### Pathophysiology

- Type of hemoglobin: HbS (sickle shaped).
- Hypoxia.
- Dehydration.
- Acidosis.

↓

Sickling of RBCs

↓

Blockage of blood vessels.

### Clinical features

- Icterus, pallor, and mild splenomegaly.
- Increased risk of recurrent febrile episodes.

### Vaso-occlusive crisis

1. **Bony pain:** Commonly affects the long bones (femur, tibia).
   - The first manifestation of sickle cell anemia in infants: **Dactylitis**.
   - Dactylitis: Painful swelling of hands and feet.
2. **Kidney:** Renal papillary necrosis.
   - Hence the inability of kidneys to concentrate urine results in **isosthenuria:** the osmolarity of urine is the same as plasma/fixed specific gravity.
3. **Spleen:** Repeated infarctions due to multiple vaso-occlusive crises.

---

- Results in auto-splenectomy at 6 years (asplenia).
4. **CNS:** Stroke.

## Acute chest syndrome

(Features resembles sudden pneumonia.)

### Symptoms

1. Fever.
2. Cough.
3. Tachypnoea.
4. Hypoxemia.

**Chest X-ray:** New infiltrates.

### Treatment

1. O₂ support.
2. IV fluids.
3. Bronchodilators.
4. Antibiotics: 3rd-generation cephalosporins + macrolides.

## Sequestration crisis

- Sickled RBCs block the venous outflow of the spleen.

↓

Engorgement of spleen.

- A sudden increase in the size of the spleen.
- Decline in Hb >2 g/dL (increased destruction of RBCs).

## Aplastic crisis

- Sudden decline in all cell counts: pancytopenia.
- Precipitated by Parvovirus B19.

### Lab features

1. Sickled RBCs.
2. Sickling test: 2% sodium metabisulfite.
3. Hb electrophoresis: HbS.
4. Gold standard: HPLC / High-performance liquid chromatography (for all hemoglobinopathies).

### Management

- Hydration and analgesia (narcotics).

---

- **Red cell transfusions:** To improve O₂ carrying capacity in aplastic/sequestration crisis.
- **Exchange transfusion:** Stroke and acute chest syndrome.
- Goal: HbS <30%.

### Drugs

- **Hydroxyurea** (in >9 months age) to HbF:
  - Decreases pain, crises, hospitalisation, and transfusion requirement.
- **L-glutamine:** Decrease vaso-occlusive crisis.
- **HSCT/Hematopoietic stem cell transplant** is curative.

## Thalassemia

Reduction in the synthesis of one or more haemoglobin polypeptide chains.

Types: α, β thalassemia.

### β Thalassemia

- β-globin chains are coded by 2 genes on chromosome 11.
- The mutation is point mutation.
- Most common: Splicing mutations.

### β Thalassemia spectrum

1. **β Thalassemia trait:** Asymptomatic, mild anaemia.
   - HbA₂ = 8% and HbF = 5%.
2. **β Thalassemia intermedia:** Anaemia, jaundice, splenomegaly.
   - Transfusion ± (infrequent if required).
3. **β Thalassemia major:** Transfusion dependent, most severe.
   - Cooley/mediterranean anaemia.
   - (Thalassa: sea, emia: related to blood.)

### Pathophysiology

Absence of β-globin chains.

↓

A relative excess of alpha chains (insoluble).

↓

Accumulate in erythroid precursors (bone marrow).

↓

Erythroblast undergo apoptosis.

---

**Ineffective erythropoiesis**

↓

Anaemia

↓

Regular blood transfusions are required.

This predisposes to **iron overload**.

- Iron overload in the heart → CCF/congestive cardiac failure.
- Cause of death: CCF.

## Extramedullary hematopoiesis

Compensation for ineffective erythropoiesis which causes the following:

### Chipmunk facies

- Frontal bossing.
- Prominence of cheeks (maxilla).
- Dental malocclusion.

- X-ray skull: expansion of skull.
- Hair on end appearance/crew-cut appearance.
- Hepatosplenomegaly.

### Management

Require blood transfusion is required:

- Interval: 3 to 4 weeks.
- The goal is to maintain pre-transfusion Hb level.
- 9.5 to 10.5 g/dL for optimal growth → decrease deformities.

### Assessment of iron overload

- MRI liver → R2/R2*.
- MRI heart → T2w.

### Iron chelation

Iron overload occurs after 1 year of transfusion therapy or ferritin >1000 ng/mL.

---

## Iron chelators

1. **Deferoxamine** (SC/IV).
2. **Deferasirox** (oral): more effective in decreasing organ damage.
3. **Deferiprone** (oral).

Oral drugs are more commonly used nowadays.

### Curative treatment

**Hematopoietic Stem Cell Transplant / HSCT:** 90% cure rate.

Needs to be done before liver involvement (hepatic fibrosis).

## Red cell metabolism

- RBCs lack mitochondria.
- Anaerobic metabolism of glucose for ATP.
- Pathway: HMP shunt.
- Rate-limiting enzyme: G6PD / Glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase.
- Role of G6PD:
  - Provides NADPH and glutathione.
  - Glutathione protects RBCs from oxidative stress.

## Disorders of red cell metabolism

### G6PD deficiency

G6PD deficiency

↓

Low levels of glutathione

↓

RBCs undergo oxidative stress

↓

Hemolysis

## G6PD deficiency

- Inheritance: X-linked recessive.
- More frequent in persons of African, Mediterranean, and Asian ancestry.

### Clinical presentation

- Intermittent episodic hemolysis.

### Factors precipitating hemolysis

1. Infections.
2. Food: Fava beans.

---

### Factors precipitating hemolysis

3. Drugs:
   - Primaquine.
   - Sulfonamide (sulfamethoxazole).
   - Phenazopyridine (Pyridium).
   - Nitrofurantoin.
   - Nalidixic acid.

### Lab features

**Heinz bodies:** Denatured haemoglobin seen as intracellular inclusion bodies in RBCs.

G6PD deficiency

↓

Oxidative stress on RBCs

↓

Hb → methaemoglobin

↓

Denaturation

↓

Intracellular inclusions: Heinz bodies.

### Bite cells

These abnormal RBCs

↓

Pass through spleen.

↓

Splenic macrophages bite chunks of RBC containing inclusion bodies.

### Management

- Most episodes of hemolysis are self-limiting.
- Avoid precipitating factors.
- Early diagnosis and treatment is recommended.

---

### Page 363

# Bleeding Disorders

## Bleeding disorders — introduction

If there is a cut in the skin leading to bleeding, the changes which happen to stop bleeding are:

**Vessels → Platelet function → Clotting factors**

- Telangiectasia (Coster-Weber-Rendu).
- Scurvy.
- Vasculitis.

### Bleeding disorders approach

| Finding | Disorders of vessels | Disorders of platelets |
|---|---|---|
| Petechiae | − | + |
| Deep dissecting hematomas | + | − |
| Hemarthrosis | + | Small |
| Bleeding from superficial cuts and scratches | + |  |

## Idiopathic thrombocytopenic purpura

- Also called as immune thrombocytopenic purpura (ITP).
- Antibody mediated.
- Usually in age group 1–7 years.

### Types

- Acute (m/c type).
- Chronic (persists >6 months).

---

## Acute ITP

### Typical presentation

Non-specific viral infection

↓

1–4 weeks

↓

Thrombocytopenia

- Petechiae, purpura in a previously well child.
- No splenomegaly, no lymphadenopathy.

### Treatment

- **>20,000/µL + no active bleed:** Observation (recovers spontaneously).
- **<20,000/µL + active bleed:** IVIG (intravenous immunoglobulins) (prevent destruction of antibody-coated platelets).
- Steroids.
- Anti-D (Rh⁺ children).

## Platelet function disorders

### Platelet receptor function

- GP IIb/IIIa → Aggregation.
- GP Ib → Adhesion.

### 2 major platelet function disorders

#### Glanzmann's thrombasthenia

- Defect in GP IIb/IIIa.
- Defect in platelet aggregation.
- Normal platelet number.
- Normal-sized platelets.

#### Bernard-Soulier syndrome

- Defect in GP Ib.
- Defect in platelet adhesion.
- ↑ platelet number.
- Large-sized platelets.

---

## Von Willebrand disease

- M/c inherited bleeding disorder worldwide.

### Functions of von Willebrand factor (VWF)

1. Carrier for factor 8.
2. Helps in attachment of platelet to endothelium.

### Types

1. **Type 1**
   - ↓ VWF (m/c).
   - Treatment: Desmopressin.
2. **Type 2**
   - Impaired function of VWF.
   - Treatment: VWF concentrates.
3. **Type 3**
   - Absent VWF.
   - Treatment: VWF concentrates.

## Hemophilia

### Types

- Hemophilia A: Factor VIII deficiency.
- Hemophilia B: Factor IX deficiency.

- **Hallmark feature:** Recurrent hemarthrosis (bleeding into joints).
- **Target joint:** Joint prone to recurrent hemarthrosis.
  - Classically, knee or ankle joints are involved.
  - Once a joint is affected, it becomes a target joint.

### Treatment

- Factor replacement.

**Hemophilia A:** Amount of factor required = % desired rise × body weight (kg) × 0.5.

**Hemophilia B:** Amount of factor required = % desired rise × body weight (kg) × 1.4.

- % rise is estimated based on severity of bleeding:
  - Severe bleed (CNS): 100%.
  - Less severe bleed: 30–50%.

If factor replacement is not available, alternative treatment options:

- Hemophilia A: FFP/Cryoprecipitate (does not contain factor IX).
- Hemophilia B: Only FFP.

---

### Page 366

# Hematological Malignancies

## Acute Lymphoblastic Leukemia (ALL)

- ALL: most common type of leukemia in children and most common malignancy overall in children.
- Peak age: 2 to 5 yrs.
- Boys > Girls.

### Risk factors

A. Ataxia telangiectasia.
B. Bloom's syndrome.
C. Severe combined immunodeficiency.
D. Down syndrome.
E. Environmental factors (like radiation exposure).
F. Li-Fraumeni syndrome.

### Types of ALL

1. Progenitor B cell (pre-B-cell): 85% cases.
2. T-cell.
3. Mature B-cell.

### Features of ALL

1. Bone pain.
2. Decrease in cell count: anaemia, thrombocytopenia, leukopenia.
3. Infiltration:
   - Hepatosplenomegaly.
   - Lymphadenopathy.
   - Mediastinal mass → SVC syndrome (more commonly associated with T-cell type).

### Diagnosis of ALL

Bone marrow aspirate: >25% lymphoblast.

---

## Standard risk and high risk

|  | Standard risk | High risk |
|---|---|---|
| **Age** | 2–10 yrs | <1 yr, >10 yrs |
| **Gender** | Female | Male |
| **Organomegaly** | Absent | Present |
| **Mediastinal mass** | Absent | Present |
| **CNS features** | Absent | Present |
| **Initial WBC count** | <50,000/cu mm | >50,000/cu mm |
| **Subtypes** | Pre-B cell | Mature B-cell |
| **Genetic factors** | Hyperdiploidy, Trisomy 4, 10 | Hypodiploidy, t(9;22), Philadelphia, t(4;11) → <1 yr → infantile leukemia |

- Intermediate risk: T-cell type of ALL.

## Treatment of ALL

### (i) Induction phase

- Prednisolone.
- Asparaginase.
- Anthracycline.
- Vincristine.

In high risk: minimal residual disease — present at the end of induction phase.

- **Minimal Residual Disease:** Minimal number of blasts present even at the end of induction phase.
  - Done using flow cytometry, PCR.

### (ii) Prophylactic CNS therapy

- Intrathecal methotrexate.

### (iii) Consolidation phase (intensification phase)

- Methotrexate.
- Epipodophyllotoxin.
- Cyclophosphamide.
- Cytosine-arabinoside.

---

### (iv) Maintenance phase

- Methotrexate.
- 6-mercaptopurine.

## Acute myeloid leukemia (AML)

- 2nd most common type of leukemia in children.

### Diagnosis of AML

Bone marrow aspirate: >20% myeloblasts.

### Types of AML

| FAB | Name | Genetic variation and prognosis |
|---|---|---|
| M₀ | Dedifferentiated/minimally differentiated | Deletion of 7q → poor prognosis |
| M₁ | Myeloblastic without maturation | t(8;21) → good prognosis |
| M₂ | Myeloblastic with maturation | t(8;21), t(15;17), inversion 16 → good prognosis |
| M₃ | Promyelocytic |  |
| M₄ | Myelomonocytic |  |
| M₅ | Monocytic |  |
| M₆ | Erythroleukemic | Down's syndrome → good prognosis |
| M₇ | Megakaryocytic |  |

### Features of AML

Features similar to ALL except:

- No lymphadenopathy.
- No hepatosplenomegaly.

But M₄ and M₅ have associated hepatosplenomegaly.

- M₃: increased risk of DIC.
- M₅: gum hypertrophy.
- Accumulation of myeloperoxidase: Chloroma.

---

## Leukemia and Down's syndrome

- Down's syndrome has increased risk of leukemia.
- Most common leukemia overall: ALL.
- Most common leukemia in age <3 yrs: AML.

### Variant in newborn period

**Transient myeloproliferative disorder:**

- Blasts + in circulation.
- Hepatosplenomegaly.
- GATA-1 mutation.
- 20% cases develop leukemia.

## Hodgkin lymphoma

### Age group

Bimodal age distribution, 15–19 years (adolescent age) and >50 years (5th or 6th decade).

- Male predominance in children. This is not observed in older population.

### Risk factors

**Infections:** HHV-6, CMV, EBV (EBV confers 4-fold high risk of developing HL).

**Autoimmune disorders:** SLE, ITP (immune/idiopathic thrombocytopenic purpura).

### Clinical presentations

1. **Lymphadenopathy:** Cervical group is usually affected.
   - Painless.
   - Firm and rubbery in consistency.
   - Discrete/matted lymph nodes.
2. **Constitutional symptoms:** Grading B or B symptoms.
   - Fever of >38°C.
   - Drenching night sweats.
   - Weight loss >10% in the preceding 6 months (significant weight loss).

- Presence of constitutional symptoms: Grading B.
- Absence of constitutional symptoms: Grading A (good prognosis).

---

- Presence of constitutional symptoms confers a poor prognosis and indicates the need for aggressive treatment.
3. Asymptomatic mediastinal mass usually involving the anterior mediastinum.

## Types of HL

Based on the pathological features of HD.

| Classical type | Nodular/lymphocyte predominant type |
|---|---|
| **CD15+ & CD30+** | **CD20+ & CD45+** |
